Newer Base deployments may use a different perps implementation in the same repository; check which market contract each deployment actually runs before you audit.

Step 5. Hands-on: debt that is not refreshed

A shared-debt system is only safe if each action first brings the debt up to date. This toy model has two LPs and a market that reports losses. Its undelegate uses a cached debt number.

contract ToyMarket {
    int public debt;
    function reportedDebt() external view returns (int) { return debt; }
    function lose(int x) external { debt += x; }               // traders win: market now owes more
}
contract ToyCore {
    ToyMarket m; int public cachedDebt; uint public totalShares;
    mapping(address => uint) public shares; mapping(address => uint) public collateral;
    constructor(ToyMarket _m) { m = _m; }
    function delegate(uint a) external { collateral[msg.sender] += a; shares[msg.sender] += a; totalShares += a; }
    function refresh() public { cachedDebt = m.reportedDebt(); }
    function debtOf(address a) public view returns (int) { return cachedDebt * int(shares[a]) / int(totalShares); }
    function undelegate(uint a) external {                      // no refresh()
        require(int(collateral[msg.sender]) - int(a) >= debtOf(msg.sender), "undercollateralised");
        collateral[msg.sender] -= a; shares[msg.sender] -= a; totalShares -= a;
    }
}
contract DebtTest is Test {
    function test_exitBeforeLoss() public {
        ToyMarket m = new ToyMarket(); ToyCore c = new ToyCore(m);
        address A = address(0xA); address B = address(0xB);
        vm.prank(A); c.delegate(100); vm.prank(B); c.delegate(100);
        m.lose(100);                                              // market owes 100, not yet reflected
        vm.prank(A); c.undelegate(100);                           // A leaves using the stale cached debt (0)
        c.refresh();
        emit log_named_int("B debt", c.debtOf(B));                // B now carries all 100
        assertEq(c.debtOf(B), 100);                               // fair share would have been 50
    }
}

Run it: A escapes with 100 and B inherits the whole loss. Fix: call refresh() at the top of undelegate (and of every function that reads debt or changes shares). Then A's debt is 50, the check allows at most 50 out, and the test's exit fails. In real Synthetix V3 the vault updates its distribution from the market's reported debt before any position change; your job is to confirm that holds on every path.

Step 6. Find the bug

// A. settle an async order
uint price = oracle.getLatestPrice(feedId);                      // price at settlement time, not commitTime + delay

// B. fill price
uint fill = price * (1e18 + skewBefore * 1e18 / skewScale) / 1e18;     // ignores the size of the trade

// C. delegateCollateral
// reads vault debt but never calls market.reportedDebt() first (see the toy above)

// D. core iterating markets
for (uint i; i < markets.length; i++) total += IMarket(markets[i]).reportedDebt();   // no try/catch, no bound

// E. permissions
function mintUsd(uint128 accountId, ...) external {
    require(msg.sender == ownerOf(accountId));                    // delegated MINT permission never consulted
}
Answers
  1. A. A settlement that uses the current price lets a trader (or keeper) commit, watch the market, and settle only if the price moved their way: a free option, and LPs pay for it. The price must be the one at commitTime + delay within the allowed window.
  2. B. Using only skewBefore ignores the trade's own impact, so any size fills at the pre-trade premium. A whale takes huge exposure at zero impact. Use the average of skew before and after.
  3. C. Stale debt: the same flaw as the toy. Update the debt distribution from every market's reported debt before changing delegation, minting or withdrawing.
  4. D. A registered market is external code. If its reportedDebt reverts or burns gas, every LP in the pool is locked out of withdrawals. Bound the list, isolate failures where safe, and make pool owners vet markets: the pool-owner role is a trust boundary.
  5. E. The permission model says a delegated address may mint. Checking only ownership either blocks legitimate integrations or, when copied inconsistently across functions, lets one path bypass the intended role. Every function must apply the same hasPermission check with the correct permission constant.

Step 7. Study material

  • Synthetix V2 oracle front-running: bots profited from the gap between Chainlink updates and real prices, which led to delayed, pull-oracle settlement. Read the SIPs and write down which V2 attack each V3 mechanism blocks.
  • Synthetix sKRW, 2019: a feed for the Korean won reported a price far off the real value and a bot exploited it before it was paused; roughly a decade-old example that oracle feeds are inputs you must bound. Details from memory; read the incident report.
  • Mango Markets, Oct 2022: again, thin-market price manipulation to borrow against profit. Compare with Synthetix's per-market debt caps and oracle nodes.
  • Read the Synthetix V3 audit reports and contest findings; classify them as debt accounting, permissions, oracle, liquidation.

Step 8. Synthetix V3 checklist

  • Debt is refreshed from every market before any delegation, mint, burn, liquidation or withdrawal.
  • C-ratio uses debt including pool share, a fresh oracle price and correct decimals; thresholds for mint and for liquidation are applied in the right places.
  • Liquidation rewards and penalties cannot exceed collateral; behaviour when collateral value is below debt (bad debt: who pays?).
  • Market registration and pool configuration: who can add a market, caps on debt, behaviour of a malicious or reverting market.
  • Permissions: each function checks the right permission; permission changes and account transfers cannot be abused.
  • Oracle graph: staleness, deviation, decimals, reducer logic, circuit breakers; fallback if a node reverts.
  • Perps: fill price, fees (maker or taker), funding sign and integration over time, margin and liquidation maths, settlement window and timestamp rules, keeper incentives.
  • Rounding in the distribution (shares, value per share), dust and the "last LP out" edge case.
  • Upgradeability: proxy modules, storage slots, who can upgrade.
Lab 16 deliverable(1) Redo the worked debt sharing with LP B delegating 100 ETH instead of 50: new shares, debt per LP, c-ratios at $2,000 and $1,000. (2) Run test_exitBeforeLoss, add the refresh, show the exit failing. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet A (settlement price at the wrong time).

Stage 6: Staking and restaking

About 25 hours of study · needs: Stage 1 (shares) and Stage 2; Lab 21 also needs Lab 6

Theory in plain words

Proof-of-stake pays validators for locking tokens and slashes them for misbehaviour. Liquid staking pools deposits, runs validators, and returns a token. There are two ways to show growth: a rebasing token (your balance rises) or an exchange-rate token (balance fixed, each token worth more). Both are shares underneath.

Rewards are reported by an oracle (Ethereum) or measured by the chain each epoch (Solana). Exits are slow because validators must leave and funds be returned, so there is a withdrawal queue; a fast exit needs a market or a limited instant path. When the market price falls below the redemption value, the token depegs, and anything that valued it at redemption value is exposed. Restaking reuses the same stake to secure additional services: more yield, but a fault in one service can slash stake that other services also rely on. Integrators must decide whether to price these tokens by rate, by market, or by the lower of the two.

Check yourself before moving on
  1. Why does your share count not change when a rebasing token's balance grows?
  2. What is a depeg and who is hurt by one?

Lab 17. Lido (stETH)

In plain wordsLido turns ETH into stETH, a token whose balance grows daily. It uses shares: your share count never changes, the ETH value of a share does. A committee of oracles reports validator balances once a day.

Key terms: shares, rebase, oracle report, withdrawal queue. See the glossary at the end.

Lido takes ETH from users, runs validators through professional node operators, and gives back stETH, a token whose balance grows every day without any transfer. That one fact, a rebasing token built on shares, drives almost everything an auditor must check: how the share price is computed, who is allowed to change it (an oracle report), what happens when validators are slashed, how people exit (a withdrawal queue), and how every integrator (lending markets, AMMs, bridges) copes with a balance that changes under its feet.

Step 1. The cast

PieceRole
Lido (stETH)Entry point. submit() takes ETH and mints shares. Holds the buffer of undeployed ETH and the accounting totals.
SharesThe real ledger. balanceOf = shares · totalPooledEther / totalShares. Rebasing means changing totalPooledEther.
wstETHWrapper holding stETH and exposing shares as a normal non-rebasing token. Used by DeFi and L2 bridges.
AccountingOracle + HashConsensusDaily report of consensus-layer (CL) balances, vaults and withdrawal data, accepted only when a quorum of oracle members agree.
OracleReportSanityCheckerLimits on how much one report may change things (maximum rebase, validator churn, balance drop).
StakingRouter + modulesSplits new deposits across node-operator modules (curated, community, DVT).
DepositSecurityModuleGuardians must sign off that a deposit is safe before 32 ETH goes to the deposit contract.
WithdrawalQueue (ERC-721)Users request withdrawals as NFTs; claimed after finalization.
WithdrawalVault, ELRewardsVault, BurnerReceive validator exits and execution rewards; burn shares for withdrawals and penalties.
Dual governance / AragonUpgrade and parameter control; stETH holders can veto changes during a delay.

Step 2. The maths

Total pooled     bufferedEther + clBalance + (deposited − seen validators) · 32 ETH       (principle)
Share rate       r = totalPooledEther / totalShares
Submit           shares = amount · totalShares / totalPooledEther        (floor)
Balance          balanceOf(a) = shares_a · r                              (floor)
Oracle report    totalPooledEther += rewards − penalties
Protocol fee     10% of rewards, paid by minting new shares:
                 feeShares = fee · totalShares / (totalPooledEther_new − fee)
wstETH           wrap(x stETH) = getSharesByPooledEth(x);   stEthPerToken = r
Withdrawal       payout = min(requested amount, shares · r at finalization)

Worked example. Pool: 9,000,000 ETH and 8,300,000 shares (r = 1.0843).

  • You submit 100 ETH: shares = 100 · 8,300,000 / 9,000,000 = 92.2222. Your balance is 100.0000.
  • The daily oracle reports 1,000 ETH of rewards. Fee 10% = 100 ETH, minted as 92.2130 new shares to the treasury and node operators.
  • Rate goes from 1.08434 to 1.08445. Your balance becomes 100.0100: +0.01% for the day, about 3.65% a year net against a 4.06% gross yield.
  • Your shares did not change. Only totalPooledEther did.

Withdrawal and slashing. You request 10 stETH. If before finalization validators are slashed and the rate drops 0.5%, your claim pays min(10, shares · new rate) = 9.95 ETH. Queue payouts use the lower rate so that exiting early cannot dodge a penalty.

The "1–2 wei" corner case. transfer(x) moves floor(x · totalShares / totalPooledEther) shares and the receiver's balance is floor(shares · r), so a transfer of x wei can credit the receiver with x − 1 or x − 2. Every integrator that credits a user with the nominal x is off by a few wei per call.

Step 3. Play with it

Step 4. Read the code

Step 5. Hands-on: a rebasing token breaks an integrator

This toy stETH has a fixed rate of 1.1 and Lido's transfer rounding. The vault credits users with the nominal amount they deposit.

contract ToyStETH {
    mapping(address => uint) public shares;
    uint constant POOLED = 1100; uint constant SHARES = 1000;                  // rate 1.1
    function setShares(address a, uint s) external { shares[a] = s; }
    function balanceOf(address a) public view returns (uint) { return shares[a] * POOLED / SHARES; }   // floor
    function transferFrom(address from, address to, uint amount) external returns (bool) {
        uint s = amount * SHARES / POOLED;                                      // floor, like getSharesByPooledEth
        shares[from] -= s; shares[to] += s; return true;
    }
}
contract ToyVault {
    ToyStETH t; uint public totalDeposits; mapping(address => uint) public dep;
    constructor(ToyStETH _t) { t = _t; }
    function deposit(uint amt) external { t.transferFrom(msg.sender, address(this), amt); dep[msg.sender] += amt; totalDeposits += amt; }
    function solvent() external view returns (bool) { return t.balanceOf(address(this)) >= totalDeposits; }
}
contract RebaseTest is Test {
    function test_dustInsolvency() public {
        ToyStETH t = new ToyStETH(); ToyVault v = new ToyVault(t);
        address alice = address(0xA11CE); t.setShares(alice, 1_000_000);
        vm.startPrank(alice);
        for (uint i; i < 100; i++) v.deposit(10);                              // 100 deposits of 10 wei
        vm.stopPrank();
        emit log_named_uint("recorded deposits", v.totalDeposits());          // 1000
        emit log_named_uint("actual balance", t.balanceOf(address(v)));        // 990
        assertFalse(v.solvent());
    }
}

Each 10-wei deposit moves 9 shares (floor of 9.09), worth 9.9, which rounds to 9, so the vault is short by about 1 wei per deposit and the last withdrawer cannot be paid in full. Fix by crediting what actually arrived:

function deposit(uint amt) external {
    uint before = t.balanceOf(address(this));
    t.transferFrom(msg.sender, address(this), amt);
    uint got = t.balanceOf(address(this)) - before;       // measured, not assumed
    dep[msg.sender] += got; totalDeposits += got;
}
// better still: integrate wstETH (shares), which does not rebase and has no 1-2 wei issue at the token level

Re-run: the vault stays solvent. Then ask the follow-up question: what if a positive rebase happens while tokens sit in the vault? The vault's balance grows, totalDeposits does not; where does the extra go, and can someone take it?

Step 6. Find the bug

// A. an AMM pair (V2 style) listing stETH
// reserves are stored numbers, tokens are rebasing

// B. a lending market
uint price = ethUsd * wstEth.stEthPerToken() / 1e18;       // assumes 1 stETH = 1 ETH

// C. an exchange integration
uint amountOut = queue.getClaimableEther(ids, hints)[0];  // stored as the user's fixed credit at request time

// D. a staking pool contract
depositContract.deposit{value: 32 ether}(pubkey, withdrawalCredentials, signature, root);   // pubkey from an operator

// E. an oracle consumer
function submitReport(Report calldata r) external { require(isMember[msg.sender]); lido.handleReport(r); }
Answers
  1. A. Rebasing tokens break pools that keep stored reserves. A positive rebase makes the real balance exceed reserves, and skim hands the surplus to anyone; a negative rebase leaves the pool holding less than its reserves, so swaps and withdrawals misbehave. Use wstETH in such pools.
  2. B. Using the share rate as the price treats stETH as exactly 1 ETH. In June 2022 stETH traded around 0.93 to 0.95 ETH; collateral valued at 1.0 meant borrowers were over-credited and liquidations came too late. Price with the minimum of the exchange-rate value and a market oracle, or accept the depeg risk explicitly.
  3. C. A claim is min(requested, value at finalization). If slashing happens between request and finalization, the payout is lower than the nominal request. Integrators must read the actual claimable amount and handle a shortfall.
  4. D. The first deposit for a pubkey fixes its withdrawal credentials. An operator can front-run with a 1 ETH deposit using their own credentials, and the 32 ETH deposit then goes to their control. Lido prevents this with guardians in the DepositSecurityModule who verify the deposit-contract root and key state. Any staking pool needs an equivalent check.
  5. E. A single member (or any member count below quorum) must never be able to apply a report; Lido requires a consensus of oracle members and sanity limits on the reported change. One compromised key could otherwise mint inflated balances or cause a mass slash.

Step 7. Study material

  • June 2022 stETH depeg: Terra's collapse, 3AC and Celsius selling stETH on Curve. Lido itself was solvent; the damage came from integrations that assumed 1:1 and from forced sellers with no queue (withdrawals were not yet live). Reconstruct who liquidated at what price.
  • Read the Lido V2 audit reports (several firms) and the public bounty rules; sort findings by oracle, accounting, queue, deposits and governance.
  • Deposit front-running: read Lido's write-up on the withdrawal-credentials attack and the guardian design. Draw the attack and the check on one page.
  • List every integrator pattern for stETH in one table (raw stETH, wstETH, bridged wstETH on an L2, Curve stETH/ETH pool) with its failure mode.

Step 8. Lido checklist

  • Shares ledger: every mint, burn and transfer rounds in the right direction; initial dead share present; totals never zero.
  • Oracle report: quorum, frame, data hash, sanity limits, behaviour on a bad report, who can change limits.
  • Fee accounting: minted shares correct; fee cannot be taken twice; rounding dust.
  • Deposits: guardian signature, deposit root, key validity, withdrawal credentials equal to Lido's; pause conditions.
  • Withdrawals: request bounds, finalization order, discount factor on slashing, claim reentrancy, NFT transfer.
  • Vaults: only the right contract can pull ETH; burning shares matches requested withdrawals.
  • Integrators: rebasing versus wrapped token, 1-2 wei handling, depeg-aware pricing, L2 bridging oracle for wstETH.
  • Governance and upgrades: roles, timelocks, dual-governance veto, proxy storage layout across compiler versions.
Lab 17 deliverable(1) With the calculator compute what a 500 ETH deposit earns from the same report, and what one report does to the rate when rewards are 0. (2) Run test_dustInsolvency, apply the balance-delta fix and show the vault solvent. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet B (stETH priced at 1 ETH).

Lab 18. Rocket Pool (rETH)

In plain wordsLike Lido, but anyone can run a validator by putting up part of the ETH plus RPL tokens as insurance. rETH does not grow in quantity; each rETH becomes worth more ETH over time.

Key terms: minipool, bond, exchange rate. See the glossary at the end.

Lido concentrates validator operation in a curated set of operators. Rocket Pool is the permissionless alternative: anyone can run a validator by putting up part of the 32 ETH themselves (a bond) plus the protocol's own token, RPL, as insurance; the rest of the ETH comes from users who hold rETH. Unlike stETH, rETH does not rebase: its balance stays fixed and its exchange rate rises. The audit questions shift accordingly: how the rate is set (by an oracle DAO), how a stranger's validator is kept from stealing user ETH, and how losses are allocated.

Step 1. The cast

PieceRole
RocketStorageOne "eternal storage" contract holding almost all state under hashed keys; the registry of which contract addresses are the current network contracts.
RocketBaseParent contract with guards such as onlyLatestNetworkContract and helpers to read/write storage keys.
RocketVaultHolds the ETH and tokens belonging to the network contracts (deposit pool balance, RPL stakes).
RocketDepositPoolUsers deposit ETH, get rETH; ETH waits here until assigned to minipools.
RocketTokenRETHThe token. Exchange rate = reported total ETH / rETH supply. Burnable for ETH when liquidity exists.
Minipool (proxy + delegate)One contract per validator, set as the validator's withdrawal credential. Splits the ETH between operator and pool when the validator exits.
RocketNodeStaking, RocketNodeDepositRPL stake and ETH bond of node operators; minimum and maximum RPL relative to borrowed or bonded ETH.
RocketNetworkBalances, RocketNetworkPricesOracle DAO submits balances (sets the rETH rate) and the RPL price.
Oracle DAO and protocol DAOTrusted nodes run the oracle and scrub bad minipools; the protocol DAO votes on settings and upgrades.

Upgrade model: not proxies. The DAO registers a new contract address in RocketStorage and the old one stops being "latest". Since all state lives in RocketStorage, a new contract inherits it. The risk is in the keys.

Step 2. The maths and the minipool flow

rETH rate           getExchangeRate = totalETH / rETH supply        (totalETH comes from the oracle report)
Deposit             rETH minted = (ETH − depositFee) · supply / totalETH
Burn                ETH out = rETH · totalETH / supply              (only if liquidity exists)
Minipool, bond b    pool supplies 32 − b;  rewards R split: operator gets (b/32)·R + commission·((32−b)/32)·R
Loss                balance < 32 ETH: the pool's ETH is made whole first, the operator's bond (and RPL) absorb the loss
RPL requirement     min stake = 10% of borrowed ETH (value),  max effective stake = 150% of bonded ETH

Worked rate. totalETH 449,000 and supply 400,000: rate 1.1225. You deposit 100 ETH with a 0.05% fee: 99.95 ETH → 89.04 rETH. Your rETH balance never changes; as the oracle reports more ETH the value rises.

Worked minipool economics. 8 ETH bond, so the pool lends 24 ETH. Validator yields 3.5% on 32 ETH = 1.12 ETH/year. Commission 14% (illustrative).

  • Operator: 8/32 · 1.12 = 0.28, plus 14% of the pool's 0.84 = 0.1176, total 0.3976 ETH on 8 ETH = 4.97% (before RPL rewards).
  • rETH holders: 0.84 − 0.1176 = 0.7224 ETH on 24 ETH = 3.01%.
  • If the validator loses 2 ETH, the 24 ETH pool portion is protected first; the 8 ETH bond (and, if short, slashed RPL) pays.
StageWhat happensProtection
InitialisedOperator pays their bond and registers the validator keyKey and credentials recorded
PrelaunchPool ETH assigned; the minipool sends 1 ETH to the deposit contract with credentials = the minipoolOracle DAO watches the beacon chain and scrubs any with wrong credentials
StakingAfter the scrub window the operator calls stake to deposit the remaining 31 ETHCredentials already proven correct
WithdrawableValidator exits, balance returns to the minipoolSplit and loss rules

This is Rocket Pool's answer to the deposit front-running problem from Lab 17: a stranger cannot be allowed to make the first (credential-setting) deposit with their own address.

Rate sniping. Because the rate jumps only when the oracle reports, someone could deposit just before a positive report and redeem just after. Houston-era Rocket Pool adds a deposit delay on rETH transfer/burn plus a deposit fee and pool caps (confirm exact settings in your version).

Step 3. Play with it

Step 4. Read the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/rocket-pool/rocketpool && cd rocketpool/contracts/contract
ls . deposit minipool network node token dao
Paths from memory; confirm.
ReadLook for

network/RocketNetworkBalances.solsubmitBalances: block ordering, member vote counting, consensus threshold, rate-change bound.

dao/node/*, dao/protocol/*Member join and leave, proposal bonds, voting power, what each DAO can change.

Step 5. Hands-on: sniping the oracle report

A toy rETH where the rate only moves when an oracle reports. The pool holds 1,000 ETH of validators and deposits go straight into the total.

contract ToyReth {
    uint public totalEth; uint public supply; mapping(address => uint) public bal;
    constructor() payable { totalEth = msg.value; supply = msg.value; }              // 1,000 ETH, 1,000 rETH
    function deposit() external payable returns (uint r) { r = msg.value * supply / totalEth; supply += r; totalEth += msg.value; bal[msg.sender] += r; }
    function report(uint newTotal) external { totalEth = newTotal; }                  // oracle (simplified)
    function burn(uint r) external { uint e = r * totalEth / supply; bal[msg.sender] -= r; supply -= r; totalEth -= e; payable(msg.sender).transfer(e); }
    receive() external payable {}
}
contract SnipeTest is Test {
    receive() external payable {}
    function test_snipe() public {
        ToyReth t = new ToyReth{value: 1000 ether}();
        vm.deal(address(this), 1000 ether);
        uint r = t.deposit{value: 1000 ether}();                // 1) deposit just before the report
        t.report(1000 ether + 40 ether + 1000 ether);           // 2) validators earned 40 ETH today; buffer holds my 1,000
        uint before = address(this).balance;
        t.burn(r);                                              // 3) redeem right after
        emit log_named_uint("profit (wei)", address(this).balance - before - 1000 ether);   // 20 ETH
        assertEq(address(this).balance - before, 1020 ether);   // took 20 of the 40 ETH reward for minutes of exposure
    }
}

The honest stakers earned 40 ETH over the day; the sniper, who owned half the supply for a few blocks, takes 20 ETH. Mitigations used in practice: a deposit delay (cannot transfer or burn for a number of blocks), a deposit fee, a cap on the deposit pool, and limited exit liquidity. Add the first one:

mapping(address => uint) public lastDeposit;
function deposit() external payable returns (uint r) { ...; lastDeposit[msg.sender] = block.number; }
function burn(uint r) external { require(block.number >= lastDeposit[msg.sender] + 5760, "deposit delay"); ... }

Re-run: the burn reverts. Now the attacker would have to hold through the report and several more, so exposure to slashing and market risk returns. Think about what the delay does not stop: someone who is already a large holder.

Step 6. Find the bug

// A. lending market valuing rETH
uint price = uniswapV3Pool.getSpotPrice(rETH, WETH);

// B. a new network contract
function setNote(string calldata key, uint value) external onlyLatestNetworkContract {
    setUint(keccak256(abi.encodePacked(key)), value);               // caller-controlled key, no namespace
}

// C. minipool stake
function stake(bytes calldata pubkey, bytes calldata sig, bytes32 root) external onlyMinipoolOwner {
    require(status == Prelaunch);                                    // no scrub window check, no credential check
    depositContract.deposit{value: 31 ether}(pubkey, withdrawalCredentials, sig, root);
}

// D. loss split
nodeAmount = balance * nodeBond / 32;  poolAmount = balance - nodeAmount;

// E. oracle balances
function submitBalances(uint256 _block, uint256 _totalEth, uint256 _supply) external onlyTrustedNode {
    // no check that _block is newer than the last accepted block
}
Answers
  1. A. A spot DEX price is flash-loan manipulable. rETH has a built-in exchange rate that cannot be moved within a block. Value rETH as getExchangeRate × ETH price and add a market-based floor for depegs (the rate is the redeemable value, not the tradable price).
  2. B. In eternal storage the key is the whole security boundary. A caller-chosen key can collide with a sensitive key (for example a setting or another contract's balance entry), so a "note" function turns into an arbitrary write. Always namespace keys with a fixed prefix and bound the key space.
  3. C. The 31 ETH must only be released after the one-ETH pre-stake has been checked on the beacon chain and the scrub window has passed. Otherwise an operator can race a deposit with wrong credentials and take pool ETH.
  4. D. Splitting by the pre-loss ratio makes rETH holders absorb their proportional loss. The design principle is the opposite: the pool's ETH is made whole first and the operator's bond (then RPL) takes the loss: poolAmount = min(balance, borrowed) and the remainder to the operator.
  5. E. Without ordering and uniqueness checks a trusted node (or a replay) can submit an older report and pull the rate backwards or let a stale total be accepted. Require a strictly newer block, one vote per member per block, and bounded change.

Step 7. Study material

  • Read Rocket Pool's audit reports (several firms) and the public contest findings for the Atlas, Houston and later upgrades. Sort by: storage and upgrade, minipool state, oracle, DAO governance.
  • Read the explanation of the scrub system and bond reduction. For each step of a validator's life, write what an attacker operator would try and what stops it.
  • Compare with Lido (Lab 17) in a table: who operates validators, how the rate is set, how front-running is prevented, how losses are allocated, who can upgrade.
  • The upcoming or recent upgrades (smaller bonds, "megapools") change the accounting model; check which version you audit.

Step 8. Rocket Pool checklist

  • Eternal storage: key construction (namespacing, collisions), who can write, upgrade and rollback steps, guardian powers.
  • Rate: oracle quorum and threshold, ordering of blocks, bounds on change, rounding in rETH maths, behaviour at zero supply.
  • Deposit pool: fee, caps, assignment order, excess ETH accounting, reentrancy on ETH sends.
  • Minipool lifecycle: valid transitions only; credentials checked before 31 ETH; scrub conditions; withdrawal address changes; delegate upgrade safety.
  • Loss and penalty maths: user ETH made whole first; RPL slashing path; no way for an operator to avoid or shift losses.
  • RPL: min and max rules with the oracle price; cooldowns; price staleness and manipulation.
  • Rewards: Merkle roots and claim replay, smoothing pool accounting, fee distributor reentrancy.
  • Governance: who can change settings, voting power sources, bonds and challenge periods.
  • Integrators: rate-based pricing, burn liquidity limits, deposit delay, depeg handling.
Lab 18 deliverable(1) With the calculator, find the validator APR at which a 16 ETH bond minipool gives the operator 4%. (2) Run test_snipe, add the delay and show the burn reverting. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet B (storage key collision).

Lab 19. EigenLayer (restaking)

In plain wordsEigenLayer lets staked ETH also secure other services, earning extra yield but accepting extra slashing risk. It records who delegated to which operator and how much each service may slash.

Key terms: restaking, operator, AVS, slashing. See the glossary at the end.

Staked ETH secures Ethereum. EigenLayer lets the same stake secure other services too (called AVSs, actively validated services: oracles, bridges, data layers) in exchange for extra yield, at the cost of extra slashing risk. Three roles matter: stakers deposit (liquid staking tokens or native ETH through an EigenPod), operators run the AVS software and receive delegations, and AVSs define rules and can slash. As an auditor your job is to make sure that stake is counted correctly, that exits cannot dodge slashing, and that proofs from the beacon chain cannot be faked.

Step 1. The cast

PieceRole
StrategyManager + StrategyBaseStakers deposit a whitelisted token into a strategy (one per token) and receive shares.
EigenPodManager + EigenPodNative restaking: a validator's withdrawal credentials point at the pod. The pod proves validator balances using beacon-chain state proofs and credits "beacon chain ETH" shares.
DelegationManagerStakers delegate to an operator, operators track total shares, withdrawals are queued with a delay and completed later.
AVSDirectory / AllocationManagerOperators register to AVSs and, since the slashing release, allocate part of their stake to AVS-defined operator sets; an AVS can slash only what is allocated to it.
RewardsCoordinatorAVSs submit reward distributions; stakers and operators claim by Merkle proof.
PermissionControllerLets accounts delegate management rights (such as acting for an operator).
Beacon chain oracle (EIP-4788)The EVM can read recent beacon block roots on chain; proofs are checked against them.

Step 2. The maths

Strategy shares   newShares = amount · (totalShares + 1000) / (priorTokenBalance + 1000)       (virtual offsets)
                  also: totalShares after a deposit must be 0 or ≥ MIN_NONZERO_TOTAL_SHARES (1e9)
Share value       tokens = shares · (tokenBalance + 1000) / (totalShares + 1000)
Magnitude         operator maxMagnitude starts at 1.0; allocated magnitude per (operator set, strategy)
Slash             maxMagnitude −= allocated · slashFraction
Effective stake   delegated shares · maxMagnitude
Native shares     = validator balance proven to the pod (can fall with penalties; can go negative)
Withdrawal        queue → wait the escrow delay → complete; shares are scaled by the magnitude at completion

Worked slash. Alice has 32 ETH of native stake delegated to Operator X (maxMagnitude 1.0). X allocates 50% to AVS Y's operator set. Y slashes 20% of the allocation.

  • Magnitude removed: 0.5 · 0.20 = 0.10, so maxMagnitude = 0.90.
  • Alice's effective stake: 32 · 0.90 = 28.8 ETH. Every delegator to X loses 10% of that strategy, pro rata.
  • Y's maximum possible loss to X was always 50%: that is the point of allocation. Before the slashing release, an AVS could in principle have claimed the whole stake.

Why the escrow delay is slashable. A staker queues a withdrawal when they see trouble. If queued shares were frozen at the old value, everyone could front-run a slash by queueing first and no AVS would ever be secured. After queueing, shares stay exposed to magnitude changes until the delay (about two weeks) ends.

Strategy shares with offsets. First deposit of 1 ETH-token (1e18 units): shares = 1e18 · 1000 / 1000 = 1e18. If someone donates 1e18 more directly, the next 1e18 deposit mints 1e18 · (1e18 + 1000) / (2e18 + 1000) ≈ 0.5e18 shares, as expected. The offsets matter only when balances are tiny, where MIN_NONZERO_TOTAL_SHARES blocks the rounding-to-zero trick from Lab 1.

Step 3. Play with it

Step 4. Read the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/Layr-Labs/eigenlayer-contracts && cd eigenlayer-contracts/src/contracts
ls core pods strategies libraries
Paths from memory; confirm. The repo has several releases; check out the tag that matches the deployment you audit.
ReadLook for
strategies/StrategyBase.soldeposit, withdraw, offsets, MIN_NONZERO_TOTAL_SHARES, balanceOf-based accounting, fee-on-transfer handling.
core/StrategyManager.solDeposit flow, whitelist, signature-based deposit, who calls addShares and removeDepositShares.
core/DelegationManager.soldelegateTo, queueWithdrawals, completeQueuedWithdrawal, share scaling, operator share updates on every deposit and withdrawal.
core/AllocationManager.sol, libraries/SlashingLib.solAllocation delay, deallocation delay, slashOperator, magnitude arithmetic, rounding.
core/AVSDirectory.solRegistration signatures: salt, expiry, nonce, domain separator.
pods/EigenPod.sol, EigenPodManager.sol, libraries/BeaconChainProofs.solWithdrawal-credential proofs, checkpoints, balance updates, negative shares, how the beacon root is fetched (EIP-4788) and validated.
core/RewardsCoordinator.solMerkle claims, cumulative claimed amounts, claimer settings, activation delay.

Step 5. Hands-on: slashing that can be dodged

A toy operator whose delegators have shares scaled by a magnitude. The bug is in how queued withdrawals are valued.

contract ToyRestake {
    uint public magnitude = 1e18; uint constant DELAY = 100;
    mapping(address => uint) public shares;
    struct Q { uint amount; uint startBlock; } mapping(address => Q) public queued;
    function deposit(uint s) external { shares[msg.sender] += s; }
    function effective(address a) public view returns (uint) { return shares[a] * magnitude / 1e18; }
    function queueWithdrawal() external {
        queued[msg.sender] = Q(effective(msg.sender), block.number);     // value frozen at queue time
        shares[msg.sender] = 0;
    }
    function completeWithdrawal() external returns (uint amt) {
        Q memory q = queued[msg.sender]; require(block.number >= q.startBlock + DELAY);
        delete queued[msg.sender]; return q.amount;                      // not rescaled by the current magnitude
    }
    function slash(uint pct) external { magnitude = magnitude * (100 - pct) / 100; }
}
contract SlashDodge is Test {
    function test_dodge() public {
        ToyRestake r = new ToyRestake(); address alice = address(0xA); address bob = address(0xB);
        vm.prank(alice); r.deposit(100); vm.prank(bob); r.deposit(100);
        vm.prank(alice); r.queueWithdrawal();                   // Alice sees the slash coming and exits first
        r.slash(50);                                            // AVS slashes 50%
        vm.roll(block.number + 100);
        vm.prank(alice); uint got = r.completeWithdrawal();
        emit log_named_uint("alice gets", got); emit log_named_uint("bob effective", r.effective(bob));
        assertEq(got, 100);                                     // Alice lost nothing
        assertEq(r.effective(bob), 50);                         // Bob bore the entire slash
    }
}

If every staker did what Alice did, the slash would hit nobody and the AVS would be unsecured. Fix: keep the raw share amount in the queue and scale it by the current magnitude at completion:

queued[msg.sender] = Q(shares[msg.sender], block.number);
function completeWithdrawal() external returns (uint amt) { ...; return q.amount * magnitude / 1e18; }

Re-run: Alice receives 50. Then think about the remaining design questions: how long must the delay be to beat the AVS's ability to detect and slash misbehaviour, and what happens to a queued withdrawal when an operator is slashed to zero?

Step 6. Find the bug

// A. strategy deposit
uint shares = totalShares == 0 ? amount : amount * totalShares / token.balanceOf(address(this));

// B. EigenPod credential proof
function verifyWithdrawalCredentials(bytes32 beaconRoot, bytes calldata proof, ...) external {
    require(Merkle.verify(proof, beaconRoot, leaf));      // beaconRoot is a caller-supplied argument
    _creditShares(validatorBalance);
}

// C. AVS registration
function registerOperator(address op, bytes calldata sig) external {
    require(SignatureChecker.isValid(op, keccak256(abi.encode(avs, op)), sig));
    registered[avs][op] = true;
}

// D. deposit after delegation
function depositIntoStrategy(...) external {
    stakerShares[msg.sender] += shares;                    // operatorShares[delegatedTo[msg.sender]] not increased
}

// E. rewards claim
function processClaim(Claim calldata c, bytes32[] calldata proof) external {
    require(verify(proof, root, keccak256(abi.encode(c.earner, c.token, c.cumulativeAmount))));
    token.transfer(c.earner, c.cumulativeAmount);
}
Answers
  1. A. The inflation attack: an empty strategy plus a direct token donation makes later depositors round to zero shares. EigenLayer uses virtual offsets (1000 on shares and balance) and refuses totals between 1 and MIN_NONZERO_TOTAL_SHARES; a plain formula with no protection is exploitable.
  2. B. The beacon root must be the one the chain itself provides (the EIP-4788 beacon-roots contract, for a timestamp the pod checks). If the caller supplies the root, anyone fabricates a tree containing a validator with a huge balance and mints unbacked restaked ETH.
  3. C. No salt, expiry or nonce, so a signature can be replayed after the operator deregisters, or reused across AVSs; no domain separator ties it to this chain and contract. Registration signatures must include all of these.
  4. D. If the staker is already delegated, operator totals must increase on every deposit. Otherwise the operator's stake is understated, the shares are not slashable at the operator level, and rewards and slashing diverge from real stake.
  5. E. The claim pays the cumulative amount each time without recording what was already claimed, so the same proof can be replayed until the contract is empty. Track cumulativeClaimed[earner][token] and pay the difference.

Step 7. Study material

  • Read the audit reports and formal-verification work for the slashing release (several firms, a public contest, and Certora specifications). List the invariants they check, such as total operator shares equals the sum of delegated shares.
  • Read the AVS security docs: what an AVS must design (slashing conditions, veto, operator-set definition). A bug in an AVS can slash honest operators, so auditors of AVSs are auditing a second trust layer.
  • Study how beacon-chain proofs work: generalized indices, SSZ merkleization, the validator container layout. Write down which field each proof in BeaconChainProofs verifies and what a wrong index would let an attacker prove.
  • Make a risk table: correlated slashing across AVSs, operator concentration, LST depeg, queue-delay changes, upgrade keys and the pauser role.

Step 8. EigenLayer checklist

  • Share maths in strategies: offsets, rounding, the non-zero minimum, tokens with fees or rebases excluded by whitelist.
  • Delegation: operator totals updated on every deposit, withdrawal, slash and delegation change; no path where staker and operator totals diverge.
  • Withdrawals: slashable while queued, correct scaling at completion, rounding, behaviour after a full slash, completion with a different receiver.
  • Allocation: delay and deallocation delay, over-allocation checks, ordering of slash and deallocate in one block.
  • Beacon proofs: roots from EIP-4788 only, correct timestamp, proof length, validator index, credentials equal the pod, checkpoint rules, handling of exited and slashed validators and negative shares.
  • Signatures: domain, nonce, expiry, salt for every EIP-712 flow.
  • Rewards: cumulative accounting, claimer rights, root activation delay, token transfer safety.
  • Admin: pauser, upgrade proxies, whitelist power, migration paths between releases.
Lab 19 deliverable(1) With the calculator, compute the loss for a 40 ETH delegator when an operator allocates 100% to one AVS and is slashed 30%. (2) Run test_dodge, apply the fix and show Alice receiving 50. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet E (reward replay).

Lab 20. ether.fi (liquid restaking)

In plain wordsether.fi combines liquid staking with restaking in a token usable across DeFi. It adds a rate-limited instant exit next to a slow queue, so the main risks are bad oracle reports and runs on exit liquidity.

Key terms: eETH, weETH, token bucket. See the glossary at the end.

ether.fi stacks three things you already know: Lido-style shares (eETH rebases, weETH wraps it), validators that point at EigenPods (Lab 19), and a queue plus rate-limited instant exit for liquidity. The new audit questions are about how these layers touch each other: who may tell the pool its value went up, how much exit liquidity exists at once, and what happens to token holders when restaking slashing or a depeg arrives. This lab also introduces a small but common tool, the token-bucket rate limiter.

Step 1. The cast

PieceRole
LiquidityPoolHeart of the protocol. Takes ETH, mints eETH shares, holds buffer ETH, tracks totalValueInLp and totalValueOutOfLp (ETH deployed to validators), applies oracle rebase.
eETH, weETHeETH: shares ledger with a rebasing balance. weETH: wrapper whose balance is fixed and whose rate rises, used across DeFi.
StakingManager, node and auction contractsCreate validators, set withdrawal credentials, register keys; operator selection has changed over versions (bidding in early versions, curated operators later).
EtherFiNode + EtherFiNodesManagerPer-validator contract that is the withdrawal address and owns an EigenPod; handles reward splits and restaking withdrawals.
EtherFiOracle + EtherFiAdminCommittee reports validator and reward data; the admin applies it (rebase, validator approvals, finalizing withdrawals) within sanity limits.
WithdrawRequestNFTQueue ticket for a withdrawal; claimed once finalized.
Redemption manager + rate limiterInstant redemption of eETH or weETH for ETH, limited by a token bucket and charged a fee.
Role registry / admin rolesWho can pause, rebase, upgrade, or run each flow.

The set of contracts has been reorganised several times. Always start the audit by drawing which version is deployed and which contract currently holds each role.

Step 2. The maths

Total pooled      totalValueInLp + totalValueOutOfLp
Shares for ETH    shares = amount · totalShares / totalPooled          (floor, on deposit)
Shares to burn    shares = ceil(amount · totalShares / totalPooled)    (round up, on withdrawal)
eETH balance      shares · totalPooled / totalShares
weETH rate        getRate = eETH per weETH = totalPooled / totalShares
Rebase            oracle reports accruedRewards ± → totalValueOutOfLp changes → rate changes
Claim             payout = min(requested amount, amountForShare(shares at request))
Token bucket      tokens = min(capacity, tokens + elapsed · refillRate);  consume only if tokens ≥ amount

Worked shares. Pool 1,000,000 ETH, 970,000 shares (rate 1.0309). You deposit 100 ETH: shares = 100 · 970,000 / 1,000,000 = 97. Wrapping into weETH gives 97 weETH (weETH is the share count). A report adds 100 ETH of rewards: pooled 1,000,100, so your eETH balance is 97 · 1,000,100 / 970,000 = 100.01, while your 97 weETH have not changed and are now redeemable for 100.01 eETH.

Rounding on withdrawal. The pool has 10 wei of value and 3 shares. You request 5 wei: floor(5 · 3 / 10) = 1 share would charge you too little; the code uses the ceiling, 2 shares. That is the rule from Lab 1: round against the user at the pool's boundary.

Token bucket. Capacity 1,000 ETH, refill 10 ETH/hour. A user redeems 800: bucket = 200. Twenty-four hours later: min(1,000, 200 + 240) = 440. A user wanting 600 must wait 16 more hours, or use the slow queue. The limiter protects the pool from a bank run draining ETH it needs for validators, at the cost of exit-liquidity risk for holders.

Slashing and the queue. If restaking or beacon penalties reduce the value of the shares before a queued withdrawal is claimed, the claim pays the lower of the original amount and the current value. Early leavers do not escape; late leavers do not subsidise them.

Step 3. Play with it

Step 5. Hands-on: a rate limiter that can be starved

A bucket limiter whose refill is public and truncates.

contract ToyBucket {
    uint public cap = 1000; uint public tokens = 1000; uint public ratePerHour = 10; uint public last;
    constructor() { last = block.timestamp; }
    function refill() public {
        uint dt = block.timestamp - last;
        uint add = dt * ratePerHour / 3600;                 // integer division: zero if dt < 360 s
        tokens = tokens + add > cap ? cap : tokens + add;
        last = block.timestamp;                             // clock moves even when nothing was credited
    }
    function consume(uint a) external { refill(); require(tokens >= a, "limit"); tokens -= a; }
}
contract BucketTest is Test {
    function test_starve() public {
        ToyBucket b = new ToyBucket(); b.consume(1000);              // a user empties the bucket
        for (uint i; i < 12; i++) { skip(300); b.refill(); }        // an attacker pokes it every 5 minutes for an hour
        emit log_named_uint("tokens after 1 hour", b.tokens());       // should be 10
        assertEq(b.tokens(), 0);                                      // never refilled
    }
}

Each call sees 300 seconds: 300 · 10 / 3600 = 0 (truncated), then resets last. The attacker burns a little gas and keeps instant redemptions at zero forever: a denial of service on the protocol's exit path. Fix: advance the clock only by the time that was actually credited, or keep the remainder.

uint add = dt * ratePerHour / 3600;
if (add > 0) { tokens = min(cap, tokens + add); last = block.timestamp; }
// or: scale tokens by 3600 and keep full precision, or advance last by add * 3600 / ratePerHour

Re-run: after the same hourly pokes, tokens becomes 10. General rule: anything that anyone can call and that mutates a clock or accumulator must not lose precision.

Step 6. Find the bug

// A. a lending market
uint price = ethUsd * weETH.getRate() / 1e18;       // LTV 93%

// B. rebase
function rebase(int128 accruedRewards) external onlyAdmin { totalValueOutOfLp = uint128(int128(totalValueOutOfLp) + accruedRewards); }

// C. claim a queued withdrawal
function claimWithdraw(uint id) external { ...; payable(owner).transfer(requests[id].amountOfEEth); }

// D. withdrawal request
uint shares = amount * totalShares / getTotalPooledEther();      // same formula as deposit

// E. register a validator
function registerValidator(bytes calldata pubkey, bytes calldata withdrawalCredentials, ...) external onlyOperator {
    depositContract.deposit{value: 1 ether}(pubkey, withdrawalCredentials, sig, root);
}
Answers
  1. A. The rate prices weETH at the protocol's claim on ETH. In market stress (Renzo's ezETH in April 2024, wide spreads on other restaking tokens in August 2024) the tradable price dips below it, while a liquidator can only exit at market. At a 93% LTV a small dip creates bad debt. Use the minimum of the rate-based value and a market oracle, and set LTVs and caps from observed depeg depth.
  2. B. No bound on the reported change. A compromised or buggy admin can inflate totalValueOutOfLp and raise every share's value (steal by withdrawing), or deflate it to force liquidations. The real flow checks that the implied APR of a report stays under a limit and requires oracle quorum; verify both and who can change the limit.
  3. C. Must pay min(requested, current value of the shares). Paying the requested amount makes a slashing loss fall on holders who did not exit and lets the pool become insolvent.
  4. D. Floor on a withdrawal charges too few shares; repeated small requests drain value. Use the ceiling.
  5. E. Credentials must be derived by the contract (the node contract / pod address), never passed by the operator; otherwise the first 1 ETH deposit with the operator's own credentials locks the validator to them (Lab 17, bug D).

Step 7. Study material

  • Renzo ezETH depeg, April 2024: on airdrop day the token fell far below its rate on several venues after a rush to sell into thin liquidity with no instant exit. Read the post-mortems and the lending-market parameter changes that followed. Which of those mitigations does the ether.fi rate limiter provide, and which does it not?
  • Market stress, August 2024: leveraged weETH loops were liquidated as the token traded below its exchange rate. Reconstruct how the loop works (deposit weETH, borrow ETH, buy more weETH) and why that makes the depeg self-reinforcing.
  • Read ether.fi's audit reports and bounty rules (several firms, including formal verification work); sort findings by: oracle and rebase, withdrawal accounting, access control, restaking integration.
  • Write a one-page comparison with Lido and Rocket Pool: who runs validators, who sets the rate, exit mechanism, how restaking adds risk.

Step 8. ether.fi checklist

  • Shares: rounding on every mint, burn, wrap and unwrap; totals never zero; transfers cannot create or lose value beyond dust.
  • Rebase: who can call, report validation, APR bound, negative reports, order relative to deposits and withdrawals in the same block.
  • Withdrawals: ticket accounting, finalization conditions, min payout rule, locked-for-withdrawal liquidity, reentrancy on ETH send, NFT transfer and invalidation.
  • Instant redemption: fee maths, rate limiter precision and permissions, reservation of liquidity for the queue, behaviour when limits are changed.
  • Validators: credentials derived internally, pre-deposit front-running, key reuse, exit handling, EigenPod checkpoints and slashing effects on totalValueOutOfLp.
  • Roles and upgrades: who holds each role, timelocks, pause powers, proxy patterns across versions.
  • Integrators: weETH price definition, caps and LTVs, L2 bridged rate feeds and their staleness.
Lab 20 deliverable(1) With the calculator, find how long a 1,000 ETH bucket with 10 ETH/hour refill takes to recover from empty to 50%. (2) Run test_starve, apply the fix and show 10 tokens after the hour. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet C (claim pays the requested amount).

Lab 21. Solana liquid staking: SPL Stake Pool and Marinade

In plain wordsSolana stake lives in stake accounts delegated to validators and rewards arrive each epoch. Marinade and the SPL Stake Pool pool that stake and issue a token whose price rises as rewards are added, so they must re-measure their stake every epoch.

Key terms: stake account, epoch, mSOL. See the glossary at the end.

Ethereum staking (Labs 17 to 20) is mostly about an oracle reporting balances. Solana staking is different: stake lives in stake accounts that a program can see and move, rewards are paid on-chain into those accounts at each epoch boundary (about two days), and the pool contract itself has to re-measure its stake every epoch. So the audit centres on three things: account validation (Lab 6 again, on stake accounts and PDAs), the epoch update cycle (a stale-state bug class like Kamino's), and exit design (delayed unstake versus instant unstake with a dynamic fee). This lab covers the shared SPL Stake Pool program (used by many liquid staking tokens) and Marinade, which has its own program.

Step 1. Solana staking in six facts

  1. A stake account belongs to the native Stake program. Two authorities: the staker (delegate, deactivate) and the withdrawer (withdraw, change authorities). A pool program holds both through PDAs.
  2. Stake is delegated to a validator's vote account. Activation and deactivation take effect at epoch boundaries and are rate-limited, so unstaking is not instant.
  3. Rewards are credited to the stake account at the start of each epoch for the previous one; they raise the account's lamports (auto-compound).
  4. A stake account must keep a rent-exempt reserve; splitting or withdrawing below it fails.
  5. Accounts can be split and merged if their states and authorities are compatible. Pools use transient stake accounts while moving stake between validators and the reserve.
  6. As far as I know Solana has no slashing for validators, so pool loss comes from smart-contract bugs, bad accounting or delinquent validators earning nothing. Check whether that has changed.

Step 2. SPL Stake Pool

PieceRole
StakePool accountManager, staker, mint, fee accounts, total_lamports, pool_token_supply, last_update_epoch, fees, preferred validators.
ValidatorList accountOne entry per validator: active stake, transient stake, last update epoch, vote account, status.
Reserve stake accountSOL deposited but not yet delegated; pays for WithdrawSol.
Withdraw authority PDAOwns all stake accounts and is the mint authority of the pool token.
Validator and transient stake PDAsDerived from (vote account, pool, seed). The program must recompute and compare them.

Epoch cycle. Every epoch someone must run, in order, UpdateValidatorListBalance (in batches over the validator list), UpdateStakePoolBalance, and CleanupRemovedValidatorEntries. Until that finishes, deposits and withdrawals are rejected because the rate is stale.

Rate            pool tokens per lamport = pool_token_supply / total_lamports
Deposit         tokens = lamports · supply / total_lamports        (floor; minus fees)
Withdraw        lamports = tokens · total_lamports / supply        (floor; minus fees)
Epoch update    total_lamports = reserve + Σ (active + transient)
Epoch fee       fee_lamports = epoch_fee · rewards;  fee_tokens = fee_lamports · supply / (total_new − fee_lamports)

Worked epoch. Pool: 2,000,000 SOL and 1,850,000 tokens (1 token = 1.0811 SOL). You deposit 10 SOL: 9.25 tokens. An epoch pays 752 SOL of rewards (about 6.5% a year); epoch fee 5% = 37.6 SOL, minted as 34.78 tokens to the manager. Rate goes from 1.08108 to 1.08147, so your 9.25 tokens are worth 10.0036 SOL (+0.036% in the epoch, about 6.2% a year net).

Step 3. Marinade

PieceRole
State and mSOL mintGlobal state, mSOL price, fees, treasury, authorities (admin and validator manager).
Validator and stake listsEach validator's score and active balance; each stake account's state; the process that decides how much to stake or unstake per validator each epoch.
ReserveUndelegated SOL; funds ticket claims and new stakes.
Liquidity pool (mSOL/SOL)Instant unstake: users sell mSOL into the pool for SOL and pay a dynamic fee.
Delayed-unstake ticketsorder_unstake burns mSOL and creates a ticket; claim pays SOL after the epoch boundary.
Crank instructionsPermissionless: update_active, update_deactivated, stake_reserve, deactivate_stake, merge_stakes and others that anyone can run each epoch.
Virtual staked   V = active + cooling down + reserve − circulating tickets
mSOL price       p = V / mSOL supply
Deposit          mSOL = lamports / p
Order unstake    burn m mSOL → ticket for m · p lamports; tickets increase 'circulating tickets'
Instant unstake  fee = maxFee − (maxFee − minFee) · min(1, solLeftInPool / target)        (linear in the pool's remaining SOL)

Worked price. Active 8,000,000, cooling down 100,000, reserve 50,000, tickets 20,000: V = 8,130,000. mSOL supply 7,000,000: price 1.1614. Depositing 100 SOL mints 86.10 mSOL.

Worked instant-unstake fee. Pool target 10,000 SOL, currently 6,000, fee range 0.3% to 3%. Unstaking 1,000 SOL worth leaves 5,000: fee = 3% − 2.7% · 0.5 = 1.65%. A near-empty pool charges close to 3%, which pays for the liquidity and discourages draining it. Formula from memory of the docs; verify in the program.

Why two exit routes. Delayed unstake is free but takes an epoch or more; instant unstake costs a variable fee but is immediate. Any bug that lets a user take the delayed route's price with the instant route's speed breaks the economics.

Step 6. Hands-on: a ticket that is not counted

A toy mSOL where order_unstake forgets to record the new ticket in the "circulating tickets" term of the price.

contract ToyMsol {
    uint public active = 1000; uint public reserve; uint public ticketsOut;
    uint public supply = 1000; uint public lpSol = 500;
    mapping(address => uint) public msol; mapping(address => uint) public ticket;
    function setMsol(address a, uint x) external { msol[a] = x; }
    function virtualStaked() public view returns (uint) { return active + reserve - ticketsOut; }
    function price() public view returns (uint) { return virtualStaked() * 1e18 / supply; }
    function orderUnstake(uint m) external {
        uint lam = m * price() / 1e18;
        msol[msg.sender] -= m; supply -= m; ticket[msg.sender] += lam;      // BUG: ticketsOut += lam is missing
    }
    function liquidUnstake(uint m) external returns (uint lam) {            // instant exit, paid from the pool's SOL
        lam = m * price() / 1e18; msol[msg.sender] -= m; supply -= m; lpSol -= lam;
    }
}
contract MsolTest is Test {
    function test_priceJump() public {
        ToyMsol t = new ToyMsol(); address alice = address(0xA); address atk = address(0xB);
        t.setMsol(alice, 400); t.setMsol(atk, 100);
        vm.prank(alice); t.orderUnstake(400);                  // mSOL burned, ticket created, price should stay 1.0
        emit log_named_uint("price", t.price());               // 1.666... instead of 1.0
        vm.prank(atk); uint got = t.liquidUnstake(100);        // attacker sells 100 mSOL into the pool
        emit log_named_uint("lamports received", got);         // 166, fair value is 100
        assertEq(got, 166);
    }
}

Burning 400 mSOL should remove 400 lamports of value from V, because those lamports now belong to the ticket. Without ticketsOut += lam the price jumps from 1.0 to 1.667, and anyone selling mSOL into the liquidity pool takes 66 lamports too many from it. Fix it and re-run: the attacker receives 100.

ticketsOut += lam;     // inside orderUnstake; decrease it again in claim()

Notice the structure: the bug is not in the exploit path; it is in a different instruction that changed supply without changing V. When you audit a price formula, list every instruction that changes any term and check each one changes all the terms it should.

Step 7. Find the bug

// A. stake pool deposit_sol: no epoch check
// (no comparison of stake_pool.last_update_epoch with Clock::get()?.epoch)

// B. deposit_stake: validator stake account
let validator_stake = next_account_info(accounts_iter)?;      // never recomputed from (vote account, pool)
Self::stake_merge(validator_stake, user_stake, ...)?;

// C. set_staker
if !(manager.is_signer || staker.is_signer) { return Err(...); }      // wrong: accepts either, but checks the keys?
if *manager.key != stake_pool.manager { return Err(...); }             // key compared, signer not required on this path

// D. epoch update
total_lamports = reserve_lamports + validator_list.iter().map(|v| v.active_stake_lamports).sum()
                 + transient_total;        // transient already included in active when decreasing

// E. marinade claim
pub fn claim(ctx: Context<Claim>) -> Result<()> {
    require!(ctx.accounts.reserve.lamports() >= ticket.lamports_amount);   // epoch condition never checked
    ...
}
Answers
  1. A. Rate sniping again. Between epochs the pool's total_lamports lags real rewards, so a deposit at the stale rate gets too many tokens and shares the next update. The real program refuses deposits and withdrawals unless last_update_epoch equals the current epoch.
  2. B. The validator's stake account must be recomputed as a PDA from the vote account and pool and compared with the account passed in. Otherwise an attacker supplies an account they control, the pool credits tokens for lamports it never takes control of, or withdrawals draw from a spoofed account.
  3. C. A signer-or-key check that does not tie the signer to the authority is Lab 6 bug 1. The signer must be the manager, whichever branch runs.
  4. D. Transient stake is already part of the validator's moved stake during a decrease; adding both counts the same lamports twice, so the rate rises without any reward and later depositors overpay while exiting holders overdraw.
  5. E. The claim must require that the ticket's creation epoch is over (so the stake had time to cool down) and that the reserve covers it without eating funds earmarked for others. Without the epoch rule an attacker orders and claims in the same epoch, using the reserve as a free, fee-less instant exit.

Step 8. Study material

  • November 2022, FTX collapse: mSOL and other liquid staking tokens traded below SOL, Marinade's instant-unstake fee rose toward its maximum and pool liquidity drained. Reconstruct the fee path with the calculator: what pool balance gives which fee?
  • Read the public audits of the SPL Stake Pool and Marinade (several firms). Sort findings by: account validation, epoch ordering, rounding and fees, authorities.
  • Read the native Stake program documentation on split, merge, activation and rent-exempt reserve. Write down three ways a pool could end up with a stake account it cannot merge.
  • Study validator concentration: stake-weighted performance, delinquent validators earning no rewards, and how each pool's validator-selection rules decentralise (or fail to).

Step 9. Solana staking checklist

  • Every stake and token account: owner program, PDA derivation from the right seeds and bump, mint, authority, and state (activating, active, deactivating, transient).
  • Epoch rules: update cycle complete before deposit and withdraw; crank instructions callable by anyone but safe in any order and in any batch size.
  • Rate and price formulas: every instruction that changes supply or any term changes the others; no double counting of transient stake; rounding in the pool's favour.
  • Fees: epoch fee, deposit, withdrawal and referral fees sum and round correctly; fee minting uses the post-fee total.
  • Authorities: manager, staker, deposit authority, validator manager, emergency roles; signer checks on each; transfer of authority flows.
  • Exit paths: delayed ticket maths, claim preconditions, instant-unstake fee bounds, liquidity pool accounting, behaviour when the pool is empty.
  • Stake mechanics: rent-exempt reserve, minimum delegation, split and merge compatibility, deactivating accounts, lockups.
  • Upgrade authority and multisig custody of admin keys.
Lab 21 deliverable(1) With the calculator find the pool balance at which the instant-unstake fee for a 1,000 SOL exit is exactly 1%. (2) Run test_priceJump, add the missing line and show the attacker receiving 100. (3) Write the account-validation table (Lab 6 format) for the stake pool DepositSol or WithdrawStake instruction.

Stage 7: Yield products

About 15 hours of study · needs: Stage 4 (AMM), Stage 6 (shares), Stage 3 for Exponent

Theory in plain words

Interest and principal can be separated. Splitting a yield-bearing asset gives a principal token, which pays a fixed amount at a date and trades below that amount before then, and a yield token, which collects all interest until the date. The discount on the principal token tells you the market's interest rate. As the date nears, the principal token's price drifts toward its face value, so any exchange for it must change its curve with time.

Aggregator vaults pool deposits and send them to strategies. The central question is 'what is the vault worth right now?', because share price, fees and withdrawals all depend on that number. Anything that lets someone fake the number, or deposit just before a known jump and leave just after, takes value from other holders. Defences you will meet: reports only at set moments, locking profit so it is released slowly, and loss rules for early leavers.

Check yourself before moving on
  1. Why is a principal token worth less than one unit before maturity?
  2. What does profit locking stop an attacker from doing?

Lab 22. Pendle (yield trading)

In plain wordsPendle splits an interest-earning asset into a principal token (a fixed amount at a date) and a yield token (all interest until then). Buying principal early at a discount is a fixed rate; buying yield is a leveraged bet on interest.

Key terms: PT, YT, maturity, implied APY. See the glossary at the end.

Pendle takes any yield-bearing asset (say a staked-ETH token) and splits it into two tradable pieces: a PT (principal token), which is worth exactly one unit of the underlying at a fixed date, and a YT (yield token), which receives all the yield the asset earns until that date. Buy a PT below par and you have a fixed rate; buy a YT and you are long yield with leverage. The audit surface is a mix of things you know (share-style accounting, an AMM, oracles) with two new ones: a yield index that can only go up, and an AMM whose curve changes with time.

Step 1. The cast

PieceRole
SY (Standardized Yield)A wrapper that gives every yield-bearing asset the same interface: deposit, redeem, exchangeRate (asset per SY). One SY contract per underlying; this is where each integration's risk lives.
PT and YTMinted together from SY for a given expiry. PT + YT = 1 unit of underlying at mint. After expiry the YT is worthless and PT redeems for the underlying.
PendleYieldTokenThe YT contract. Holds the SY backing all PT/YT, tracks the PY index and per-user interest.
PendleMarketV3AMM pool of PT and SY for one expiry, issues LP tokens, records an oracle observation array.
Router and approx libraryUser entry points; computes the exact input needed by a binary search off-chain-guided (ApproxParams).
Oracle contractsRead TWAPs of the PT-to-asset rate, the LP rate, and the YT rate.
vePENDLE, gauges, factoriesVote-escrow governance, incentive distribution, permissionless market creation.

Step 2. The maths

Mint                PT = YT = SY · pyIndex                 (in underlying units)
PY index            pyIndex = max(previous, SY.exchangeRate())      (monotonic: never decreases)
YT interest (in SY) ytBalance · (indexNow − indexUser) / (indexNow · indexUser)
PT redeem at expiry SY out = PT / pyIndex
Prices              PT = 1 / (1 + r)^t = e^(−r_cc · t)             r = implied APY, t in years
                    YT = 1 − PT                                     (in underlying units)
AMM (core)          p = PT / (PT + SY·index);   exchangeRate = ln(p / (1 − p)) / rateScalar + rateAnchor
                    rateScalar = scalarRoot · 365d / timeToExpiry;  impliedRate = ln(exchangeRate) · 365d / timeToExpiry

Worked example. Six months to expiry (t = 0.5), implied APY 3%.

  • PT = 1 / 1.03^0.5 = 0.98533. Buying it and holding to expiry returns 1 / 0.98533 − 1 = 1.489% in six months, which annualises to 3.0%: a fixed rate.
  • YT = 1 − 0.98533 = 0.01467: about 68× leverage on the yield.
  • If the asset actually earns 5% annualised, six months of yield is 1.05^0.5 − 1 = 2.469% of principal. YT return: 0.02469 / 0.01467 − 1 = +68%. If it only earns 1%, yield is 0.499%, a −66% return.
  • At a constant implied rate, PT drifts up to 1 as expiry approaches ("pull to par"). That is why the AMM's curve must change with time: its rateScalar grows as timeToExpiry shrinks, making the price curve steeper near expiry.
  • Each trade moves rateAnchor so the implied rate stays continuous across time; otherwise the price would jump when time passes.

Why the index only goes up. YT interest is computed from the change in index. If the index could fall, past payouts would have to be clawed back. Taking the max keeps accounting safe against honest dips, but it also means any one-time spike in the SY exchange rate is locked in forever. A spike that reverses leaves the contract owing yield it never earned.

Step 3. Play with it

Step 4. Read the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/pendle-finance/pendle-core-v2-public && cd pendle-core-v2-public/contracts
ls core core/YieldContracts core/Market core/StandardizedYield router oracles
Paths from memory; confirm.
