Step 5. Hands-on: a spike that the index never forgets

A toy YT contract whose SY rate can be pushed up for one transaction (standing in for a flash-loan-manipulable or donation-inflatable rate source).

contract RateSource { uint public rate = 1e18; function set(uint r) external { rate = r; } }
contract ToyYT {
    RateSource src; uint public index = 1e18; uint public syHeld;                 // SY backing all PT and YT
    mapping(address => uint) public yt; mapping(address => uint) public userIndex;
    constructor(RateSource s) { src = s; }
    function _update() internal { uint r = src.rate(); if (r > index) index = r; }   // monotonic max
    function mint(address to, uint sy) external { _update(); syHeld += sy; yt[to] += sy * index / 1e18; userIndex[to] = index; }
    function claim() external returns (uint sy) {
        _update();
        sy = yt[msg.sender] * (index - userIndex[msg.sender]) * 1e18 / (index * userIndex[msg.sender]);
        userIndex[msg.sender] = index; syHeld -= sy;
    }
    function ptRedeem(uint pt) external view returns (uint) { return pt * 1e18 / index; }   // SY per PT at expiry
}
contract IndexTest is Test {
    function test_ratchet() public {
        RateSource src = new RateSource(); ToyYT t = new ToyYT(src);
        address atk = address(0xA77);
        t.mint(atk, 1000e18);                                    // 1000 SY split: attacker gets 1000 YT (PT goes to others)
        src.set(2e18);                                           // rate spikes for one transaction
        vm.prank(atk); uint got = t.claim();                     // attacker claims "interest"
        src.set(1e18);                                           // spike disappears
        emit log_named_uint("attacker SY claimed", got);         // 500
        emit log_named_uint("SY per 1000 PT at expiry", t.ptRedeem(1000e18));   // 500 instead of 1000
        assertEq(got, 500e18);
        assertEq(t.ptRedeem(1000e18), 500e18);
    }
}

The attacker never earned yield, yet takes 500 of the 1000 SY that back the PT, and PT holders can redeem only 500 SY (worth 500 underlying) against the 1000 they were promised. Real attacks need a rate source that can be bent, such as an ERC-4626 vault vulnerable to donation, a rate derived from a spot pool, or a rate read from a contract with a bug. Fix with a bound on how fast the index can rise:

function _update() internal {
    uint r = src.rate();
    if (r > index) { require(r <= index * (1e18 + MAX_STEP) / 1e18, "rate jump"); index = r; }
}

Re-run: the spike makes claim revert. Real systems layer this with SY-level checks and with a market-by-market review; Pendle is permissionless at market creation, so the review of each SY is the integrator's and auditor's job.

Step 6. Find the bug

// A. an SY wrapper
function exchangeRate() public view returns (uint) { return vault.totalAssets() * 1e18 / vault.totalSupply(); }
// vault.totalAssets() = token.balanceOf(vault)

// B. lending market pricing PT collateral
uint ptPrice = IPMarket(market).getPtToAssetSpot();            // spot, same block

// C. PT price helper
uint t = expiry - block.timestamp;                             // unchecked arithmetic in an older compiler
uint price = 1e18 * 1e18 / (1e18 + rate) ** (t / 365 days);

// D. YT transfer
function _transfer(address from, address to, uint amt) internal override { balances[from] -= amt; balances[to] += amt; }

// E. incentives for LPs
function addLiquidity(uint amt) external { lp[msg.sender] += amt; totalLp += amt; _updateReward(msg.sender); }
Answers
  1. A. A donation changes totalAssets in one transaction, spiking the rate. Because the PY index ratchets, one donation (or any flash-loan-inflatable source) gives a permanent index gain, which is the test_ratchet exploit. SY wrappers should use rates that cannot be moved atomically, and the index must be bounded.
  2. B. Spot price from an AMM can be moved in one transaction. Use the TWAP oracle with a duration long enough for the market's depth, ensure the observation cardinality is big enough (else the call reverts or reads stale data), and treat the rate near expiry specially.
  3. C. After expiry expiry - block.timestamp underflows (wraps) on an unchecked compiler, giving a gigantic exponent. PT after expiry is exactly 1 underlying; clamp the time to zero and return 1 at or after expiry. Also decimals and the integer exponent truncation in t / 365 days drop fractions of a year.
  4. D. Interest must be settled for both sender and receiver before a balance moves. Otherwise the new holder's stored index is stale (or zero), and they can claim yield that accrued while the old holder held the tokens. That is a theft of past interest.
  5. E. Reward-index pattern: accrue rewards (_updateReward) before changing the balance and total. Updating after lets a new depositor claim rewards earned by others for the period before they joined.

Step 7. Study material

  • Penpie, September 2024 (about $27M): Penpie, a reward-boosting layer on top of Pendle, let anyone register a Pendle market. The attacker registered a malicious market with a fake SY, then used reentrancy during reward harvesting to claim inflated rewards. Lesson: a permissionless market is untrusted code; every contract that integrates a market must treat its SY, its tokens and its callbacks as hostile. Read the post-mortem and rebuild the call sequence.
  • Exchange-rate manipulation incidents in 2025 (for example Resupply, roughly $9–10M, which exploited an inflatable vault rate): read a post-mortem and map it to snippet A. These are good practice for deciding which SY rates are safe to wrap.
  • Read the Pendle V2 audit reports (several firms) and the public bug bounty; classify findings: index and SY, AMM maths, router and approximation, oracle, governance.
  • Read the market maths article in Pendle's documentation. Derive by hand that the implied rate stays unchanged after a trade because of the rateAnchor update.

Step 8. Pendle checklist

  • SY: exchange rate source, manipulability (donation, flash loans, oracle), decimals, behaviour on pause or depeg, reward handling, permitted deposit tokens.
  • PY index: monotonic by design, bounded growth, init value, behaviour at expiry, interest update on every balance change (mint, burn, transfer, redeem).
  • Rounding: PT/YT minting, redemption, interest accrual, LP mint and burn always against the user at the boundary.
  • AMM: time decay and rate scalar, rateAnchor update order, fee maths, exchangeRate floor at 1, behaviour at and after expiry, extreme proportions near 0 and 1.
  • Router: slippage and deadline, approximation tolerance, receiver, callbacks, tokens in/out validation, native ETH.
  • Oracle: duration versus cardinality, direction (PT to asset vs asset to PT), post-expiry treatment, decimals.
  • Rewards and incentives: reward index updated before balance changes, multiple reward tokens, claim reentrancy.
  • Integrators and factories: market and SY trust, whitelisting, permissionless creation abuse, vePENDLE and gauge voting accounting (epoch boundaries).
Lab 22 deliverable(1) With the calculator, find the realised APY at which a YT bought at an implied 3% breaks even for a 91 day expiry. (2) Run test_ratchet, add the bound and show the claim reverting. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet D (interest not settled on transfer).

Lab 23. Yearn V3

In plain wordsYearn V3 vaults pool deposits and send them to strategies that earn yield. The share price changes only when a strategy reports profit, and profit is released slowly to stop quick in-and-out gaming.

Key terms: strategy, report, profit locking. See the glossary at the end.

Yearn V3 is an ERC-4626 yield aggregator: users deposit one asset, and managers route it to strategies that earn yield elsewhere (lending, LP positions, staking). Two ideas make it different from the vaults you have seen. First, share price only moves when a report happens, not whenever tokens arrive, which blocks donation-style inflation. Second, profit is locked and released slowly, so you cannot deposit just before a harvest and withdraw just after. Most V3 risk lives in what the strategy reports and who controls the allocation.

Step 1. The cast

PieceRole
Tokenized StrategyA strategy is a small contract (BaseStrategy) that holds the position; all ERC-4626 logic lives in one shared TokenizedStrategy implementation reached by delegatecall in the fallback. The author writes only _deployFunds, _freeFunds, _harvestAndReport, and optionally _tend, limits and _emergencyWithdraw.
VaultV3 (Vyper)Multi-strategy ERC-4626 vault. Holds idle funds plus debt allocated to up to ten strategies; tracks total_idle, total_debt, per-strategy current_debt and max_debt; processes reports; locks profit.
RolesA bitmask per address: add or revoke strategy, queue manager, debt manager, reporting manager, deposit/withdraw limit managers, minimum idle, profit unlock time, emergency manager. A single holder of several roles is a trust assumption.
AccountantSeparate contract the vault calls on a report to charge performance and management fees and give refunds.
Keepers and debt allocatorBots that call report, tend and update_debt; a debt allocator contract can choose targets automatically.
Auctions and swappersHelpers a strategy uses to sell reward tokens (an on-chain Dutch auction, or a DEX swapper).

Step 2. The maths

Share price        pps = totalAssets / totalSupply         (both only change at reports, deposits, withdrawals)
Report             gain = newValue − recordedValue;   fee shares to Accountant; rest of gain → shares minted to the VAULT (locked)
Unlock             locked shares are released linearly over profitMaxUnlockTime (default 7 days):
                   pps(u) = (A + profit) / (S + feeShares + lockedShares · (1 − u))       u = fraction unlocked
Unrealised loss    a withdrawer from a strategy whose value is below its recorded debt is charged a proportional share
Max loss           withdraw and redeem take a max_loss in basis points; revert if the loss exceeds it (ERC-4626 defaults to 0)
Withdraw order     idle first, then strategies in the withdraw queue; each limited by what it can free right now

Worked report. Vault: 1,000,000 assets, 1,000,000 shares (pps 1.0). A strategy earns 10,000. Performance fee 10%.

  • Fee 1,000 → 1,000 fee shares to the Accountant.
  • Remaining profit 9,000 → 9,000 shares minted to the vault itself and locked, so the share price right after the report is 1,010,000 / 1,010,000 = 1.0000.
  • As the shares unlock over 7 days, supply falls and pps climbs toward 1,010,000 / 1,001,000 = 1.00899. Holders earn 9,000 of the 10,000 (the other 1,000 went to fees).
  • A new depositor on day 0 buys at 1.0000 but only earns the part of the profit that unlocks while they hold: the flat entry price is the protection, not the fee.

Worked unrealised loss. A strategy has current_debt 1,000 but is worth 800 (a 20% unrealised loss). Alice wants 100 assets out. The vault computes her share of the loss (20%) and she would receive 80. With max_loss = 0 (the default) the call reverts. She must set max_loss ≥ 2000 bps to accept it. This stops a first mover from pushing the hole onto the people who stay.

Step 3. Play with it

Step 4. Read the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/yearn/yearn-vaults-v3         # contracts/VaultV3.vy
git clone https://github.com/yearn/tokenized-strategy       # src/TokenizedStrategy.sol, src/BaseStrategy.sol
git clone https://github.com/yearn/tokenized-strategy-periphery   # Accountant, DebtAllocator, Auction, swappers
Names from memory; confirm. VaultV3 is Vyper (see Lab 12); the strategy side is Solidity.
ReadLook for
VaultV3.vy: process_reportGain and loss maths, fee call to the Accountant, share minting to the vault, unlock rate and date update when a second report arrives mid-lock, burning of locked shares to cover losses.
VaultV3.vy: update_debtHow the amount actually moved is measured (balance difference), max_debt, minimum_total_idle, ordering of state updates and external calls.
VaultV3.vy: _redeem, _assess_share_of_unrealised_lossesWithdraw loop through the queue, max_loss checks, rounding of shares and assets, strategies that return less than requested.
TokenizedStrategy.solreport, tend, shutdownStrategy, _deposit/_withdraw with maxLoss, profit locking, fee shares, the delegatecall dispatch.
BaseStrategy.solWhich functions are guarded (onlySelf, onlyKeepers, onlyManagement), how the fallback forwards calls.
A concrete strategyWhat _harvestAndReport reads: balanceOf, external protocol values, spot prices; what is swapped and with what slippage.
Roles and factoryRole bitmask checks, open reporting, who can add a strategy and how a strategy is validated.

Step 5. Hands-on: lying at report time

A toy vault whose strategy value comes from a manipulable source. In the buggy version the reported value moves totalAssets immediately; in the fixed version profit is locked and released over seven days.

contract MockOracle { uint public value = 1000; function set(uint v) external { value = v; } }   // value of the strategy position
contract ToyVault {
    MockOracle o; bool public lockProfit;
    uint public cash = 1000; uint public supply = 1000; uint public stored = 1000;
    uint public lockedProfit; uint public lockEnd; uint constant UNLOCK = 7 days;
    mapping(address => uint) public shares;
    constructor(MockOracle _o, bool _lock) { o = _o; lockProfit = _lock; }
    function setShares(address a, uint s) external { shares[a] = s; }
    function totalAssets() public view returns (uint) {
        if (!lockProfit || block.timestamp >= lockEnd) return stored;
        return stored - lockedProfit * (lockEnd - block.timestamp) / UNLOCK;
    }
    function report() external {
        uint v = o.value();
        if (lockProfit && v > stored) { lockedProfit = v - stored; lockEnd = block.timestamp + UNLOCK; }
        stored = v;
    }
    function redeem(uint s) external returns (uint a) {          // pays real tokens out of 'cash'
        a = s * totalAssets() / supply; shares[msg.sender] -= s; supply -= s; cash -= a; stored -= a;
    }
}
contract ReportTest is Test {
    function _run(bool lock) internal returns (uint got) {
        MockOracle o = new MockOracle(); ToyVault v = new ToyVault(o, lock);
        address atk = address(0xA77); v.setShares(atk, 100);          // attacker owns 10% of the shares
        o.set(2000);                                                 // attacker bends the value source (spot pool, donation, ...)
        v.report();
        vm.prank(atk); got = v.redeem(100);
    }
    function test_bug() public { uint got = _run(false); emit log_named_uint("attacker got", got); assertEq(got, 200); }   // fair value: 100
    function test_fix() public { uint got = _run(true);  emit log_named_uint("attacker got", got); assertEq(got, 100); }
}

Without locking, the manipulated value doubles the share price at once. The attacker takes 200 real tokens for shares worth 100, leaving 800 tokens to back 900 shares: everyone else is short about 11%. With locking, the same fake profit shows up only gradually: on day 0 the attacker still gets 100. In a real vault the attack also needs a way to push the value, and the defence is layered: manipulation-resistant valuation, locking, max_loss, limits, and keepers that only report in safe conditions.

Lesson: a report is a trusted statement of value. Anything a strategy reads while computing it (spot pool ratios, an external vault's convertToAssets, a rebasing token balance) is part of the attack surface.

Step 6. Find the bug

// A. a strategy's harvest
function _harvestAndReport() internal override returns (uint) {
    uint rewards = reward.balanceOf(address(this));
    swapper.swap(reward, asset, rewards, 0);                       // minAmountOut = 0
    return asset.balanceOf(address(this)) + staking.balanceOf(address(this));
}

// B. an integrator zap
vault.redeem(shares, msg.sender, msg.sender, 10_000, new address[](0));   // max_loss hard-coded to 100%

// C. update_debt
current_debt[strategy] += amount;                                 // before the transfer
strategy.deposit(amount, address(this));                          // returns shares; actual assets moved not measured

// D. process_report, second report during an unlock period
locked_shares = new_locked_shares;                                // old remainder overwritten
profit_unlocking_rate = locked_shares / unlock_time;

// E. strategy
function report() external returns (uint, uint) { return _report(); }     // no onlyKeepers
Answers
  1. A. Selling rewards with a zero minimum output hands the harvest to any sandwich bot, and the number returned by _harvestAndReport is lower than it should be; worse, if the swap is manipulated up front the strategy books a wrong value. Use an auction with a floor price, an oracle-bound minimum, and private order flow for keepers.
  2. B. A hard-coded 100% max loss turns the vault's protection off. A user withdrawing through the zap during a strategy loss silently takes the loss that the standard path would refuse. Pass the user's chosen max_loss, default 0.
  3. C. The recorded debt must reflect what the strategy actually received (measure the balance difference around the deposit). A strategy with a deposit fee, rounding or a partial fill leaves the vault with debt recorded at a higher value than reality, hiding a loss until withdrawals fail.
  4. D. The unreleased shares from the first report must be carried into the new rate: (remaining old locked + new locked) / unlock_time. Overwriting makes the old remainder disappear from the unlock schedule, so those shares stay locked forever (an accounting loss for holders) or release incorrectly.
  5. E. Anyone can force a report at a time of their choosing, for example right after distorting the value, or to trigger fee charging and profit locking at a bad moment. report and tend must be restricted to keepers or the management role.

Step 7. Study material

  • Yearn yDAI vault, February 2021 (about $2.8M): a flash loan moved Curve pool prices around a vault action that trusted the pool's current value. Compare with your test_bug: where does the vault read a value, and what moves it?
  • Legacy iearn yUSDT, April 2023 (about $10M): an old, misconfigured pool token minted more than it should against a manipulated state. Lesson: deprecated contracts still hold funds; ask what is deployed, not just what is current. Read the post-mortem for the exact mechanism; my summary is from memory.
  • Read the V3 audit reports from the repositories and docs (several independent reviewers) and sort findings: share accounting, debt and loss maths, roles, strategy template, periphery contracts.
  • Write a table of every external call a strategy makes during report, and for each, what the call could return if an attacker controlled the other side.

Step 8. Yearn V3 checklist

  • Share maths: preview functions match actual execution; rounding in favour of the vault; zero-supply case; locked shares excluded or included consistently.
  • Report: _harvestAndReport uses only manipulation-resistant values; profit locking, fee shares and loss absorption in the right order; multiple reports inside one unlock window.
  • Debt: movement measured by balance difference; max_debt and minimum idle respected; behaviour when a strategy returns less than requested.
  • Withdrawals: queue ordering, unrealised-loss share, max_loss semantics on every entry point (including wrappers), reentrancy through strategy callbacks.
  • Roles: each privileged function checks the right role; holders of multiple roles; open mode (anyone may call) used only where safe; emergency shutdown paths.
  • Strategy template: guarded functions, delegatecall fallback storage layout, decimals, allowance patterns, fee-on-transfer assets excluded.
  • Periphery: Accountant fee limits and refunds, auctions (floor price, settlement), swappers (min out, path), allocator permissions.
  • Upgradability and factories: who can deploy vaults and strategies, and what governance can change after the fact.
Lab 23 deliverable(1) With the calculator compute the pps at day 1, 3.5 and 7 for a 20,000 profit on a 2,000,000 vault with a 15% fee. (2) Run both tests and then add a reporting delay (keeper-only) as an extra fix; explain what it does and does not stop. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet A (zero minimum out on reward swaps).

Lab 24. Exponent (Solana yield stripping)

In plain wordsExponent does Pendle's job on Solana. This lab teaches you to map an unfamiliar Solana program from its published interface (the IDL) and to check that the code you read is the code that runs.

Key terms: IDL, tranche, program hash. See the glossary at the end.

Exponent is Pendle's idea on Solana: split a yield-bearing asset into a fixed-rate piece and a variable-yield piece, and trade both on a market. This lab is different from the others: I could confirm the public facts but not the internal code, so it teaches you to derive the audit map yourself from the open-source repository and its IDL. That is the skill you need for any new protocol anyway.

What I verified from public sources, and what I did not
  • Verified: Exponent is a Solana yield-stripping and interest-swap exchange. Deposits of a yield-bearing asset become a principal-side token (the app calls them Income tokens, PT) and a yield token (YT), traded on a "time-dynamic" AMM. App version 1 had Income (fixed yield), Farm (variable yield and points exposure) and Liquidity; version 2 adds yield limit orders, active liquidity, and strategy vaults with on-chain limits on what managers can do. It has also launched a risk-tranching product (senior and junior shares of one yield source) with OnRe, and PT/YT markets are used by other protocols (for example Kamino and Loopscale).
  • Verified: the core program is open source (Anchor, license BUSL-1.1) at github.com/exponent-finance/exponent-core. Its README gives the mainnet program id ExponentnaRg3CQbW6dqQNZKXp7gtZ9DGMp1cwC4HAS7, an on-chain program hash, and the deployed commit. Audit reports from OtterSec, Offside Labs and Certora are in the exponent-audits repository; a bug bounty page is linked from the README.
  • Not verified: instruction names, account layouts, the exact AMM formula, how yield is tracked for YT held in a wallet, and the tranche rules. Everything below that touches those is a question for you to answer from the IDL and source, not a claim.

Step 1. What carries over from earlier labs

ConceptFromQuestion to answer for Exponent
SY wrapper and rate sourceLab 22Where does the exchange rate for each supported asset come from (a stake pool, a lending reserve, a vault)? Is that account validated by owner, key and freshness? Can one spike ratchet an index?
Monotonic yield indexLab 22Is there a max-style index? Is growth bounded?
PT price and time decayLab 22What is the AMM formula and how does it behave at and after maturity?
Interest accounting for YTLab 22, Lab 6An ordinary SPL token transfer cannot run program code (Token-2022 hooks aside). So how does the program settle interest between holders: a stake-like position account, a claim instruction with an index checkpoint, or a hook?
Account validationLab 6Every instruction's account list: owner, signer, PDA seeds, program ids for CPIs.
Vaults and manager limitsLab 23What exactly can a vault manager do on chain, and what stops it from doing more?
IntegrationsLabs 7, 8, 22How do lending markets price PT collateral, and what happens at maturity or when the SY rate source reverts?

Step 2. Hands-on: build the map from the IDL

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/exponent-finance/exponent-core && cd exponent-core
ls idls programs libraries tests
Read README.md first, then use the script below on idls/*.json to list every instruction and the flags on every account.
# idl_map.py   run: python3 idl_map.py      (Anchor IDL, old or new format)
import json, glob

def walk(accounts, depth=1):
    for a in accounts:
        if 'accounts' in a:                                  # nested account group (older IDL format)
            print('   ' * depth + a['name'] + ':'); walk(a['accounts'], depth + 1); continue
        flags = []
        if a.get('isMut') or a.get('writable'): flags.append('mut')
        if a.get('isSigner') or a.get('signer'): flags.append('SIGNER')
        if a.get('pda'): flags.append('pda')
        if a.get('address'): flags.append('fixed-address')
        print('   ' * depth + a['name'].ljust(28) + ' '.join(flags))

for f in sorted(glob.glob('idls/*.json')):
    idl = json.load(open(f))
    print('=====', f, idl.get('name') or idl.get('metadata', {}).get('name'))
    for ins in idl['instructions']:
        print('\n' + ins['name']); walk(ins['accounts'])

I wrote this from the general Anchor IDL layout, not from Exponent's files; adjust key names if a field is missing. Then do these four steps:

  1. Group the instructions by purpose: initialise (vault, market, SY), strip and merge, trade, liquidity, claim, admin, vault-manager. Name each group in your notes.
  2. Mark every account without pda or fixed-address that is not a signer. Each of those needs a runtime check: find it in programs/ (a typed Account<T> constraint, a has_one, or a manual comparison). Missing ones are your first findings.
  3. Find the admin and manager instructions (names containing admin, manager, set, update, emergency). For each: who must sign, and is that signer's key stored in the program's own state or hard-coded?
  4. Verify the deployment. Compare the program hash on mainnet with the README, to confirm the repository you read is what runs: 
    cargo install solana-verify
solana-verify get-program-hash -u mainnet-beta ExponentnaRg3CQbW6dqQNZKXp7gtZ9DGMp1cwC4HAS7
# compare with the 'On-chain Program Hash' in the README; for a full rebuild use: solana-verify verify-from-repo ...
    Commands from memory; check the tool's help. Also note the upgrade authority from solana program show and who controls it.

Step 3. Tranching: a waterfall you can model today

A risk-tranched product splits one yield source into a senior share (paid first, protected by a first-loss buffer) and a junior share (paid after, absorbs losses first). The rules below are the usual structure; Exponent's actual parameters must be read from its docs and code.

Yield waterfall   total yield Y;  senior gets min(Y, seniorTarget · seniorAssets);  junior gets the remainder
Loss waterfall    total loss L;   junior loses min(L, juniorAssets);  senior loses the rest
Leverage of junior   juniorYield = (Y − seniorPaid) / juniorAssets    (can be large or negative)

Worked numbers (illustrative). Senior 800, junior 200 (total 1,000). Source yields 12% = 120. Senior target 8% of 800 = 64. Junior gets 120 − 64 = 56, which is 28% on 200. If the source earns only 6% (60): senior gets 60 (short by 4) and junior gets 0. A loss of 150: junior goes 200 to 50, senior is untouched; a loss of 300 wipes junior (200) and cuts senior by 100.

Step 4. Hands-on: the waterfall that is not a waterfall

contract ToyTranche {
    uint public senior = 800; uint public junior = 200;
    function applyLoss(uint loss) external {              // loss shared pro rata
        uint total = senior + junior;
        uint sLoss = loss * senior / total;
        senior -= sLoss; junior -= loss - sLoss;
    }
}
contract TrancheTest is Test {
    function test_prorata() public {
        ToyTranche t = new ToyTranche();
        t.applyLoss(150);
        emit log_named_uint("senior", t.senior());             // 680
        emit log_named_uint("junior", t.junior());             // 170
        assertEq(t.senior(), 680);                             // senior lost 120 although junior had a 200 buffer
    }
}

The senior holders believed they were protected by 200 of first-loss capital. Pro rata sharing makes them pay 80% of the loss. Fix: junior first.

uint jl = loss > junior ? junior : loss;
junior -= jl; senior -= loss - jl;

Re-run with 150 (senior stays 800, junior 50) and 300 (junior 0, senior 700). Then think about the harder real-world version: junior holders can exit before a loss is recorded if their NAV is stale, leaving senior holders to absorb it. What would you check to see that exits are priced after losses, and who may report a loss?

Step 5. Find the bug

// A. rate source (Anchor-style)
pub rate_source: AccountInfo<'info>,                          // e.g. a lending reserve or stake pool
let rate = read_rate(&ctx.accounts.rate_source)?;             // key and owner never compared to the SY's stored source

// B. redeem principal
require!(clock.unix_timestamp <= vault.maturity_ts);          // redeem allowed while time is still before maturity

// C. yield accounting
pub struct YieldPosition { pub owner: Pubkey, pub yt_amount: u64, pub last_index: u128 }
// PDA seeds: [b"position", vault.key()]                      // user key missing

// D. vault manager action
invoke(&ix, &[a, b, c, ctx.accounts.target_program.clone()])?;   // target_program is AccountInfo, manager chooses it

// E. PT mint
let pt = sy_amount * index / INDEX_SCALE;                      // u64 * u128 cast to u64
vault.total_pt += pt as u64;
Answers
  1. A. Lab 6 bug 7 on a Pendle-style index: the attacker passes an account they own with a high "rate", and the index ratchets up for good. Compare the key with the one stored in the SY configuration, check the owner program, and bound the growth (Lab 22).
  2. B. The comparison is reversed: principal must be redeemable at or after maturity, not before. (Merging PT and YT back into SY is allowed before maturity; redeeming PT alone for underlying is not.) Reversed time comparisons are common in code reviews because the test passes at one boundary.
  3. C. Without the owner in the seeds every user maps to the same position account: one shared position, shared interest. Seeds need the vault and the owner (Lab 6 bug 4).
  4. D. A manager-chosen program is an arbitrary CPI. A strategy vault that promises "hard-coded constraints" must allow only a list of known program ids and instruction types, validated on chain.
  5. E. Mixed widths: multiplying and then casting to u64 can silently truncate in release builds. Do the arithmetic in u128, use checked_*, convert with try_from, and round in the direction that favours the vault.

Step 6. Study material

  • Read the three audit reports in the exponent-audits repository (the OtterSec admin audit, Offside Labs Oct 2024, Certora June 2025). Make one table: finding, severity, instruction, bug class from Lab 6, fix. Predict, before you read, what each firm focused on.
  • Read the bug-bounty page's scope and severity table. A bounty scope is a free list of what the team fears most.
  • I did not find a public exploit of Exponent's core program in my searches; confirm that against the bounty page and recent security news before you rely on it.
  • Read the Penpie post-mortem from Lab 22 again. Which of its lessons apply to a protocol where other protocols (lending markets, vaults) integrate Exponent PT and YT tokens?

Step 7. Exponent checklist

  • Every account in every instruction: signer, owner, discriminator, PDA seeds with user and vault, stored-key comparisons for rate sources and mints; program ids for CPIs.
  • Rate sources: validated account, freshness, decimals, bounded index growth, behaviour when the source reverts or is closed.
  • Maturity logic: comparison directions at the boundary, what each token is worth after maturity, behaviour if a vault never gets settled.
  • Yield accounting for YT: where interest is checkpointed, what happens on transfers, closing and re-opening positions, rounding of per-index maths.
  • AMM: formula bounds, time decay, anchor updates, fee maths, extremes of reserves, oracle or TWAP outputs consumed by other protocols.
  • Limit orders: signature or PDA authority, price units (APY versus price), partial fills, cancellation, matching ordering.
  • Vaults: manager permissions enforced on chain, allowed programs, position limits, withdrawal rights, share accounting from Lab 23.
  • Tranches: loss and yield waterfall order, exit pricing after losses, who reports, caps.
  • Admin: upgrade authority, multisig, pause powers, parameter ranges; verified build matches the repository.
Lab 24 deliverable(1) Run idl_map.py on the real IDL files and attach the output for the five instructions you consider highest risk. (2) For two of them, write the account-validation table (Lab 6 format) with the source line enforcing each check. (3) Run test_prorata, apply the fix, and write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet D. (4) Report whether the on-chain program hash matches the README.

Stage 8: Cross-chain messaging

About 15 hours of study · needs: Stages 1, 3 and 4

Theory in plain words

Chains cannot see each other. Cross-chain protocols make a group of outside parties (guardians, verifier networks, oracle networks) vouch that something happened on another chain. So the real security of any cross-chain application is: how many independent parties must be fooled, versus how much value they protect. A single verifier protecting hundreds of millions is a single point of failure, however good the code is.

Good designs add layers: a second independent approval, limits on how much can pass in a time window, and a way to halt. The receiving application has two jobs the protocol cannot do for it: check who sent the message (the source chain and the sending contract) and make sure each message is processed once. Most real losses came from stolen keys, a single verifier, or a missing check on the receiver, not from broken cryptography.

Check yourself before moving on
  1. How many keys must an attacker control to forge a message verified by 2 required and 3 optional verifiers with a threshold of 2?
  2. What are the two checks a receiving application must always make?

Lab 25. Wormhole (cross-chain messaging)

In plain wordsA chain cannot read another chain, so Wormhole uses 19 guardians who sign what they see; 13 signatures make a message valid. The receiving application must still check who sent the message and use it only once.

Key terms: guardian, VAA, emitter, replay. See the glossary at the end.

A blockchain cannot read another blockchain. Every cross-chain protocol therefore has to answer one question: why should chain B believe that something happened on chain A? Wormhole's answer is a group of 19 independent guardians who watch each chain and sign what they see; if at least 13 of 19 sign, the signed bundle (a VAA) is accepted anywhere. Everything else (token transfers, relayers, rate limits) is built on top. Cross-chain bugs fall in two layers: the verification layer (are the signatures and the signer set really checked?) and the application layer (does the receiving contract check who sent the message, and that it is used only once?). Most big losses came from one of these two.

Step 1. How a message travels

  1. Emit: a contract on the source chain calls the Core Contract's publishMessage, which logs the message (chain id of the emitter, emitter address, sequence number, payload, consistency level).
  2. Observe and sign: guardians watch the Core Contract on every chain, wait for finality, and each signs the message body. For some chains a delegated subset observes and the full set signs after a delegate quorum agrees; the final VAA is still 13-of-19.
  3. Relay: anyone may carry the VAA to the destination chain (a user, a bot, or a relayer service such as the Executor framework). The relayer is not trusted for correctness.
  4. Verify and execute: the destination application calls the destination Core Contract to verify the VAA's signatures against the current guardian set, then checks the emitter and marks it consumed, then acts.
PieceRole
Core Contract (per chain)Publishes messages; verifies VAAs against the guardian set; upgrades the guardian set through governance VAAs.
Guardian network19 validator companies in a peer-to-peer network. Quorum floor(2n/3) + 1 = 13.
VAAVersion, guardian-set index, list of (guardian index, signature), and a body: timestamp, nonce, emitter chain, emitter address, sequence, consistency level, payload.
Token transfer contracts (wrapped token transfers, formerly "Token Bridge")Lock native tokens on the origin chain and mint wrapped tokens elsewhere; a message type for attestation (token metadata) and one for transfer.
Native Token Transfers and CCTP integrationVariants that burn and mint the native token instead of wrapping it.
Governor and Global AccountantGuardian-side safety nets: per-chain transfer limits with delays for large amounts, and a ledger that checks wrapped supply never exceeds what is locked.

Step 2. The maths and the checks

Quorum              q = floor(2n/3) + 1                       n = 19 → q = 13
Forgery needs       k ≥ q compromised guardian keys           (13 of 19)
Halt needs          honest guardians < q                       (7 or more offline or malicious)
VAA hash            keccak256(keccak256(body))                (signed digest; also the replay key)
Signature check     indices strictly increasing, each ecrecover(hash) == guardianSet[index], count ≥ q
Guardian set        new set activates, old set stays valid for a short window (about a day) then expires
Token amount        normalised to at most 8 decimals on the wire; dust below that is not transferred

Worked numbers. With 19 guardians the attacker needs 13 keys; losing 7 honest guardians stops the network. Compare Ronin (5 of 9 validators, March 2022): a smaller n makes a lower absolute number of keys sufficient.

Decimals. A transfer of 1.123456789012345678 of an 18-decimal token is normalised to 8 decimals: 112345678. The remaining 0.000000009012345678 is dust that stays with the sender. On the other side the amount is multiplied back by 10(decimals − 8). A wrong exponent here would mint 1010 times too much.

Step 3. Read the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/wormhole-foundation/wormhole && cd wormhole
ls ethereum/contracts ethereum/contracts/bridge solana/bridge/program/src node/pkg
Also read wormhole-solidity-sdk (receiver helpers) and the docs on VAAs. Paths from memory; confirm.
ReadLook for
ethereum/contracts/Messages.solparseAndVerifyVM, verifySignatures, parseVM (parse only), quorum, strictly increasing indices, guardian-set expiry check, the double hash.
Implementation.sol, Governance.solpublishMessage fee and sequence, governance VAA checks (emitter, chain, module), guardian-set upgrade and expiry.
bridge/Bridge.solcompleteTransfer: verify, emitter registered for the chain, replay flag set before the transfer, normalise and denormalise, fee, wrapped versus native branch, outstanding amount accounting.
bridge/BridgeGovernance.solregisterChain: which emitter is trusted per source chain; who can change it.
solana/bridge/program/src/api/verify_signature.rs and post_vaa.rsHow the secp256k1 precompile result is read through the instructions sysvar; how the signature set account is created; how the posted VAA account is derived.
node/pkg/governor, node/pkg/accountantLimits and delay rules, how violations are handled.

Step 4. Hands-on: a bridge that trusts any signed message

This model builds a real quorum check with ECDSA signatures from four guardians (quorum 3), then a receiving contract with the two most common integrator bugs: no emitter check and no replay protection. Guardians are honest here: they sign what they observe, including a message published by an attacker's contract.

contract ToyCore {
    address[] public guardians;
    constructor(address[] memory g) { guardians = g; }
    function quorum() public view returns (uint) { return guardians.length * 2 / 3 + 1; }
    struct VM { uint16 emitterChain; bytes32 emitterAddress; uint64 sequence; bytes payload; bytes32 hash; }
    function parseAndVerify(bytes memory body, uint8[] memory idx, uint8[] memory v, bytes32[] memory r, bytes32[] memory s)
        public view returns (VM memory vm)
    {
        require(idx.length >= quorum(), "no quorum");
        bytes32 h = keccak256(abi.encodePacked(keccak256(body)));
        for (uint i; i < idx.length; i++) {
            require(i == 0 || idx[i] > idx[i - 1], "indices must increase");
            require(ecrecover(h, v[i], r[i], s[i]) == guardians[idx[i]], "bad signature");
        }
        (uint16 c, bytes32 e, uint64 seq, bytes memory p) = abi.decode(body, (uint16, bytes32, uint64, bytes));
        vm = VM(c, e, seq, p, h);
    }
}
contract ToyBridge {
    ToyCore core; mapping(address => uint) public balance;
    mapping(bytes32 => bool) public used; mapping(uint16 => bytes32) public trusted;     // set by governance
    constructor(ToyCore c) { core = c; }
    function complete(bytes memory body, uint8[] memory idx, uint8[] memory v, bytes32[] memory r, bytes32[] memory s) external {
        ToyCore.VM memory vm = core.parseAndVerify(body, idx, v, r, s);
        // BUG 1: vm.emitterAddress is never compared with trusted[vm.emitterChain]
        // BUG 2: vm.hash is never recorded in used[]
        (address to, uint amt) = abi.decode(vm.payload, (address, uint));
        balance[to] += amt;
    }
}
contract BridgeTest is Test {
    function test_forgedEmitterAndReplay() public {
        uint[4] memory pk = [uint(1), 2, 3, 4]; address[] memory g = new address[](4);
        for (uint i; i < 4; i++) g[i] = vm.addr(pk[i]);
        ToyCore core = new ToyCore(g); ToyBridge b = new ToyBridge(core);
        address attacker = address(0xBAD);
        // The attacker's own contract on chain 2 published "mint 1,000,000 to attacker"; honest guardians signed it.
        bytes memory body = abi.encode(uint16(2), bytes32(uint(uint160(address(0xA77ACC)))), uint64(1), abi.encode(attacker, 1_000_000e18));
        bytes32 h = keccak256(abi.encodePacked(keccak256(body)));
        uint8[] memory idx = new uint8[](3); uint8[] memory v = new uint8[](3); bytes32[] memory r = new bytes32[](3); bytes32[] memory s = new bytes32[](3);
        for (uint i; i < 3; i++) { idx[i] = uint8(i); (v[i], r[i], s[i]) = vm.sign(pk[i], h); }
        b.complete(body, idx, v, r, s);                  // accepted: valid signatures, untrusted emitter
        b.complete(body, idx, v, r, s);                  // accepted again: replay
        emit log_named_uint("attacker balance", b.balance(attacker));
        assertEq(b.balance(attacker), 2_000_000e18);
    }
}

Everything cryptographic here is correct, and the bridge still mints two million tokens. The signatures prove only "guardians saw some contract publish this". Whether that contract is yours is the application's job. Fix both:

require(vm.emitterAddress == trusted[vm.emitterChain], "untrusted emitter");
require(!used[vm.hash], "already used");
used[vm.hash] = true;                                // before any external call or transfer

Set trusted[2] to the legitimate emitter in the test and re-run: the first call now reverts. Then ask the follow-ups an auditor asks: who can change trusted? Is the destination chain and recipient part of the signed payload? What if the same payload is valid on two different destination chains?

Step 5. The Solana verification bug (February 2022)

On Solana, signature verification happens in two steps. A secp256k1 precompile instruction checks the signatures, and the bridge's verify_signatures instruction then reads that earlier instruction through the instructions sysvar account to confirm what was checked. If the program does not verify that the account passed in is the real sysvar, the attacker passes a fake account whose contents claim "13 guardian signatures verified". The program trusted a "system" account without checking its address, marked a forged signature set as valid, and a forged VAA let the attacker mint 120,000 wrapped ETH on Solana (about $320M; the funds were later replaced by the backer). The fix was to use the checked loader that validates the sysvar address. This is Lab 6, bug 7, at the highest stakes: every account must be validated, including the ones that look like part of the runtime.

Step 6. Find the bug

// A. Solana verify_signatures
let ix = load_instruction_at(index, &ctx.accounts.instruction_acc.to_account_info())?;    // sysvar address not pinned

// B. EVM receiver
(IWormhole.VM memory vm) = wormhole.parseVM(encodedVm);                                  // parse only
require(vm.emitterAddress == trusted[vm.emitterChain]);

// C. replay key
require(!consumed[vm.sequence]); consumed[vm.sequence] = true;

// D. guardian set
require(vm.guardianSetIndex <= currentIndex);                                            // any older set, forever

// E. completing a transfer
uint amount = vm.amount * 10 ** tokenDecimals;                                           // wire amount has 8 decimals
Answers
  1. A. The February 2022 exploit. Use the checked loader (load_instruction_at_checked) or compare the account key to the sysvar id before reading.
  2. B. parseVM only decodes the bytes; it does not verify signatures. The app must call parseAndVerifyVM and require valid. Anyone can craft bytes with a perfect emitter field.
  3. C. Sequence numbers are per emitter, not global. Two emitters (or chains) can share a sequence, so one message blocks another (denial of service) or the wrong one is marked used. Key by the VAA hash, or by (chain, emitter, sequence).
  4. D. A previous guardian set must be accepted only for a short window after a rotation. Without the expiry, compromised or retired keys can sign valid VAAs forever.
  5. E. The wire amount is normalised to 8 decimals, so the multiplier must be 10 ** (tokenDecimals - 8) (when decimals exceed 8). The code above mints 108 times too much on an 18-decimal token.

Step 7. Study material

  • Wormhole, February 2022 (about $320M): read the post-mortem and the fix. Rebuild the transaction: fake sysvar account, signature set, posted VAA, mint. Write down which single check would have stopped it.
  • Bridge failures to compare (amounts approximate, from memory): Poly Network, August 2021 (about $600M; a crafted message changed the keeper key); Ronin, March 2022 (about $600M; 5 of 9 validator keys compromised); Harmony Horizon, June 2022 (about $100M; 2 of 5 multisig); Nomad, August 2022 (about $190M; a root of zero was accepted as proven, so any message passed); Multichain, July 2023 (private-key custody collapse). For each, label it: verification flaw, key-management failure, or application flaw.
  • Read the guardian-set and governance documentation, then answer: what happens if a governance VAA is replayed, and who can ever change the trusted emitter for a chain?
  • Read the Wormhole audit reports and bug-bounty scope (several firms, a large bounty) and classify findings by layer.

Step 8. Cross-chain messaging checklist

  • Verification: signatures checked by the real verifier, quorum formula, strictly increasing indices, guardian set index and expiry, hash domain, upgrade path for the set.
  • Application: emitter registered per source chain, destination chain and recipient inside the signed payload, replay protection keyed by hash, consumption flag written before external calls, payload length and type validation.
  • Token handling: decimals normalisation both ways, dust, wrapped versus native branches, outstanding or locked accounting, fee-on-transfer and rebasing tokens, mint authority.
  • Relayers: not trusted for content, refund and gas logic, griefing by front-running delivery, partial execution and retry safety.
  • Finality and consistency: what the consistency level means on each chain, reorg handling, sequencing across chains.
  • Solana specifics: every account validated (sysvars, PDAs, program ids), VAA and signature-set accounts derived and owner-checked, rent and closure.
  • Governance and keys: who can register chains, upgrade contracts, change guardian sets; rate limits and emergency pauses; behaviour when the network halts.
Lab 25 deliverable(1) With the calculator, find the largest number of compromised guardians that does not allow forgery for n = 19, and the least number of offline guardians that stalls the network. (2) Run test_forgedEmitterAndReplay, apply both fixes and show the first call reverting. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet B (parseVM without verification).

Lab 26. LayerZero V2

In plain wordsLayerZero lets each application choose which verifier networks must approve its messages. The protocol can be flawless and the application still unsafe if it chooses a single verifier.

Key terms: DVN, peer, OFT. See the glossary at the end.

Wormhole (Lab 25) fixed who verifies messages: 19 guardians. LayerZero V2 makes that a choice of the application. The protocol core is a small, immutable Endpoint; the application (an OApp, or a token built on it, an OFT) picks its own security stack: which DVNs (Decentralized Verifier Networks) must attest to each message, how many block confirmations to wait, and which executor delivers it. That flexibility is the whole audit problem. On April 18, 2026 about $292M of rsETH was drained through a bridge whose configuration required exactly one verifier. This lab teaches you to read that configuration as part of the system you are auditing.

Step 1. How a message travels

  1. Send: the OApp calls _lzSend; the Endpoint creates a packet (nonce, source and destination endpoint ids, sender, receiver, guid, message) and hands it to the OApp's send library, which charges fees for the chosen DVNs and executor.
  2. Verify: each configured DVN waits for the required confirmations on the source chain, then calls verify on the destination receive library (the ReceiveUln302), storing "DVN X attests this payload hash".
  3. Commit: once all required DVNs and at least the optional threshold of optional DVNs have attested, anyone may call commitVerification, which records the packet on the Endpoint.
  4. Execute: the executor (or anyone) calls lzReceive on the Endpoint, which calls your OApp's _lzReceive. Your code must make sure the sender is your trusted peer on that source chain.
PieceRole
EndpointV2Immutable core: nonces, channel state, message library registry, delivery; no opinion about security.
Message libraries (SendUln302, ReceiveUln302)Implement the ultra light node scheme: fee quote, DVN attestation storage, the "all required plus threshold of optional" rule.
DVNsIndependent verifier services (LayerZero Labs, Google Cloud, Polyhedra, Canary and others, plus custom ones).
ExecutorDelivers committed messages with a gas budget the sender paid for. Not trusted for correctness.
OApp / OFT / OFTAdapterYour contract. OFT burns and mints; an adapter locks and unlocks an existing token.
DelegateThe address allowed to change the OApp's configuration, often the same as the owner.

Step 2. The security stack

UlnConfig { confirmations, requiredDVNCount, optionalDVNCount, optionalDVNThreshold, requiredDVNs[], optionalDVNs[] }

Message accepted when:  every required DVN attested  AND  at least optionalDVNThreshold optional DVNs attested
Minimum compromises to forge:   R + T         (R required, T = threshold)
Liveness lost when:     any required DVN is down,  OR  fewer than T optional DVNs are up
Default config          set by LayerZero Labs per pathway; applies if the OApp sets nothing, and LayerZero may change it
Pinned config           the OApp sets its own values (and can state "none" explicitly, distinct from "use default")
StackForge needsStalls when
1 required, 0 optional (1/1)1 keythat DVN is down
2 required (2/2)2 keyseither is down
3 required (3/3)3 keysany is down
2 required + 3 optional, threshold 24 keysa required is down, or 2 optionals are down
0 required + 5 optional, threshold 33 keys3 optionals are down

After the April 2026 incident, public analyses reported that of about 2,665 active OApps over a 90-day window, roughly 47% ran 1-of-1, 45% ran 2-of-2, and about 5% ran 3-of-3 or higher. LayerZero said afterwards that its own DVN would stop signing for applications configured 1-of-1 and that defaults would move to at least 3-of-3.

Confirmations. If a DVN attests after only 1 block and the source chain reorganises, the destination executes a message for a transaction that no longer exists. Pick confirmations by the chain's finality and the value at stake.

OFT decimals. OFTs use a shared decimal format (default 6) on the wire, stored as a uint64. With a local token of 18 decimals the conversion rate is 1012: sending 1.123456789012345678 moves 1.123456 and leaves 0.000000789012345678 dust behind. The amount must fit in 64 bits of shared units (about 1.8×1013 tokens).

Step 3. Play with it

Step 4. Read the code
