Your first questions on any LayerZero integration: (1) What is the actual on-chain receive config for every route (read it, do not trust docs or scripts)? (2) Who is the delegate and what controls that key? (3) What is the maximum loss if the entire verifier set is wrong, and what limits it (rate limit, cap, pause, monitors)?

Step 5. Hands-on: one key, one forged message

This toy has the receive library rule (required plus optional threshold), an adapter that releases locked tokens, and a trusted peer. The attacker controls one DVN key, not the source chain.

contract ToyUln {
    address[] public required; address[] public optional; uint public threshold;
    mapping(bytes32 => mapping(address => bool)) public attested;
    constructor(address[] memory r, address[] memory o, uint t) { required = r; optional = o; threshold = t; }
    function verify(bytes32 h) external { attested[h][msg.sender] = true; }          // only configured DVNs matter
    function canCommit(bytes32 h) public view returns (bool) {
        for (uint i; i < required.length; i++) if (!attested[h][required[i]]) return false;
        uint n; for (uint j; j < optional.length; j++) if (attested[h][optional[j]]) n++;
        return n >= threshold;
    }
}
contract ToyAdapter {                                   // locks the real token on chain A, releases on message
    ToyUln public uln; bytes32 public peer; uint public locked = 1_000_000e18; mapping(address => uint) public balance;
    constructor(ToyUln u, bytes32 p) { uln = u; peer = p; }
    function lzReceive(bytes32 sender, bytes memory m) external {
        require(sender == peer, "wrong peer");
        require(uln.canCommit(keccak256(abi.encode(sender, m))), "not verified");
        (address to, uint amt) = abi.decode(m, (address, uint));
        locked -= amt; balance[to] += amt;
    }
}
contract DvnTest is Test {
    address dvnA = address(0xD1); address dvnB = address(0xD2); address attacker = address(0xBAD);
    bytes32 peer = bytes32(uint(uint160(address(0xBEEF))));            // the legitimate OFT on the other chain
    function _fake() internal view returns (bytes memory) { return abi.encode(attacker, 500_000e18); }   // nothing was ever burned
    function test_oneOfOne() public {
        address[] memory r = new address[](1); r[0] = dvnA; address[] memory o = new address[](0);
        ToyAdapter a = new ToyAdapter(new ToyUln(r, o, 0), peer);
        bytes32 h = keccak256(abi.encode(peer, _fake()));
        ToyUln u = a.uln();                                      // read the getter first: a prank applies to the very next call
        vm.prank(dvnA); u.verify(h);                             // compromised DVN attests to a message that never happened
        a.lzReceive(peer, _fake());
        assertEq(a.balance(attacker), 500_000e18);                      // half the locked tokens gone
    }
    function test_twoOfTwo() public {
        address[] memory r = new address[](2); r[0] = dvnA; r[1] = dvnB; address[] memory o = new address[](0);
        ToyAdapter a = new ToyAdapter(new ToyUln(r, o, 0), peer);
        bytes32 h = keccak256(abi.encode(peer, _fake()));
        ToyUln u = a.uln();                                      // read the getter first: a prank applies to the very next call
        vm.prank(dvnA); u.verify(h);                             // only one DVN is compromised
        vm.expectRevert("not verified");
        a.lzReceive(peer, _fake());
    }
}

Nothing is wrong with the endpoint, the adapter or the peer check. A single verifier lied and the system had no second opinion. With 2/2 the same attacker needs two independent compromises. Two more tests to write:

  1. Empty config: deploy ToyUln with no required and no optional DVNs and threshold 0. canCommit returns true for every hash, so anyone can drain the adapter. A correct library must reject a config that requires nothing.
  2. Limits: add a per-day cap to lzReceive (for example 5% of locked) and show that even test_oneOfOne now loses at most the cap. This does not replace multiple DVNs; it bounds the blast radius while you detect the incident.

Step 6. Find the bug

// A. custom receiver
function lzReceive(Origin calldata o, bytes32 guid, bytes calldata m, address ex, bytes calldata extra) public payable override {
    _lzReceive(o, guid, m, ex, extra);                           // the endpoint-only check was dropped when overriding
}

// B. peer check
require(address(uint160(uint(origin.sender))) == trustedPeer[origin.srcEid]);   // trustedPeer is an address

// C. OFT amounts
function _toSD(uint amountLD) internal view returns (uint64) { return uint64(amountLD / decimalConversionRate); }

// D. receive hook
function _lzReceive(Origin calldata o, bytes32, bytes calldata m, address, bytes calldata) internal override {
    (address to, uint amt) = abi.decode(m, (address, uint));
    require(!blacklist[to], "blocked");                          // reverts on an app-level condition
    _credit(to, amt);
}

// E. sending
function bridge(uint amt) external payable {
    _lzSend(dstEid, abi.encode(msg.sender, amt), "", MessagingFee(msg.value, 0), payable(msg.sender));   // options left empty, none enforced
}
Answers
  1. A. Without msg.sender == endpoint anyone can call lzReceive with an invented origin and message. The base OAppReceiver includes the check; overriding the function and forgetting it is a classic mistake. Verify both that the caller is the endpoint and that the origin sender is the registered peer.
  2. B. Peers are 32-byte values because some chains use 32-byte addresses. Truncating to 20 bytes accepts any 32-byte sender whose low 160 bits match the trusted EVM address (for example a Solana address crafted to collide in its low bytes). Compare full bytes32 values.
  3. C. Casting a large amount to uint64 truncates silently: a user could pay a lot locally but be credited a tiny amount (or the reverse in other directions). The real code reverts when the shared-decimals amount does not fit in 64 bits. Check the bound before casting.
  4. D. In ordered delivery a revert in _lzReceive blocks the channel: later messages from that source cannot execute until the nonce is cleared, skipped or the message succeeds. An attacker who can make a victim message revert (a blacklisted recipient, a cap, a paused token) can freeze the route. Handle expected failures by returning funds or storing for retry, and know who may call skip and clear.
  5. E. With no enforced options the executor may run lzReceive with too little gas. The message is verified but execution runs out of gas; funds sit in a failed state until someone retries with more gas. Set enforced options for each route, sized from tests of the worst-case receive path.

Step 7. Study material

  • KelpDAO rsETH, April 18, 2026 (about $292M, 116,500 rsETH): an attacker forged a message that appeared to come from the token's Unichain deployment and passed it through the single DVN the Ethereum-side adapter relied on. Per LayerZero's statement, its own DVN's infrastructure was attacked and the activity was attributed to a state-linked group; the protocol contracts, endpoint and the DVN's keys were not exploited directly. Kelp and LayerZero publicly disagreed on responsibility: LayerZero said the application chose a 1-of-1 setup against its recommendations; Kelp said LayerZero staff reviewed the configuration and that LayerZero's quickstart showed single-DVN setups. LayerZero later acknowledged it had been a mistake to let its DVN operate 1-of-1 for high-value flows. Read both statements and the on-chain config yourself, and write down what you would have asked in an audit.
  • Read the Dune analysis of DVN configurations across OApps and the post-incident LayerZero changes (no signing for 1/1 apps, stronger default stacks). Note that "defaults" are a trust relationship with whoever sets them.
  • Compare with the earlier bridges in Lab 25 (Ronin: 5 of 9 keys; Harmony: 2 of 5; Poly: a forged message to a privileged function; Nomad: a verification rule that accepted anything). The Kelp case is the same pattern: the verification set was smaller than the value it protected.
  • Read the LayerZero V2 audit reports (several firms) and note what they exclude: application-level verifier choices are outside the audited protocol code. That is exactly where your work begins.

Step 8. LayerZero checklist

  • Security stack (read on-chain, per route and per direction): required and optional DVNs, threshold, confirmations, library address; compare send and receive sides; no 1-of-1 for material value; DVNs run by independent operators on independent infrastructure; DVN contracts' own multisig thresholds.
  • Defaults: does any route depend on a default that LayerZero can change? Pin the values you rely on.
  • Delegate and owner: who can change config, peers, enforced options and libraries; multisig and timelock; delay on library or config changes.
  • Receive path: endpoint-only caller, full 32-byte peer comparison, replay and ordering behaviour, no revert on predictable conditions, safe handling of compose messages.
  • OFT maths: shared decimals, dust, uint64 bounds, slippage, fee-on-transfer or rebasing underlying in adapters, supply conservation (locked equals minted elsewhere).
  • Gas and options: enforced options for every route, refund address handling, fee quoting, executor failure and retry.
  • Blast radius: rate limits, caps per window, pause switch, monitoring and incident playbook, behaviour of dependent protocols (lending markets listing the bridged token).
  • Stuck messages: who can call skip, clear, nilify, burn; whether a malicious payload can be skipped safely.
Lab 26 deliverable(1) With the calculator find a stack of at most 5 DVNs that needs 4 keys to forge and tolerates one optional DVN being offline. (2) Run both DVN tests, then write the empty-config and cap tests from Step 5. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for a protocol whose receive config is one required DVN and no optional ones: severity, impact in dollars, likelihood, proof, fix.

Lab 27. Chainlink CCIP

In plain wordsChainlink CCIP needs two independent networks to approve a message and limits how much value can pass in a time window. It shows a layered design: verification plus a cap on damage.

Key terms: DON, RMN, rate limit. See the glossary at the end.

You have now seen three answers to "why should chain B believe chain A": 19 guardians (Wormhole), whatever verifiers the app picks (LayerZero), and CCIP's answer: two separate systems must both approve each message, and limits cap what any approved message can do. A Chainlink oracle network (the Committing DON) writes a Merkle root of source-chain messages to the destination chain; an independent Risk Management Network (RMN), run by different operators and written in a different language, must also bless that root; only then can the Executing DON deliver individual messages with Merkle proofs. If the RMN sees something wrong it can curse a lane and halt it. Token pools add rate limits per token and lane. This lab follows that chain and gives you a final Merkle-proof exploit to run.

Step 1. How a message travels

  1. Send: an app calls ccipSend on the source Router. The OnRamp validates the destination, takes the fee, and, for tokens, tells the token pool to lock or burn them (checking the pool's outbound rate limit). It emits a message event with a unique id and sequence number.
  2. Commit: after source-chain finality the Committing DON batches messages into a Merkle root and writes it to the destination chain (the CommitStore in older versions, the OffRamp in v1.6).
  3. Bless: RMN nodes independently observe the source chain, build their own commitment, and, where blessing is enabled for that lane, attest to the root on the destination's RMN contract. Quorum of blessings makes the root executable.
  4. Execute: the Executing DON submits a message plus its Merkle proof. The OffRamp checks the proof against a committed (and, if required, blessed) root, checks the message was not executed before, has the token pool release or mint (inbound rate limit applies), and calls the Router, which calls the receiver's ccipReceive with the gas limit set at send time.
  5. Failure and retry: if the receiver reverts or runs out of gas the message is marked failed and can be manually executed; after a configured delay anyone may do so.
PieceRole
RouterThe single address apps call; forwards to the right ramp and calls receivers on the destination.
OnRamp / OffRampPer-lane source and destination logic: fees, sequence numbers, Merkle proof verification, execution state, aggregate rate limit.
Committing and Executing DONIn v1.6 one "Role DON" runs two off-chain plugins (commit and execute) over OCR. Same operators, different roles.
Risk Management Network and contractIndependent nodes and an on-chain contract holding the bless and curse quorum logic. Docs note that RMN usage is configurable per chain and that its role is evolving; check each lane you audit.
Token poolsPer token: lock-and-release, burn-and-mint, and variants. Hold per-lane, per-direction rate limits. Registered through a token admin registry that token owners control.
Fee quoterPrices gas and data on the destination, in LINK or the native token.
Solana (SVM) versionRouter, OffRamp, fee quoter and token pool programs plus an RMN program holding "curses"; all of them check the curse list before processing.

Step 2. The maths and the limits

Merkle root       leaves = hash(0x00 ‖ message);   inner = hash(0x01 ‖ left ‖ right)       (domain separation)
Proof             sibling hashes from leaf to root; verify by recomputing the root
Execution state   per message id: untouched | in progress | success | failure
Rate limiter      tokens = min(capacity, tokens + elapsed · rate);   consume amount or revert
Two levels        aggregate limit on the lane (value in USD)  +  limit per token pool and lane (token units)
Blast radius      max loss before a halt ≈ capacity + rate · detection time

Worked Merkle proof. Four messages with leaf hashes h0 h1 h2 h3. h01 = H(h0, h1), h23 = H(h2, h3), root = H(h01, h23). To prove message 2 you send [h3, h01]: the verifier computes H(h2,h3), then H(h01, that) and compares with the root. Proof length grows with log2 of the batch size: 4 messages need 2 hashes, 1,024 need 10.

Worked blast radius. A pool allows a capacity of 100,000 tokens, refilling 1,000 per minute. A forged message (the Kelp scenario from Lab 26) can release at most 100,000 immediately and 1,000 per minute after that. If monitoring catches it in 30 minutes: 100,000 + 30,000 = 130,000. Without a limit the loss is the entire pool. With RMN blessing the forgery would also need to fool the second network; with a curse the lane stops.

Step 3. Read the code

Do this 
git clone https://github.com/smartcontractkit/chainlink-ccip      # off-chain plugins
git clone https://github.com/smartcontractkit/ccip                # on-chain contracts (also in the main chainlink repo under contracts/src/v0.8/ccip)
git clone https://github.com/smartcontractkit/chainlink-ccip-svm  # Solana programs, if present
Paths and repo names from memory; confirm in Chainlink's docs.
ReadLook for
Router.solccipSend: fee payment and refund, token transfer handling, routeMessage from OffRamps only, receiver call with gas limit and failure handling.
OnRamp.sol, OffRamp.solMessage validation, sequence and nonce rules, execute and manuallyExecute, execution state transitions, the Merkle proof check and its leaf and node hashing, aggregate rate limit, RMN curse checks.
libraries/MerkleMultiProof.solDomain separation of leaves versus inner nodes, proof flags, proof length versus leaf count.
pools/TokenPool.sol and variantslockOrBurn, releaseOrMint, remote pool and token allowlists per chain, rate limiter use, decimal handling, who may change config.
RateLimiter.solToken bucket arithmetic: refill, caps, overflow, behaviour when disabled or reconfigured.
RMN / RMNRemote, RMNHomeCurse and uncurse authority, bless quorum, how keys and thresholds change.
TokenAdminRegistry.sol, RegistryModuleOwnerCustom.solHow a token administrator is proposed, accepted and changed; who can set the pool for a token.
CCIPReceiver.sol (receiver base)onlyRouter, interface support, what a safe _ccipReceive looks like.

Step 4. Hands-on: an inner node accepted as a message

Merkle proofs are the heart of the destination-side check, and the classic flaw is hashing leaves and inner nodes the same way. Then an inner node (the concatenation of two child hashes) can be presented as if it were a message.

library ToyMerkle {
    function hashLeaf(bytes memory m, bool dom) internal pure returns (bytes32) {
        return dom ? keccak256(abi.encodePacked(bytes1(0x00), m)) : keccak256(m);
    }
    function hashPair(bytes32 a, bytes32 b, bool dom) internal pure returns (bytes32) {
        (a, b) = a < b ? (a, b) : (b, a);
        return dom ? keccak256(abi.encodePacked(bytes1(0x01), a, b)) : keccak256(abi.encodePacked(a, b));
    }
    function verify(bytes memory m, bytes32[] memory proof, bytes32 root, bool dom) internal pure returns (bool) {
        bytes32 h = hashLeaf(m, dom);
        for (uint i; i < proof.length; i++) h = hashPair(h, proof[i], dom);
        return h == root;
    }
}
contract MerkleTest is Test {
    function _tree(bool dom) internal pure returns (bytes32 root, bytes32 h01, bytes32 h23) {
        bytes32 a = ToyMerkle.hashLeaf("m0", dom); bytes32 b = ToyMerkle.hashLeaf("m1", dom);
        bytes32 c = ToyMerkle.hashLeaf("m2", dom); bytes32 d = ToyMerkle.hashLeaf("m3", dom);
        h01 = ToyMerkle.hashPair(a, b, dom); h23 = ToyMerkle.hashPair(c, d, dom);
        root = ToyMerkle.hashPair(h01, h23, dom);
    }
    function test_innerNodeAsMessage() public {
        bytes32[] memory none = new bytes32[](0);
        (bytes32 root, bytes32 x, bytes32 y) = _tree(false);
        bytes memory fake = x < y ? abi.encodePacked(x, y) : abi.encodePacked(y, x);
        assertTrue(ToyMerkle.verify(fake, none, root, false));         // a 64-byte "message" verifies with an EMPTY proof
        (bytes32 root2, bytes32 x2, bytes32 y2) = _tree(true);
        bytes memory fake2 = x2 < y2 ? abi.encodePacked(x2, y2) : abi.encodePacked(y2, x2);
        assertFalse(ToyMerkle.verify(fake2, none, root2, true));       // with domain separation the same trick fails
    }
}

Without domain separation the verifier cannot tell "a leaf" from "an inner node": keccak256(left‖right) is both. An attacker who can influence message contents (even indirectly, by choosing which messages get batched) can try to make an inner node parse as a valid message. The standard fix, which CCIP's multi-proof library applies, is a different prefix for leaves and nodes. Check this for every Merkle verifier you audit, including airdrop distributors and bridges. Nomad's failure (Lab 25) was in the same family: a verification rule that accepted something it should not.

Step 5. Find the bug

// A. receiver
function ccipReceive(Client.Any2EVMMessage calldata m) external override { _handle(m); }

// B. receiver logic
function _handle(Client.Any2EVMMessage calldata m) internal {
    (address to, uint amt) = abi.decode(m.data, (address, uint));
    token.transfer(to, amt);                                       // sender and source chain never checked
}

// C. receiver failure behaviour
function _handle(Client.Any2EVMMessage calldata m) internal {
    require(!blacklisted[decodeTo(m.data)], "blocked");
    ...
}

// D. pool config
RateLimiter.Config memory off = RateLimiter.Config({isEnabled: false, capacity: 0, rate: 0});
pool.setChainRateLimiterConfig(remoteChain, off, off);

// E. a custom Merkle check
bytes32 leaf = keccak256(message);                                 // same hash for leaves and inner nodes
Answers
  1. A. Without onlyRouter anyone can call ccipReceive with an invented message. Use the base CCIPReceiver or restrict msg.sender to the Router.
  2. B. CCIP is permissionless to send: any contract on any supported chain can send a message to your receiver. The receiver must check m.sourceChainSelector and decode m.sender against an allowlist of expected senders (the same lesson as the Wormhole emitter check). The message being genuine only proves that someone sent it.
  3. C. A revert marks the message failed and it waits for manual execution; with ordered delivery it can also block later messages from that sender. Prefer to accept and park a bad message (store it, refund, or emit an event) and keep gas limits consistent with the worst-case path.
  4. D. Disabling the limiter removes the bound on loss from a forged or buggy message. Pools should keep limits enabled on both directions with values chosen from the token's normal flow, and treat who may change them as a critical permission.
  5. E. The second-preimage flaw from Step 4: add a leaf prefix and a different prefix for inner nodes, and bind the proof length to the batch size where possible.

Step 6. Compare the three trust models

Wormhole (Lab 25)LayerZero V2 (Lab 26)Chainlink CCIP
Who verifies19 guardians, 13 must signDVNs the app selects (required plus optional threshold)Chainlink DON commits; independent RMN blesses; both needed where enabled
Who chooses the trust setThe network (governance)The application, or a default someone else setsThe protocol, with per-lane configuration and token-owner control of pools
Containment after a forged messageGovernor limits and global accountantOnly what the app builds (caps, pause)Rate limits per lane and per pool, plus curse to halt
Main application bugMissing emitter or replay checkWeak DVN config, peer checksMissing source-chain and sender allowlist
Main auditor questionIs the verification code and every account correct?What does the real on-chain config trust?Which lanes have RMN blessing, what are the limits, who controls the token pool?

Step 7. Study material

  • KelpDAO after the April 2026 exploit: reports said it moved its rsETH bridging to Chainlink's stack. Read what changed in the security model, then identify what a CCIP deployment still asks the application to get right (allowlisted senders, token pool permissions, limit values).
  • Read Chainlink's description of the Risk Management Network and the later note that its role is evolving (blessing configurable per chain). Write down the exact conditions under which a message could be executed without an RMN blessing on a lane you are auditing.
  • Read the public CCIP audit reports (several firms are listed in Chainlink's docs) and sort findings by: Merkle verification, execution state, rate limiter, token pool, fee handling, admin.
  • Re-read Nomad's incident (Lab 25 study list) with this lab's Merkle section open: which exact check was missing?

Step 8. CCIP checklist

  • Receiver: onlyRouter, allowlist of (sourceChainSelector, sender), decoding with length checks, extraArgs gas limit sized for the worst path, safe handling of failure, ordering semantics for the version in use.
  • Tokens: pool type fits the token (lock and release versus burn and mint), pool has only the permissions it needs, remote pool and token mappings correct, decimals, fee-on-transfer and rebasing tokens excluded, admin registry roles held by a multisig.
  • Limits: both directions enabled for every lane, capacity and refill from real flow data, who may change them, aggregate lane limit, behaviour when reconfigured mid-window.
  • Verification: Merkle domain separation, proof flags, double-execution protection, behaviour of manual execution (who, after how long, with what gas).
  • RMN: blessing enabled per lane, quorum and key rotation, who can curse and uncurse, behaviour of in-flight messages when a curse is applied.
  • Fees: quote then pay pattern, refund of excess, fee token approvals scoped, behaviour when gas prices move between quote and send.
  • Solana receivers: every account in the receive context validated (Lab 6), the router program as the only caller, PDA derivation for message state.
  • Operations: monitoring of lane volume against limits, incident procedure with contacts for pausing, upgrade and ownership transfer procedures.
Lab 27 deliverable(1) With the calculator find the capacity at which a $292M pool loses at most $12M in a 20-minute detection window with $1M/hour refill. (2) Run test_innerNodeAsMessage and write the two-line explanation of why domain separation fixes it. (3) Write a finding in the Lab 1 format for snippet B (receiver with no source and sender allowlist).

Glossary: plain-word definitions

Terms are in alphabetical order. Each lab also lists its key terms at the top.

Access control
Rules about who may call a function. A missing check lets anyone do an admin action.
Account (Solana)
A piece of stored data with an owner program, a balance and bytes. Programs receive accounts from the caller and must validate them.
AMM
Automated market maker. A pool of tokens that sets prices by a formula on its reserves instead of an order book.
Arbitrage
Trading between two venues to profit from a price gap. It pulls pool prices back toward the wider market.
AVS
Actively validated service. An external service (oracle, bridge, data layer) secured by restaked stake that can be slashed.
Bad debt
Debt that is larger than the collateral backing it. Depositors or the protocol must absorb the loss.
Basis point (bp)
One hundredth of a percent. 100 bp is 1%.
Blast radius
How much can be lost if one assumption or component fails.
Callback
A function a contract calls on another contract to hand control back during an operation. A source of reentrancy.
Collateral
Assets posted to back a loan. They are seized if the borrower becomes unsafe.
Concentrated liquidity
Liquidity placed only in a chosen price range, so the same money gives deeper trading near the current price.
Constant product
The rule x times y equals a constant. Prices change as reserves change.
CPI
Cross-program invocation. One Solana program calling another.
Delegatecall
A call that runs another contract's code using the caller's storage. Powerful and dangerous.
Depeg
A token trading below (or above) the value it is meant to track or redeem for.
Donation attack
Sending tokens directly to a pool to distort its share price, so later depositors receive too few shares.
DVN
Decentralized verifier network. A group that attests a cross-chain message is real (LayerZero).
Epoch
A fixed period on the chain (about two days on Solana) after which rewards are paid and stake activates.
Exchange rate
How much of the underlying asset one share or wrapped token is worth now.
Flash loan
A loan borrowed and repaid inside one transaction. It gives temporary huge capital for price manipulation.
Funding rate
A payment between long and short traders that keeps a perpetual's price near the real price.
Guardian
One of the parties that watch a chain and sign cross-chain messages (Wormhole).
Health factor
Collateral value (weighted) divided by debt. Below 1 means the account can be liquidated.
Impermanent loss
The shortfall of providing liquidity compared with just holding the tokens, caused by price changes.
Implied APY
The yearly interest rate the market price of a principal token implies.
Index
A growing number used to track interest or yield for everyone at once. Balances are stored as amounts divided by the index.
Invariant
A statement that must always be true, used as a test of correctness.
Keeper
An off-chain bot that calls a function at the right time, such as executing orders or reporting.
Liquid staking token
A token representing staked assets that stays tradable while the stake earns rewards.
Liquidation
Forced repayment of a risky loan by a third party who takes collateral at a discount.
LTV
Loan to value. The maximum borrowing as a fraction of collateral value. The liquidation threshold is a higher limit.
Maturity
The date a principal token becomes redeemable for the underlying.
Merkle proof
A short list of hashes proving an item is part of a larger committed set.
MEV
Value extracted by choosing the order of transactions, such as sandwiching a trade.
Multisig
A wallet needing several approvals to act. Its safety is the number of independent keys needed.
Open interest
Total size of open positions in a perpetual market.
Operator
A node runner who accepts delegated stake and runs service software (restaking).
Oracle
A source that brings an outside value, usually a price, onto the chain.
PDA
Program derived address. A Solana address computed from seeds and a program id, controlled only by that program.
Perpetual (perp)
A leveraged contract on a price with no expiry.
Price impact
How much your own trade moves the price against you.
Proxy
A contract that forwards calls to another contract holding the logic so that logic can be upgraded.
PT and YT
Principal token and yield token. The two pieces made by splitting a yield-bearing asset.
Rate limiter
A cap on how much can pass in a time window, often a token bucket that refills at a steady rate.
Read-only reentrancy
Reading a view function while a contract is in the middle of an update, when its numbers are temporarily wrong.
Rebase
A token whose balances change automatically as value accrues, without transfers.
Reentrancy
Being called again, through an external call, before the first call has finished updating state.
Restaking
Reusing already staked assets to secure additional services, with extra slashing risk.
Rounding direction
Which party gains the leftover when a division is not exact. It should favour the pool.
Sandwich attack
Trading before and after a victim's trade so their price worsens and you keep the difference.
Shares
Units that represent a fraction of a pool. Their value rises as the pool grows.
Signer
An account whose key approved the transaction. It is the Solana replacement for the caller address.
Skew
The difference between long and short open interest.
Slashing
A penalty that destroys part of a validator's or operator's stake for misbehaviour.
Slippage
The difference between the expected price and the executed price. Users set limits to bound it.
Spot price vs TWAP
Spot is the price right now. TWAP is the average over a time window and is harder to manipulate.
Stale state
Stored numbers (prices, indexes) that were not updated before use.
Sysvar
A special Solana account supplied by the runtime, such as the clock or the instructions list.
Tick
A discrete price step in concentrated-liquidity pools. One tick is 0.01%.
TVL
Total value locked. The total assets deposited in a protocol.
Utilization
The share of deposited funds that is currently borrowed.
VAA
Verifiable action approval. A message signed by Wormhole guardians.
Validator
A node that takes part in consensus and earns rewards for staked tokens.
Vault
A contract that holds deposits and issues shares against them, usually following the ERC-4626 standard.
Withdrawal queue
A line for exiting staked assets, because the underlying stake needs time to be released.

Capstone: bug-class index and what to do next

All 26 protocols are now covered. The point of 27 labs was never to memorise 26 codebases; it was to build a small set of reusable questions. This table maps each bug class to where you practised it and the one-line check to apply to any new protocol.

Bug classPractised inThe check
Missing access control or signerM1, 6, 11, 14, 21For every state-changing function: who is allowed, and where is that enforced? On Solana: is the authority a Signer?
Reentrancy, including read-onlyM1, 12, 13State updated before external calls? Can a view be read mid-call by another protocol?
Share inflation, donation, rounding directionM1, 4, 5, 9, 13, 22, 23Does accounting use tracked state or balanceOf? Does every conversion round against the user?
Oracle: spot price, staleness, decimals, rate manipulationM1, 2, 3, 7, 8, 10, 15, 16, 17, 20, 22Can this value move within one transaction? Is it bounded, fresh and in the right decimals? What happens if it spikes and returns?
Stale state or missing refresh7, 16, 17, 21Does every action first bring the global state up to date for this block, slot or epoch?
Account validation (Solana)6, 7, 8, 14, 21, 24, 25For every account: owner, signer, type, PDA seeds, stored-key equality, program ids. Including the "system" ones.
Callbacks and hooks as untrusted code10, 11, 22 (Penpie), 26Is the caller of this callback verified? Is state final before it runs? Can a revert block everyone?
Rounding and fixed-point scales2, 9, 10, 13, 25, 26Which scale is each variable in? Overflow in intermediate products? Which side does the rounding favour?
Time and ordering (epochs, maturity, windows)17, 18, 19, 21, 22, 24Boundary comparisons correct? Can a user act just before or after an update to capture value?
Cross-chain message authenticity25, 26, 27Who verifies, how many keys forge a message, is the sender chain and address checked, is replay blocked, what limits the loss?
Solvency controls and blast radius15, 20, 26, 27Reserve factors, caps, rate limits, pauses: what is the worst case if every assumption fails?
Privileged keys and upgradesAllWho can change parameters or code, with what delay, behind what multisig?

A workflow you can reuse on any protocol

  1. Orient: read the docs and previous audit reports; list the contracts, the trust assumptions and the money flows (M0, four-pass method).
  2. Model the maths: reproduce the core formulas in a calculator or test with real numbers. If you cannot predict an output, you do not yet understand the code.
  3. List the entry points: every external function or instruction and every account or callback an attacker controls.
  4. Hunt by class: go down the table above and ask each check at each entry point.
  5. Prove it: write a Foundry or Anchor test that exploits the finding; then write the fix and show the test failing.
  6. Write the report: title, impact, likelihood, proof, fix (Lab 1 format), with severity and a clear one-paragraph summary.

Keep practising

  • Challenge sets: Ethernaut, Damn Vulnerable DeFi, Paradigm CTF archives, Secureum RACE. For Solana, the Sealevel attacks examples and the Neodyme workshop material.
  • Contests and bounties: Code4rena, Sherlock, Cantina and Immunefi. Read the judged reports of past contests for the protocols in this course; compare what the winners found with what you found.
  • Repeat the labs without notes: choose a protocol, close the course, and reproduce its worked example, its toy exploit and its checklist from memory. Then audit a fork or a small protocol you have never seen.
  • Verify everything here. I wrote these labs from my knowledge and some searches. Contract names, addresses, parameters and incident figures can be wrong or out of date. Treating each claim as a hypothesis to check against the source is itself the main audit skill.
